Pāṭihāriyāni kevaṭṭasutte vitthāritāneva. ‘‘Ime kho, āvuso’’tiādīsu vuttanayeneva yojetabbaṃ. Iti samasaṭṭhiyā tikānaṃ vasena asītisatapañhe kathento thero sāmaggirasaṃ dassesīti. Tikavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Catukkavaṇṇanā
305. Having shown the completeness in terms of dyads in this way, now, in order to show it in terms of triads, he begins again.

There, lubbhati means lobho (greed). That which is unwholesome and a root, or a root of unwholesome things, is akusalamūlaṃ (unwholesome root). dussati means doso (hatred). muyhati means moho (delusion). Alobha (non-greed) and so on should be understood as the opposites of these.

duṭṭhu caritāni, or virūpāni vā caritāni means duccaritāni (misconduct). kāyena duccaritaṃ, or kāyato vā pavattaṃ duccaritanti means kāyaduccaritaṃ (bodily misconduct). The same method applies to the rest.

suṭṭhu caritāni, or sundarāni vā caritānīti means sucaritāni (good conduct). These two triads should be spoken of in terms of designations (paññatti) or in terms of the paths of action (kammapatha).

Firstly, in terms of designation, the transgression of a training rule (sikkhāpada) designated at the body door is kāyaduccaritaṃ (bodily misconduct). Non-transgression is kāyasucaritaṃ (bodily good conduct). The transgression of a training rule designated at the speech door is vacīduccaritaṃ (verbal misconduct), non-transgression is vacīsucaritaṃ (verbal good conduct). Transgression of a training rule designated in both cases is manoduccaritaṃ (mental misconduct), non-transgression is manosucaritaṃ (mental good conduct). This is the talk of designation.

However, the three volitions (cetanā) such as killing, arising at the body door and at the speech door, is kāyaduccaritaṃ (bodily misconduct). The four volitions beginning with lying is vacīduccaritaṃ (verbal misconduct). The three mental factors (cetanasampayuttadhammā): covetousness (abhijjhā), ill-will (byāpādo), and wrong view (micchādiṭṭhī) is manoduccaritaṃ (mental misconduct). The three volitions that arise in one who abstains from killing and so on, both volition and abstinence, is kāyasucaritaṃ (bodily good conduct). The four volitions that arise in one who abstains from lying and so on, both volition and abstinence, is vacīsucaritaṃ (verbal good conduct). The three mental factors: non-covetousness (anabhijjhā), non-ill-will (abyāpādo), and right view (sammādiṭṭhī) is manosucaritanti (mental good conduct). This is the talk of the path of action.

duccaritāni (misconduct). kāyaduccaritaṃ (bodily misconduct). sucaritāni (good conduct). kāyaduccaritaṃ (bodily misconduct). kāyasucaritaṃ (bodily good conduct). vacīduccaritaṃ (verbal misconduct), vacīsucaritaṃ (verbal good conduct). manoduccaritaṃ (mental misconduct), manosucaritaṃ (mental good conduct). kāyaduccaritaṃ (bodily misconduct). vacīduccaritaṃ (verbal misconduct). manoduccaritaṃ (mental misconduct).

A thought connected with sensual pleasure is kāmavitakko (sensual thought). A thought connected with ill-will is byāpādavitakko (thought of ill-will). A thought connected with harming is vihiṃsāvitakko (harmful thought).
In them, two, both beings and conditioned phenomena, arise.

Thinking imbued with sense-desire (kāmavitakka) arises in one who is thinking about beings or conditioned phenomena that are dear and agreeable.

Thinking imbued with ill-will (byāpādavitakka) arises when, having become angry towards beings or conditioned phenomena that are unpleasant and disagreeable, [one's anger] extends from the time of looking askance [at them] to the time of wishing for their destruction.

Thinking imbued with harming (vihiṃsāvitakka) does not arise in conditioned phenomena.

For a conditioned phenomenon is not something that should be caused to suffer.

These beings, may they be struck down, may they be eradicated, may they be destroyed, or may they not exist—thinking [in this way], however, arises in beings at the time of reflection.

Thinking imbued with sense-desire.

Thinking imbued with ill-will.

Thinking imbued with harming.

Thinking connected with renunciation (nekkhammapaṭisaṃyutto vitakko) is thinking of renunciation (nekkhammavitakko).

It, in the initial stage of the unattractive (asubhapubbabhāge), belongs to the desire realm (kāmāvacaro) and is.

In the unattractive absorption (asubhajjhāne), it belongs to the form realm (rūpāvacaro).

Having made that meditation the foundation, at the time of the arising of the path and its fruit (uppannamaggaphalakāle), it is supramundane (lokuttaro).

Thinking connected with non-ill-will (abyāpādapaṭisaṃyutto vitakko) is thinking of non-ill-will (abyāpādavitakko).

It, in the initial stage of loving-kindness (mettāpubbabhāge), belongs to the desire realm (kāmāvacaro) and is.

In the loving-kindness absorption (mettājhāne), it belongs to the form realm (rūpāvacaro).

Having made that meditation the foundation, at the time of the arising of the path and its fruit (uppannamaggaphalakāle), it is supramundane (lokuttaro).

Thinking connected with non-harming (avihiṃsāpaṭisaṃyutto vitakko) is thinking of non-harming (avihiṃsāvitakko).

It, in the initial stage of compassion (karuṇāpubbabhāge), belongs to the desire realm (kāmāvacaro).

In the compassion absorption (karuṇājhāne), it belongs to the form realm (rūpāvacaro).

Having made that meditation the foundation, at the time of the arising of the path and its fruit (uppannamaggaphalakāle), it is supramundane (lokuttaro).

When non-greed (alobho) is pre-eminent, then the other two are in conformity with it.

When loving-kindness (mettā) is pre-eminent, then the other two are in conformity with it.

When compassion (karuṇā) is pre-eminent, then the other two are in conformity with it.

The sense-desire conceptions, etc., should be understood in just the way that has been stated.

This is merely a matter of presentation.

But in terms of meaning, there is no distinction between the sense-desire thoughts, etc., and the sense-desire conceptions, etc.

Thinking of renunciation.

Thinking of non-ill-will.

Thinking of non-harming.

A conception connected with sense-desire (kāmapaṭisaṃyuttā saññā) is a sense-desire conception (kāmasaññā).

A conception connected with ill-will (byāpādapaṭisaṃyuttā saññā) is an ill-will conception (byāpādasaññā).

A conception connected with harming (vihiṃsāpaṭisaṃyuttā saññā) is a harming conception (vihiṃsāsaññā).

The manner of arising of these, too, like the sense-desire thoughts, etc., should be understood.

For these are indeed associated with that.

Renunciation conceptions, etc., are also associated with renunciation thoughts, etc.

Therefore, the state of belonging to the desire realm, etc., of these, too, should be understood in that same way.
Kāmasaññā (perception of sensual pleasure).
Byāpādasaññā (perception of ill-will).
Vihiṃsāsaññā (perception of violence).
Nekkhammasaññādayo (perception of renunciation)

In the kāmadhātu and so on: "Thinking and applied thought connected with sensual desire, wrong thinking, this is called the kāmadhātu (desire realm). All unwholesome dharmas are kāmadhātu." This is the kāmadhātu.

"Thinking and applied thought connected with ill-will, this is called byāpādadhātu (the element of ill-will). Among the ten grounds for resentment, agitation of the mind, resistance, and displeasure of the mind, this is called byāpādadhātu."

"Thinking and applied thought connected with violence, this is called vihiṃsādhātu (the element of violence). Here, one person injures beings with a hand, a clod, a stick, a weapon, or a rope, or in some other way. This is called vihiṃsādhātu."

There are two kinds of explanations here: the all-inclusive and the non-overlapping.

There, when the kāmadhātu is taken, the other two are also taken. But then, having removed them, [and] demonstrating, 'This is the byāpādadhātu, this is the vihiṃsādhātu,' this is called the all-inclusive explanation.

But when the Blessed One, in explaining the kāmadhātu, having placed the byāpādadhātu in the place of the byāpādadhātu, and the vihiṃsādhātu in the place of the vihiṃsādhātu, and saying that the remainder is called kāmadhātu, this is called the non-overlapping explanation.

Kāmadhātu (desire realm).
Byāpādadhātu (element of ill-will).
Vihiṃsādhātu (element of violence).

In the nekkhammadhātu and so on: "Thinking and applied thought connected with renunciation, right thinking, this is called nekkhammadhātu (the element of renunciation). All wholesome dharmas are nekkhammadhātu." This is the nekkhammadhātu.

"Thinking connected with non-ill-will... This is called abyāpādadhātu (the element of non-ill-will). Loving-kindness towards beings... liberation of mind through loving-kindness." This is the abyāpādadhātu.

"Thinking connected with non-violence... This is called avihiṃsādhātu (the element of non-violence). Compassion towards beings... liberation of mind through compassion." This is the avihiṃsādhātu.

Here also, two explanations should be understood in just the same way as stated earlier.

Nekkhammadhātu (element of renunciation).
Abyāpādadhātu (element of non-ill-will).
Avihiṃsādhātu (element of non-violence).

"There are also three other dhātus": that is, there are three other dhātus by way of emptiness.

Among these, "What is the kāmadhātu? Having made the Avīci hell the lower limit," the kāmabhava (sensual existence) thus extended is called kāmadhātu.

"Having made the Brahmā world the lower limit, and having made the gods who have reached the plane of nothingness the upper limit," the rūpārūpabhava (existence in the realms of form and formlessness) thus extended are the other two dhātus.

Here, the place of arrival of a dhātu should be distinguished by existence.

In the place of arrival of an existence, the dhātu should be distinguished.

Here, distinction by existence is spoken of.
In the realms of form (rūpadhātu) and so on, the realms of form and formlessness (rūpārūpadhātuyo) are only for existence in form and formlessness (rūpārūpabhavāyeva). Cessation as an element (nirodhadhātuyā) is said to be nirvāṇa (nibbānaṃ kathitaṃ).

Moreover, there are three elements (aparāpi tisso dhātuyo): the element of desire (kāmadhātu). The twelve unwholesome states of mind are the base elements (hīnā dīsu hīnā dhātūti dvādasa akusalacittuppādā). The remaining threefold wholesome states are the medium element (avasesā tebhūmakadhammā majjhimadhātu). The nine supramundane states are the superior element (nava lokuttaradhammā paṇītadhātu). Base (hīnā), medium (majjhimadhātu), and superior elements (paṇītadhātu).

"Desire-thirst" (kāmataṇhā) is craving associated with the five desirable sense objects (pañcakāmaguṇiko rāgo). However, craving in the realms of form and formlessness (rūpārūpabhavesu), craving associated with delight in meditation, eternalism, and views, is desire based on existence, namely, "existence-thirst" (bhavataṇhā). Craving associated with annihilationism is "non-existence-thirst" (vibhavataṇhā). Moreover, setting aside the latter two cravings, the remaining craving is called "desire-thirst" (kāmataṇhā nāma). As he said, "What is existence-thirst there? Craving associated with the view of existence, attachment, attachment of the mind, this is called existence-thirst. What is non-existence-thirst there? Craving associated with the view of annihilation, attachment, attachment of the mind, this is called non-existence-thirst. The remaining craving is desire-thirst."

Again, among desire-thirst and so on, craving associated with the five desirable sense objects is desire-thirst (kāmataṇhā). Desire and craving in the realms of form and formlessness are the other two thirsts. In the Abhidhamma, these are explained in detail as "associated with the desire realm... associated with the formless realm." What does this show by way of example? All threefold states are objects of craving by way of being desirable. Having encompassed all craving within desire-thirst, then extracting from it, it shows the other two cravings. Among form-thirst and so on, desire and craving in the realm of form is "form-thirst" (rūpataṇhā). Desire and craving in the formless realm is "formless-thirst" (arūpataṇhā). Craving associated with the view of annihilation is "cessation-thirst" (nirodhataṇhā).

Desire-thirst (kāmataṇhā), existence-thirst (bhavataṇhā), non-existence-thirst (vibhavataṇhā), form-thirst (rūpataṇhā), formless-thirst (arūpataṇhā), cessation-thirst (nirodhataṇhā).

In the round of the threefold fetters (saṃyojanattike vaṭṭasmiṃ), "fetters" (saṃyojanāni) are so called because they bind and connect. The view that there is a self in the body, where there is a distinction of form and so on, or the view that a self exists in the body, is "personality belief" (sakkāyadiṭṭhi). Because one is troubled by investigating this and is unable to come to a conclusion, it is "doubt" (vicikicchā). Because one grasps at morality and vows, it is "clinging to rules and vows" (sīlabbataparāmāso). In meaning, however, the twenty kinds of views that come in the way of the method beginning with "one perceives form as self" is called "personality belief" (sakkāyadiṭṭhi). The eight kinds of uncertainty that come in the way of the method beginning with "one doubts the teacher" is called "doubt" (vicikicchā). "Here, a certain person is purified by morality, purified by vows, purified by morality and vows; he grasps at morality, he grasps at vows, he grasps at morality and vows."
That sort of view, the distortion that comes about through views and so on, is called śīlabbataparāmāsa (clinging to mere rules and rituals).

The saṃyojanas (fetters):
Sakkāyadiṭṭhi (belief in a self),
vicikicchā (doubt),
śīlabbataparāmāsa (clinging to mere rules and rituals).
Sakkāyadiṭṭhi (belief in a self), vicikicchā (doubt), śīlabbataparāmāsa (clinging to mere rules and rituals) are three.

"Āsavas" - here, by way of long habituation or by way of outflowing, are the āsavas (cankers).
Therein, "No first point of ignorance is discerned, : 'Before this there was no ignorance, afterwards it came into being," "No first point of becoming-craving or view is discerned: 'Before this there was no becoming-craving or view, afterwards it came into being." Thus, to that extent, the āsavas are to be understood by way of long habituation.
From the eye, it flows (savati) into forms, it outflows (āsavati), it trickles (sandati), it streams (pavattati).
From the ear, into sounds.
From the nose, into smells.
From the tongue, into tastes.
From the body, into tactile objects.
From the mind, it flows (savati) into ideas, it outflows (āsavati), it trickles (sandati), it streams (pavattati). Thus, the āsavas are to be understood by way of outflowing.

The three āsavas.

In the Pāḷi, however, sometimes two āsavas are mentioned: "Those āsavas pertaining to the immediate present and those pertaining to the future," sometimes, "There are these three āsavas, monks: the kāmāsava (canker of sensual desire), the bhavāsava (canker of becoming), the avijjāsava (canker of ignorance)."
In the Abhidhamma, those three, together with the diṭṭhāsava (canker of views), are four.
In the Nibbedhika-pariyāya Sutta, "There are these āsavas, monks, that lead to hell; there are these āsavas that lead to the animal realm; there are these āsavas that lead to the realm of ghosts; there are these āsavas that lead to the human world; there are these āsavas that lead to the divine sphere" - thus, five.
In the Chakkanipāta, Āhuneyya Sutta: "There are these āsavas, monks, to be abandoned by restraint; there are these āsavas to be abandoned by using; there are these āsavas to be abandoned by avoiding; there are these āsavas to be abandoned by tolerating; there are these āsavas to be abandoned by destroying; there are these āsavas to be abandoned by developing" - thus, six.
In the Sabbāsava-pariyāya Sutta, those six, together with those to be abandoned by seeing, are seven.
In this Saṅgīti Sutta, three.

Therein, "Yo kāmesu kāmacchando (whatever desire there is for sensual pleasures)" - thus, that which is called the five strands of sense-pleasure is called kāmāsava (canker of sensual desire).
"Yo bhavesu bhavacchando (whatever desire there is for becoming)" - thus, that desire which is accompanied by the eternalist view, or the yearning based on becoming, is called bhavāsava (canker of becoming).
"Dukkhe aññāṇa (ignorance concerning suffering)" and so on - by that method, ignorance is called avijjāsava (canker of ignorance).
The kāmabhava and so on have already been described according to the kāmadhātu and so on.

Kāmāsava (canker of sensual desire), bhavāsava (canker of becoming), avijjāsava (canker of ignorance).
Concerning the searches, what is the search for sense-pleasures? Whatever desire, longing, and obsession there is for sense-pleasures, that is called the search for sense-pleasures. Thus, the craving to seek after sense-pleasures is called kāmesanā (search for sense-pleasures).
What is the search for becoming? Whatever desire, longing, and obsession there is for becoming, that is called the search for becoming. Thus, the craving to seek after becoming is called bhavesanā (search for becoming).
What is the search for the spiritual life? The view that the world is eternal, and so on... or that the tathāgata neither exists nor does not exist after death, whatever view of that sort, the distortion of views, is called the search for the spiritual life. Thus, the search-view of the spiritual life that is esteemed due to the course of views is called brahmacariyesanā (search for the spiritual life).
It is not only the craving-view of becoming, but the volitional action connected with that is also itself a search.
It has been said: "What is the search for sense-pleasures? The craving for sense-pleasures, the unwholesome volitional action of body, speech, and mind connected with that, is called the search for sense-pleasures.
What is the search for becoming? The craving for becoming, the unwholesome volitional action of body, speech, and mind connected with that, is called the search for becoming.
What is the search for the spiritual life? The view that clings to extremes, the unwholesome volitional action of body, speech, and mind connected with that, is called the search for the spiritual life."
kāmesanā, bhavesanā, brahmacariyesanā. In the section on vidhā [it says,] "What kind of person do the virtuous call moral? What kind of person do the wise call intelligent?" etc. (Saṃ.
Ni.
1.1).
95) ākārasaṇṭhānaṃ is called vidhā.
In the section on "A single kind of object of knowledge, two kinds of objects of knowledge," etc. (Vibha.
751) [it is called] koṭṭhāsa.
In the section on "I am better, thus vidhā," etc. (Vibha.
920) pride is called vidhā.
Here, it is intended.
For pride is called vidhā because of distinguishing by way of being better, and so on.
By this, "I am better," the three types of pride are stated by way of being better, similar, and inferior.
The same rule applies to those who are similar and inferior.

vidhā [and] "I am better":
Indeed, this pride is threefold in the case of what is better, threefold in the case of what is similar, threefold in the case of what is inferior; thus, it is ninefold.
There, the pride "I am better than the better" arises in kings and renunciates.

Pride [is] ninefold:
For a king generates the pride, "Who is similar to me?" by way of his kingdom or wealth and vehicles.
A renunciate also generates the pride, "Who is similar to me?" by way of morality, ascetic practices, and so on.
The pride, "I am similar to the better" also arises in these very [individuals].
For a king generates the pride, "What is the difference between me and other kings?" by way of his kingdom or wealth and vehicles.
A renunciate also generates the pride, "What is the difference between me and another bhikṣu?" by way of morality, ascetic practices, and so on.
The pride, "I am inferior to the better" also arises in these very [individuals].
For if a king's kingdom or wealth, vehicles, and so on are not very prosperous, then he generates the pride, "I am only nominally a king; what kind of king am I?"
A renunciate also, having little gain and honor, [and thinking,] "I am a dharmakathika, learned, and a senior monk," [but] only nominally, [then thinks,] "What kind of dharmakathika am I? What kind of learned person? What kind of senior monk, since I have no gain and honor?" and generates this pride.

"I am better than the similar": pride and so on arise in ministers and so on.
For a minister, considering his kingdom, his fellow countrymen, his enjoyment, vehicles, and conveyances, [thinks,] "Who is another royal official similar to me?" or "What is the difference between me and others?" or "I am only nominally a minister; I do not even have enough food and clothing; what kind of minister am I?" and generates these prides.

"I am better than the inferior": pride and so on arise in slaves and so on.
For a slave, whether by way of his mother or father, [thinks,] "Who is another slave similar to me? Others, unable to live, have become slaves for the sake of their bellies, but I am better because of ancestral lineage," or "What is the difference between me and another slave by way of ancestral lineage, being a slave pure on both sides, [and] that particular slave?" or "I have become a slave by way of my belly, but by way of my mother's and father's lineage, there is no slave status for me; what kind of slave am I?" and generates these prides.
Just as a slave [does], so do pukkusas, caṇḍālas, and so on generate these prides.

And here, "I am better than the better," "I am similar to the similar," and "I am inferior to the inferior": these three prides are called appropriate prides [and are] obstructive to the path of Arhatship.
The remaining six prides are called inappropriate prides [and are] obstructive to the first path.

"Tayo addhā" means three times.
In "atīto addhā" and so on, there are two meanings: the meaning of the sūtrānta and the meaning of the abhidhamma.
In the meaning of the sūtrānta, before rebirth is called "atīto addhā."
After cessation, it is called "the future, indeed" (anāgato addhā).

Together with cessation and rebirth, in the interval, it is called "the present, indeed" (paccuppanno addhā).

In the mode of Abhidhamma, after disintegration in the three moments, it is called "the past, indeed" (atīto addhā).

Before arising, it is called "the future, indeed" (anāgato addhā).

In the three moments, it is called "the present, indeed" (paccuppanno addhā).

The distinction of past, etc., belongs to dharmas, not to time.

However, having taken dharmas as the object, here, even though time does not exist in the ultimate sense (paramārtha), it should be understood that it is spoken of in that very convention.

These are the three ends: past, indeed; past, indeed; future, indeed; present, indeed; past, indeed; future, indeed; present, indeed.

"These three antas" means these three divisions.

In passages beginning with "The anta of the body's binding decays" (kāyabandhanassa anto jīratī)..., the anta is indeed the anta.

In passages beginning with "This is the anta of suffering" (esevanto dukkhassā)..., parabhāga is the anta.

In passages beginning with "This is the anta, monks, of livelihood" (antamidaṃ, bhikkhave, jīvikānaṃ)..., here, lāmakabhāva is the anta.

In passages beginning with "The satkāya, friend, is the first anta" (sakkāyo kho, āvuso, paṭhamo anto)..., koṭṭhāsa is the anta.

Here, koṭṭhāsa is intended.

"Satkāya (sakkāyo)" means the five aggregates of grasping (pañcupādānakkhandhā).

"Satkāyasamudaya (sakkāyasamudayo)" means the prior craving (purimataṇhā) that is the cause of their arising (tesaṃ nibbattikā).

"Satkāyanirodha (sakkāyanirodho)" means nirvāṇa, which is the non-arising (appavattibhūtaṃ) of both.

However, the path (magga) is the means of attaining cessation (nirodhādhigamassa upāyattā); therefore, it should be understood that when cessation is grasped, the path is also grasped (nirodhe gahite gahitovāti veditabbo).

These are the three ends: satkāya (sakkāyo), satkāyasamudaya (sakkāyasamudayo), satkāyanirodha (sakkāyanirodho).

"Duḥkhaduḥkhatā (dukkhadukkhatā)" means suffering that is suffering (dukkhabhūtā dukkhatā).

This is a name for painful feeling (duḥkhavedanāyetaṃ nāmaṃ).

"Saṃkhāraduḥkhatā (saṅkhāradukkhatā)" means suffering due to conditioned phenomena (saṅkhārabhāvena dukkhatā).

This is a name for neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling (adukkhamasukhāvedanāyetaṃ nāmaṃ).

Since it is conditioned (saṃkhatattā) and afflicted by origination, aging, and disintegration (uppādajarābhaṅgapīḷitā), therefore, because of the absence of another nature of suffering (aññadukkhasabhāvavirahato), it is said to be saṅkhāraduḥkhatā (saṅkhāraduḥkhatāti vuttā).

"Vipariṇāmaduḥkhatā (vipariṇāmadukkhatā)" means suffering due to change (vipariṇāme dukkhatā).

This is a name for pleasant feeling (sukhavedanāyetaṃ nāmaṃ).
Because in the change of pleasant feeling, suffering arises, therefore pleasant feeling is called suffering of change.

Moreover, setting aside painful feeling and pleasant feeling, all the three-realm phenomena are to be understood as suffering of formations, because of the saying, "all conditioned things are suffering."

Dukkha-dukkhata (suffering of suffering), saṅkhāradukkhatā (suffering of formations), vipariṇāmadukkhatā (suffering of change).

Micchattaniyato means fixed in wrongness, having become a wrong nature.

This is the name for the immediately effective karma together with fixed wrong view.

Sammattaniyato means fixed in rightness, in a state of rightness.

This is the name of the four noble paths.

Aniyato means not fixed.

This is the name of the remaining phenomena.

Micchattaniyato, sammattaniyato, aniyato.

Tayo tamā means the three darknesses, because of the saying, "darkness is obscurity, confusion, a flood of ignorance, great fear," ignorance is called darkness.

Here, uncertainty is spoken of as headed by ignorance.

Ārabbhā means having approached.

Kaṅkhatī means he produces doubt.

Vicikicchatī means while investigating, he falls into difficulty; he is not able to decide.

Nādhimucchatī means there, he is not able to be resolved.

Na sampasīdatī means having approached that, he is not able to bring about confidence.

Ārabbhā, kaṅkhatī, vicikicchatī, nādhimucchatī, na sampasīdatī.

Arakkheyyānī means they are not to be guarded.

In the three doors, there is no guarding to be done individually; he reveals that all are guarded by mindfulness itself.

Natthi tathāgatassā means there is no such thing for the Tathāgata.

"This bodily misconduct arose for me suddenly; I will guard and conceal it so that another does not know of me," thus there is no bodily misconduct of the Tathāgata to be guarded.

The same principle applies to the remainder.

Are the bodily behaviors and so forth of the remaining perfected ones impure? No, they are not impure.

However, they are not as pure as those of the Tathāgata.

Although an unlearned perfected one does not incur worldly offenses, due to his lack of skill in the precepts, he incurs offenses in the body door such as construction of monasteries, construction of huts, shared dwellings, and shared beds.

He incurs offenses in the speech door such as gossiping, speaking contrary to the Dharma, speaking more than five or six sentences, and revealing what is true.

He incurs the offense of accepting silver in the mind door through the way of being attached to what has been set aside; even for a perfected one like the general of the Dharma, misconduct of mind arises in the mind door through the force of anger.

Arakkheyyānī

Natthi tathāgatassā

Indeed, when the elder was forgiven by the Śākyas at Cātumā, who had pleased the Bhagavat for the sake of Śāriputra and Maudgalyāyana together with five hundred monks at the time of their banishment, the Bhagavat asked, "What was it to you, Śāriputra, when I banished the monastic community?" I was banished by the teacher due to my unexpressed state to the assembly.

Having generated the thought, "From here on, I will not advise anyone," he said, "Thus, O venerable one, it occurred to me: the monastic community has been banished by the Bhagavat, now the Bhagavat, being without exertion, will abide devoted to the dwelling of happiness in the visible Dharma; we too, now being without exertion, will abide devoted to the dwelling of happiness in the visible Dharma."

Then, while the teacher was raising blame in that misconduct of mind, he said, "Come here, Śāriputra, it is not proper for you, Śāriputra, to generate such a thought again."

Thus, even the mere thought, "I will not advise or instruct anyone further," is called misconduct of mind for an elder.

But for the Bhagavat, there is no such thing; and that is not surprising.

Misconduct would not occur for one who has attained omniscience.

Even for one abiding in the Bodhisattva grounds, while practicing exertion for six years, there was no such thing.
With the skin of the belly clinging to the spine, even when doubt arose in the minds of the gods, thinking, "The ascetic Gotama has died," [Māra said,] "Siddhattha, why do you exert yourself? It is possible to enjoy pleasures and do meritorious deeds." But not even the slightest thought of "I will enjoy pleasures" arose in him while the sinful Māra was speaking.

Then Māra, having followed him for six years during the time of the Bodhisattva and one year during the time of the Buddha, having found no fault, departed, saying this:

‘‘For seven years, I followed the Blessed One, step by step; I did not find an opportunity; [such is] the mindfulness of the Fully Awakened One.’’

(su. ni. 448);

Moreover, the Blessed One's lack of misconduct should be understood by way of the eighteen qualities of a Buddha.

The eighteen qualities of a Buddha are as follows: there is no bodily misconduct of the Tathāgata, there is no verbal misconduct, there is no mental misconduct, the Buddha's knowledge is unimpeded in the past, in the future, and in the present, all bodily actions of the Buddha, the Blessed One, accord with knowledge, all verbal actions, all mental actions of the Buddha, the Blessed One, accord with knowledge, there is no decline of aspiration, there is no decline of energy, there is no decline of mindfulness, there is no agitation, there is no clamor, there is no restlessness, there is no hastiness, there is no unsteadiness of mind, there is no unwholesome thought.

Kiñcana means impediments. Attachment is an impediment, for attachment, when arising, binds beings, obstructs them; therefore, it is called an impediment.

The same method applies in the other two cases.

Aggī means fire, by way of not burning up. Rāgaggī means the fire of passion: attachment, when arising, burns beings, it scorches them; therefore, it is called fire.

The same method applies in the other cases.

In that connection: A certain young nun went to the uposatha hall in the Cittalapabbata Monastery. She stood gazing at a figure of a doorkeeper. Then passion arose within her. She was consumed by that very [passion] and died.

As the nuns were going [to her], they said, "This young one is standing [here]. Call her!"

One nun went and, taking her hand, said, "Why are you standing [here]?"

As soon as she was touched, she collapsed and fell down.

This, to be sure, is an instance of attachment not burning up [immediately].

Furthermore, the gods called manopadosikā are an instance of aversion not burning up [immediately].

The gods called khiḍḍāpadosikā should be regarded as an instance of delusion not burning up [immediately].

For, through the power of delusion, they experience loss of mindfulness. Therefore, through the power of amusement, having exceeded the time for food, they die.

Aggī means fire. Rāgaggī, manopadosikā, khiḍḍāpadosikā.

In āhuneyyaggī etc., āhunaṃ means offerings, āhunaṃ arahanti means worthy of offerings. Parents, indeed, are worthy of offerings because they are of great assistance to their children. Children who act contrary to them are reborn in hells, etc. Therefore, even though parents do not burn up [immediately], they are, however, a cause for not burning up [immediately].

Thus, by way of not burning up, they are called āhuneyyaggī.

This meaning should be illustrated by the story of Mittavindaka –

Āhuneyyaggī, āhuneyyā.
Mittavindaka, his mother said, “Dear, today, having observed the uposatha, spend the entire night in the monastery listening to the Dharma."

Hearing this, and motivated by greed for wealth, he took the uposatha, went to the monastery, and thinking, "This place is safe from all fear," lay down beneath the Dharma seat, slept the entire night, and then went home.

His mother cooked rice gruel early in the morning and offered it to him.

He drank it all, taking the thousand [coins].

Then it occurred to him, "I shall amass wealth."

He wanted to travel the ocean by ship.

Then his mother stopped him, saying, "Dear, in this family there are forty koṭis of wealth; there is no need for you to go."

Ignoring her words, he went anyway.

His mother stood in front of him.

Then, becoming angry, he kicked her, saying, "Why are you standing in front of me?" and went away, creating a separation.

His mother, rising up, said, "Having committed such an act against a mother like me, may you find happiness in the place where you go!" Thus she cursed him, her son.

As he was traveling on the ship, on the seventh day, the ship stopped.

Then the people said, "Surely there is an evil person here; let us draw lots!"

The lot repeatedly fell to him three times.

They gave him a raft and threw him into the ocean.

Going to one island, enjoying prosperity with the celestial nymphs, and being told by them, "Do not go further, do not go further," seeing double and double again the prosperity, he gradually saw one with a razor-edged wheel.

That wheel appeared to him like a lotus blossom.

He said to him, "Hey, give me this lotus that you are wearing."

"This is not a lotus, sir, it is a razor-edged wheel."

He, saying, "You deceive me! Have I never seen a lotus before?" and saying, "You, having smeared yourself with red sandalwood, are unwilling to give me, a person deserving of it, the lotus flower!"

He thought, "This one also, having performed a deed similar to what I did, desires to experience its result."

Then, saying, "Come here, you!" he threw the wheel onto his head.

Therefore it was said:

"Four go to eight, and eight also to sixteen;

Sixteen also to thirty-two; insatiable, the wheel overtakes [him];

For a person overcome by desire, the wheel spins on his head."

(Jātaka, 1.1.104)

Here, gahapati means householder.

He is very helpful by providing robes, beds, ornaments, and so on to the mātugāma.

Transgressing against him, the mātugāma is reborn in hells and so forth; therefore, he is called gahapataggi because of his act of burning in accordance with the previous method.

Gahapati: There is a story about this. In the time of the Buddha Kaśyapa, the wife of a stream-enterer lay follower was unfaithful.

He saw her in the act and said, "Why are you doing this?"

She said, "If I am doing such a thing, may this dog of mine, while snatching, devour me!" Saying this, she died and, having become a goddess with clipped ears in a cremation ground, was reborn as a celestial nymph.

By day she experiences prosperity, by night suffering.
At that time, the king of Vārāṇasī, while wandering around hunting, entered a forest and gradually arrived at the Kaṇṇamuṇḍaka pond, where he experienced enjoyment with her.

She deceived him and experienced suffering at night.

He, knowing this, thought, "Where is she going?" and followed her from behind. Not far away, he saw her emerge from the Kaṇṇamuṇḍaka pond, eating a dog, making a "paṭapaṭa" sound. He cut it in two with his sword.

There were two.

When cut again, there were four.

When cut again, there were eight.

When cut again, there were sixteen.

She said, "What are you doing, my lord?"

He said, "What is this?"

She said, "Instead of doing that, you should spit out a lump of saliva on the ground and rub it with your foot."

He did so.

The dogs disappeared.

That day, her karma was exhausted.

The king, feeling remorse, began to leave.

She said, "My lord, my karma is exhausted; do not go."

The king left without being persuaded.

dakkhiṇeyyaggīti here, dakkhiṇāti has four conditions; the monastic community is worthy of offerings (dakkhiṇeyyo).

He is very helpful to householders by guiding them in the three refuges, the five precepts, the ten precepts, honoring mother and father, honoring righteous ascetics and brāhmaṇas, and other such virtuous things. Householders who wrongly treat the monastic community, scold and insult them, are reborn in hells and other such places. Therefore, that too, because of the meaning of non-burning according to the earlier way, is called worthy of offerings (dakkhiṇeyyaggī)
It is said.

To explain the meaning of this, the Revatī story in the Vimānavatthu should be expanded.

dakkhiṇeyyaggī dakkhiṇā ‘‘tividhena rūpasaṅgaho’’ti here, tividhenā means with three parts.

saṅgahoti: saṅgaho is fourfold based on birth, shared birth, action, and counting.

There, "May all the kṣatriyas come!" etc., (Ma. Ni. 1.462) is jātisaṅgaho.

"All Kosalans!" etc. is sañjātisaṅgaho.

"All elephant riders!" etc. is kiriyasaṅgaho.

What is the extent of the eye (cakkhāyatanaṃ)? Which aggregate of counting does it go to? The extent of the eye goes to the aggregate of counting of form (rūpa).

If the extent of the eye is included in the aggregate of form, this is an aggregate of counting. That is what is intended here.

Therefore, by the rūpa aggregate in three ways (tividhena rūpasaṅgahoti) it means counting rūpa with three parts.

tividhenā saṅgaho sanidassanā means with appearance (nidassanena), referring to the cakkhuviññāṇa that arises in relation to oneself among the sanidassanā and others.

Sappaṭighaṃ means with resistance (paṭighena) because of the capacity to strike the eye.

That, in meaning, is just the realm of form (rūpāyatana).

Anidassanaṃ means cakkhuviññāṇa is not its appearance (nidassana).

Due to the capacity to resist sound and other things, and with resistance (paṭighena).
Sappaṭighaṃ.
That, in essence, is the nine sense spheres beginning with the eye sense-sphere ( cakkhāyatana).
Because its manner has been stated, it is not an illustration; hence, anidassanaṃ.
Because it does not have resistance, it is appaṭighaṃ.
That, in essence, is subtle matter, leaving aside the ten sense spheres.

Sanidassanā sanidassanaṃ.
Sappaṭighaṃ.
Anidassanaṃ.

Appaṭighaṃ.
Tayo saṅkhārā: the saṅkhārā fashion together and heap up co-nascent phenomena and fruit phenomena in the next life; therefore, they are saṅkhārā.
Abhisaṅkharoti: therefore, it is abhisaṅkhāro.
Puñño abhisaṅkhāro: a meritorious fashioning; therefore, it is puññābhisaṅkhāro.

Tayo saṅkhārā puññābhisaṅkhāro.
‘‘There, what is the meritorious fashioning (puññābhisaṅkhāro)? Wholesome volitions (kusalā cetanā) pertaining to the desire realm (kāmāvacarā), pertaining to the form realm (rūpāvacarā), consisting of giving (dānamayā), consisting of ethical conduct (sīlamayā), consisting of development (bhāvanāmayā)’’: this is a designation for those eight volitions of great wholesome minds pertaining to the desire realm, and for the five wholesome volitions pertaining to the form realm.
And here, there are indeed eight volitions consisting of giving and ethical conduct.
And there are thirteen consisting of development (bhāvanāmayā).
Just as someone reciting a well-learned doctrine (dhamma), even having reached one or two connections (anusandhiṃ), does not know, [but] afterwards, adverting [to it], he knows, just so, for one doing preliminary work on a kasiṇa, experiencing well-learned absorption (jhāna), contemplating, even development (bhāvanā) disconnected from knowledge (ñāṇa) occurs.
Therefore, it was said, ‘‘There are thirteen consisting of development (bhāvanāmayā)’’.

There, among those consisting of giving etc., ‘‘The volition (cetanā), intention (sañcetanā), and volitional state (cetayitattaṃ) which arise dependent on giving (dānaṃ ārabbha), concerning giving (dānamadhikicca), this is called a meritorious fashioning consisting of giving (dānamayo puññābhisaṅkhāro).
Dependent on ethical conduct (sīlaṃ ārabbha), dependent on development (bhāvanaṃ ārabbha), concerning development (bhāvanamadhikicca), the volition (cetanā), intention (sañcetanā), and volitional state (cetayitattaṃ) which arise, this is called a meritorious fashioning consisting of development (bhāvanāmayo puññābhisaṅkhāro)’’: this is an explanation in brief.

But for one giving robes etc. among the four requisites, or among the six objects beginning with forms, or among the ten objects of giving beginning with food, at the time of producing each of those, in the prior part, at the time of bestowing, and afterwards, in the recollection with a glad mind, the volition (cetanā) occurring at these three times is called consisting of giving (dānamayā).
But for one going to a monastery thinking, ‘‘I will go forth for the sake of perfecting ethical conduct (sīla)’’, for one going forth, having brought the wish to the summit, thinking, ‘‘I am one who has gone forth, it is good, good!’’ adverting [to it], restraining the pātimokkha, contemplating the requisites beginning with robes, restraining the doors of the eye etc. among forms etc. which have come into the path, and for one purifying his livelihood, the volition (cetanā) occurring is called consisting of ethical conduct (sīlamayā).

In the Paṭisambhidā, by the path of insight (vipassanā) stated, ‘‘For one developing the eye as impermanent, as suffering, as not-self… mind…
forms.
phenomena.
eye-consciousness… mind-consciousness.
eye-contact… mind-contact.
feeling born of eye-contact… feeling born of mind-contact.
perception of forms, old age and death as impermanent, as suffering, as not-self, the volition (cetanā) occurring is called consisting of development (bhāvanāmayā)’’: this is a detailed account.

And that fashioning is unmeritorious; therefore, it is an unmeritorious fashioning (apuññābhisaṅkhāro).
This is a designation for the volitions connected with the twelve unwholesome minds.
It was also said, "What is unmeritorious activity? Unwholesome intention in the desire realm; this is called unmeritorious activity."

"Imperturbable," still, peaceful, the result of which is formless; thus he generates, it is called "imperturbable activity." This is a designation for the four wholesome consciousnesses pertaining to the formless realm. As it is said, "What is imperturbable activity? Wholesome intention in the formless realm; this is called imperturbable activity."

"Unmeritorious activity."

"Imperturbable activity."

In the Personal Dispositions, a person is one of seven kinds, he undertakes three trainings, so he is called "one in training." One whose influxes are destroyed, who has completed the training, does not train again, so he is called "one no longer in training." An ordinary person is excluded from the trainings, so he is "neither in training nor no longer in training."

Personal Dispositions

"One in training."

"One no longer in training."

"Neither in training nor no longer in training."

In the Elderly Dispositions, an elderly householder by birth is called an "elder by birth."

"There are these four qualities, monks, that make one an elder. Here, monks, an elder is virtuous, learned, attains the four jhānas, destroys the āsavas, is learned, attains the four jhānas, and by destroying the āsavas, dwells having realized with his own insight, here and now, the cetovimutti and paññāvimutti that are free from āsavas. These, monks, are the four qualities that make one an elder" (A. Ni. 4.22).

Thus, in the teachings that were spoken, one who is endowed with one or many is called an "elder in the dhamma."

Another monk named Thera, thus named Thera, or whomever the novices and others see as an old man and call "elder," this is called a "conventional elder."

Elder by birth, elder in the Dhamma, conventional elder.

Among the bases for meritorious action, giving itself is "consisting of giving." That meritorious action and that object of those benefits is called a "base for meritorious action."

In the other two as well, this is the method. In meaning, however, these three bases of meritorious action should be understood along with the intention consisting of giving previously mentioned, and with the intentions of the prior and subsequent stages.

For each one, beginning from the prior stage, when one does it with the body, there is bodily action. While uttering speech for that purpose, there is verbal action. Without stirring the bodily and verbal actions, while thinking in the mind, there is mental action.

While giving food and so forth, also thinking, "I am giving food and so forth," or reflecting on the perfection of giving, there is a base for meritorious action consisting of giving at the time of giving.

While standing at the head of the bed and giving, it consists of ethical discipline.

Having established contemplation on cessation and decay, while giving, there is a base for meritorious action consisting of cultivation.

Bases for meritorious action, consisting of giving.

Base for meritorious action.

Furthermore, there are these seven other bases for meritorious action: a base for meritorious action accompanied by respect, accompanied by service, giving transference of merit, rejoicing in others’ merit, consisting of teaching, consisting of hearing, and consisting of straightening one’s view.
There, having seen an elder, it should be understood as being accompanied by honoring (apaciti), by way of rising to greet, receiving the bowl and robe, respectful salutation, offering a seat, and so forth.

Due to the practice of duties towards those who are older, having seen a bhikṣu who has entered a village for alms, taking his bowl, causing the village to be pleased with the alms, by way of approaching, having heard "Go, bring the bowl for the bhikṣus," going quickly, and by way of bringing the bowl and so forth, it should be understood as being accompanied by service (veyyāvacca).

Having given the four requisites, "May the merit be for all beings," it should be understood as giving the transfer of merit by way of bestowing the offering.

Having approved the merit given by others, "Good, excellent!" it should be understood as rejoicing in the merit of others.

One dwells with the intention that "In this way, they will recognize me as a preacher of the dharma," becoming heavy with gain, he teaches; that is not of great fruit.

One, not expecting reward, teaches the qualities of his own excellence to others; this is called the meritorious object of teaching (desanāmayaṃ puṇñakiriyavatthu).

One, hearing, thinks "In this way, they will recognize me as faithful"; that is not of great fruit.

One, with a softened mind and diffusion of benefit, hears the dharma thinking, "In this way, great fruit will be mine"; this is the meritorious object of hearing (savanamayaṃ puññakiriyavatthu).

But the characteristic of fixed intention (niyamalakkhaṇaṃ) is for all those with right view (diṭṭhi).

Indeed, whatever merit one makes, due to right view (diṭṭhi) alone, it is of great fruit.

Thus, these seven meritorious objects (puññakiriyavatthu) are included in the former three.

Here, honoring (apaciti) and service (veyyāvacca) are in morality (sīlamaye).

Giving the transfer of merit and rejoicing in the merit of others are in generosity (dānamaye).

Teaching and hearing are in mental development (bhāvanāmaye).

Right view (diṭṭhi) goes to inclusion in all three.

The grounds for accusation (codanāvatthūni) are the causes for accusation (codanākāraṇāni).

"By sight (diṭṭhenā)" means having seen a transgression with the physical eye or with the divine eye, he accuses.

"By hearing (sutenā)" means having heard another's sound with the natural ear or with the divine ear, he accuses.

"Or by suspicion (parisaṅkāya vā)" means having been suspected by what is seen, or having been suspected by what is heard, or having been suspected by what is thought, he accuses.

This is the summary here; the expansion, however, should be understood in the same way as stated in the Samantapāsādikā.

Grounds for accusation (codanāvatthūni), by sight (diṭṭhenā), by hearing (sutenā), or by suspicion (parisaṅkāya vā):

"Enjoyments of sensual pleasure (kāmūpapattiyo)" means indulging in sensual pleasure, or the obtaining of sensual pleasure.

"Those with desires close at hand (paccupaṭṭhitakāmā)" means those with bound desires, with bound objects.

"Just as humans (seyyathāpi manussā)" means like humans.

Humans, indeed, constantly live under the control of an object.

Where their minds are attached, having given even a hundred or a thousand, having brought a woman, they enjoy constant enjoyment.

"Some gods (ekacce devā)” are those who dwell in the realm of the Four Great Kings (catudevaloka).

They, too, constantly live under the control of an object.

"Some beings in the states of loss (ekacce vinipātikā)” means having placed beings in Naraka, the remaining fish, turtles, and so forth constantly live under the control of an object.

The fish [is attached] to its female fish, the turtle to its female turtle.

"Having created and created (nimminitvā nimminitvā)" means whatever form they desire, according to blue, yellow, and so forth, having created that and that, like delightful deities with bodies, before the venerable Anuruddha.

"Delighting in creation (nimmānaratī)" means thus, in this way, delight is in the creation created by themselves; therefore, they are nimmānaratī.

"Controlling the creations of others (paranimmitakāmā)" means desires created by others.

For, having known their minds, others create whatever sensual enjoyment is pleasing to them; they live there under control.
How do they know another's mind? Through the habit of associating with them.

Just as a skilled cook, when the king is eating, knows what the king likes based on what he takes a lot of, so too, having known the object that is favored by nature, they create just that sort of thing.

They maintain their residence there and engage in sexual intercourse.

Some elders say, "Their sexual activity is accomplished merely by smiling, merely by glancing, and merely by embracing," but that is rejected in the commentary, saying, "But this is not so."

For, without touching tangible objects with the body, one cannot accomplish sexual activity.

For all beings in the realm of desire, desires are natural.

And this has been said:

The attainment of desires, desires that are present, just like humans.

Some gods, some are fallen.

Having created and created again.

Delighting in creation, causing others to create.

These six are beings in the realm of desire, successful in all desires;

How long is the lifespan of all of them counted together?

(Vibhanga 1023);

"Attainment of pleasure" means the acquisition of pleasure.

"Having produced and produced again, they dwell in happiness" means that they, having created happiness of the first jhāna below, experience the happiness of the resultant jhāna above—this is the meaning.

"Suffused with happiness" means soaked with the happiness of the second jhāna.

"Completely suffused" means soaked on all sides.

"Completely full" means perfectly full.

"Completely pervaded" is a synonym for that itself.

This too was said referring to only the happiness of the resultant jhāna.

"O happiness! O happiness!"—it seems great craving for existence arises in them.

Therefore, sometimes, occasionally, they utter such inspired utterances.

"It is peaceful indeed" means it is excellent indeed.

"Contented" means having become satisfied due to not desiring further happiness than that.

"Experiencing happiness" means they experience the happiness of the third jhāna.

Attainment of pleasure, having produced and produced again, they dwell in happiness, suffused with happiness, completely suffused, completely full, completely pervaded, O happiness! O happiness! It is peaceful indeed, contented, experiencing happiness.

"Learner's wisdom" means the seven noble wisdoms.

The wisdom of an arhat is "no longer learning."

The remaining wisdom is "neither learning nor not-learning."

Learner's wisdom, no longer learning.

Neither learning nor not-learning.

In regard to the cintāmaya, etc., this is the expansion: "There, what is wisdom born of thinking (cintāmayā prajñā)? Any preference, view, choice, opinion, judgment, liking for pondering over things, that concerns ownership of deeds or is in conformity with truth or is of such kind as to conform with ‘Materiality is impermanent’ or ‘Feeling … perception … formations … consciousness is impermanent’ that one acquires without hearing it from another—that is called wisdom born of thinking (cintāmayā prajñā).

There, what is wisdom born of hearing (śrutamayā prajñā)? Any preference, view, choice, opinion, judgment, liking for pondering over things that concerns ownership of deeds or is in conformity with truth or is of such kind as to conform with ‘Materiality is impermanent’ or ‘Feeling … perception … formations … consciousness is impermanent’ that one acquires after hearing it from another—that is called wisdom born of hearing (śrutamayā prajñā).

(There, what is wisdom born of meditation (bhāvanāmayā prajñā)?) All the wisdom of one who has attained is "wisdom born of meditation" (bhāvanāmayā prajñā)" (Vibhanga 768-69).

Wisdom born of thinking.

Wisdom born of thinking.

Wisdom born of hearing.
"bhāvanāmayā paññā:"

sutāvudha means suta eva āvudhaṃ. That is, the Tipiṭaka, the Buddha's word, is a weapon in the sense of its meaning. Indeed, relying on that, a bhikkhu, relying on the weapon of wisdom (paññāvudha), crosses the great wilderness of saṃsāra without being harmed, like a brave warrior who is unwavering in a great wilderness. Therefore, it was said: "An noble disciple with the sutāvudha, abandons the unskillful, cultivates the skillful, abandons the blameworthy, cultivates the blameless, and maintains a pure self” (A. Ni. 7.67).

sutāvudha

pavivekāvudha means “kāyaviveko, cittaviveko, upadhiviveko.” This threefold isolation itself is a weapon. Its distinction is that kāyaviveka is for those who are fond of renunciation, who have bodies that are the basis of isolation. Cittaviveka is for those with perfectly pure minds, who have attained the highest purification. Upadhiviveka is for individuals without defilements. Indeed, one who is fond of this threefold isolation is not afraid of anything. Therefore, this too is called a weapon in the sense of being a refuge. Wisdom (paññā), both mundane and supramundane, is a weapon, hence paññāvudha. Whoever has that is not afraid of anything, nor is anyone afraid of him. Therefore, that too is called a weapon in the sense of being a refuge.

pavivekāvudha, paññāvudha

anaññātaññassāmītindriya means the faculty that arises in one who has undertaken to know the Dhamma, which was previously unknown and unlearned. This is a designation for the knowledge of the path of stream-entry (sotāpatti).

aññindriya means the faculty that has the nature of knowledge, that is the nature of understanding. This is a designation for knowledge in six instances, beginning with the fruit of stream-entry.

aññātāvindriya means the faculty in those who have reached the end of the task of knowing among those who have known. This is a designation for the knowledge of the fruit of arahantship.

anaññātaññassāmītindriya, aññindriya, aññātāvindriya

maṃsacakkhu is eye-sensitivity ( cakkhupasādo).

dibbacakkhu is knowledge based on light.

paññācakkhu is wisdom, both mundane and supramundane.

maṃsacakkhu, dibbacakkhu, paññācakkhu

Among adhisīlasikkhā etc., adhisīlasikkhā is higher morality ( adhisīla ) and the training because it is to be trained ( sikkhā ). The same method applies to the other two as well. There, it should be understood that this is the distinction: morality is adhisīla, mind is adhicitta, and wisdom is adhipaññā—

adhisīlasikkhā, adhisīlasikkhā

sīla means the five precepts and the ten precepts; the restraint of the pātimokkha is called adhisīla. The eight attainments are citta, the jhāna that is the basis of insight (vipassanā) is adhicitta. Knowledge of the ownership of karma is paññā, the wisdom of insight (vipassanā) is adhipaññā. Indeed, the five precepts and the ten precepts exist even when a Buddha has not arisen, thus it is merely morality (sīla); the morality of the pātimokkha restraint exists only when a Buddha has arisen, thus it is higher morality (adhisīla). The same method applies to mind and wisdom as well.
Moreover, for one desiring nirvāṇa, taking up the five precepts or the ten precepts is indeed higher morality (adhiśīla).
Having attained the eight attainments (aṭṭha samāpatti), it is indeed higher mind (adhicitta).
All worldly morality is simply morality (śīla), [while] supramundane is higher morality (adhiśīla).
The same method applies in the case of mental wisdom (cittapaññā).

Morality (śīla), Higher Morality (adhiśīla), Higher Mind (adhicitta).
Higher Wisdom (adhipaññā).

In cultivations (bhāvanāsu), the body consisting of the five sense doors of one with outflows destroyed is called body cultivation (kāyabhāvanā).
The eight attainments (aṭṭha samāpatti) are called mind cultivation (cittabhāvanā).
The wisdom of the fruit of arhatship is called wisdom cultivation (paññābhāvanā).
For one with outflows destroyed, the body consisting of the five sense doors is well cultivated simply and entirely.
And for him, the eight attainments are not weak like those of others, and his wisdom is cultivated; it is called the attainment of the fullness of wisdom.
Therefore, it is said thus.

In cultivations (bhāvanāsu), [there is] body cultivation (kāyabhāvanā), mind cultivation (cittabhāvanā), wisdom cultivation (paññābhāvanā).
In the unsurpassed things (anuttariyesu), insight (vipassanā) [is] unsurpassed vision (dassanānuttariyaṃ), the path [is] unsurpassed practice (paṭipadānussariyaṃ).
The fruit [is] unsurpassed liberation (vimuttānuttariyaṃ).
Or, the fruit [is] unsurpassed vision (dassanānuttariyaṃ).
The path [is] unsurpassed practice (paṭipadānuttariyaṃ).
Nirvāṇa [is] unsurpassed liberation (vimuttānuttariyaṃ).
Or, nirvāṇa [is] unsurpassed vision (dassanānuttariyaṃ), because there is nothing further to be seen.
The path [is] unsurpassed practice (paṭipadānuttariyaṃ).
The fruit [is] unsurpassed liberation (vimuttānuttariyaṃ).
"Unsurpassed" (anuttariya) means the highest, the most excellent.

In the unsurpassed things (anuttariyesu), [there is] unsurpassed vision (dassanānuttariyaṃ), [and] unsurpassed practice (paṭipadānussariyaṃ).
Unsurpassed liberation (vimuttānuttariyaṃ).
Unsurpassed (anuttariya).
In concentrations (samādhīsu), the concentration of the first jhāna [is] with initial application and sustained application (savitakkasavicāro).
By the method of the pentad, the concentration of the second jhāna [is] with only sustained application but without initial application (avitakkavicāramatto).
The remainder [is] without initial application and without sustained application (avitakkaavicāro).

In concentrations (samādhīsu), [there is that which is] with initial application and sustained application (savitakkasavicāro).
[That which is] with only sustained application but without initial application (avitakkavicāramatto).
[And that which is] without initial application and without sustained application (avitakkaavicāro).

In emptiness (śūnyatā) and so on, there are three kinds of talk: from the perspective of arrival, from the perspective of quality, [and] from the perspective of object.
From the perspective of arrival, a certain bhikṣu after thoroughly entering into not-self, after seeing not-self, after emerging from not-self, his insight is called emptiness (śūnyatā).
Why? Because of the absence of defilements that make the characteristics of self.
By the arrival of insight, the concentration of the path (maggasamādhi) is called emptiness (śūnyatā).
By the arrival of the path, the concentration of the fruit (phalasamādhi) is called emptiness (śūnyatā).
Another, after thoroughly entering into impermanence, after seeing impermanence, after emerging from impermanence,
his insight is called signless (animittā).
Why? Because of the absence of defilements that make the characteristics of signs.With the coming of insight (vipassanā), the path-samādhi is called "signless" (animitto). With the coming of the path, the fruit is called "signless" (animittaṃ).

Furthermore, having entered into suffering, having seen suffering, and having emerged from suffering, his/her vipassanā is called "desireless" (appaṇihitā). Why is that? Because of the absence of afflictions that are the makers of desires (paṇidhikārakakilesābhāvā). With the coming of insight, the path-samādhi is called "desireless" (appaṇihito). With the coming of the path, the fruit is called "desireless" (appaṇihitaṃ) - this is the talk from the perspective of coming (āgamanato).

Moreover, path-samādhi is empty (suññato) because of being empty of passion etc. (rāgādīhi suññatattā), signless (animitto) because of the absence of the signs of passion etc. (rāganimittādīnaṃ abhāvā), desireless (appaṇihito) because of the absence of the desires of passion etc. (rāgapaṇidhiādīnaṃ abhāvā) - this is the talk from the perspective of qualities (saguṇato).

Nirvāṇa is empty (suññatañceva) because of being empty of passion etc. (rāgādīhi suññatattā) and signless and desireless because of the absence of the signs and desires of passion etc. (rāgādinimittapaṇidhīnañca abhāvā).

The path-samādhi with that as its object (tadārammaṇo) is empty (suññato), signless (animitto), desireless (appaṇihito). This is the talk from the perspective of the object (ārammaṇato).

"Purity" (soceyyānī) means those things which make for purity, the practices of purity. The detailed explanation here should be understood in terms of the three kinds of good conduct mentioned in the passage that begins, "What is purity of body (kāyasoceyyaṃ)? Abstaining from killing," and so forth.

"Austerities" (moneyyānī) means those things which make for sagehood, the practices of sagehood. Their detailed explanation [is as follows]: "What is austerity of body (kāyamoneyyaṃ)? Abandoning the three kinds of bodily misconduct is austerity of body; the three kinds of good bodily conduct are austerity of body; knowledge related to the body is austerity of body; complete understanding of the body is austerity of body; the path associated with complete understanding is austerity of body; abandoning desire and passion for the body is austerity of body; the attainment of the fourth jhāna through the cessation of bodily functions is austerity of body. What is austerity of speech (vacīmoneyyaṃ)? Abandoning the four kinds of verbal misconduct is austerity of speech; the four kinds of good verbal conduct are austerity of speech; knowledge related to speech is austerity of speech; complete understanding of speech is austerity of speech; the path associated with complete understanding is austerity of speech; abandoning desire and passion for speech is austerity of speech; the attainment of the second jhāna through the cessation of verbal functions is austerity of speech. What is austerity of mind (manomoneyyaṃ)? Abandoning the three kinds of mental misconduct is austerity of mind, the three kinds of good mental conduct are austerity of mind, knowledge related to the mind is austerity of mind, complete understanding of the mind is austerity of mind. The path associated with complete understanding, abandoning desire and passion for the mind, the attainment of the cessation of perception and feeling through the cessation of mental functions is austerity of mind."

Among skills (kosallesu), "growth" (āyo) means increase. "Decline" (apāyo) means non-increase. "Means" (upāyo) [is] the cause of that. Skillfulness is understanding them. But the detailed explanation has already been stated in the Vibhaṅga.

Indeed, it was said: "What is skill in growth? When one attends to these things, both unarisen unwholesome things do not arise, and arisen unwholesome things cease. Or when one attends to these things, both unarisen wholesome things arise, and arisen wholesome things conduce to greater abundance, expansion, cultivation, and perfection; that is, wisdom there, understanding…right view. This is called skill in growth. What is skill in decline? When one attends to these things, both unarisen wholesome things do not arise, and arisen wholesome things cease. Or when one attends to these things, both unarisen unwholesome things arise, and arisen unwholesome things conduce to greater abundance, expansion, cultivation, and perfection; that is, wisdom there, understanding…right view. This is called skill in decline. And all understanding of means thereto is skill in means."
This, however, should be understood as being solely by way of knowing the cause of its arising, for the purpose of treating it, when an emergent matter or a fear has arisen.

Madā means those things that are occurring by way of the state of intoxication.
Among them, "I am healthy, sixty or seventy years have passed, I have never even eaten a harītakī fruit piece before, but these other people go around saying, 'Such-and-such a place is hurting, I will eat medicine,'" thus, making pride in this way is called the intoxication of health.
"We will make merit in old age, for now, we are young," thus, staying in youth and making pride in this way is called the intoxication of youth.
"I have lived long, I am living long, I will live long; I have lived happily, I am living happily, I will live happily," thus, making pride in this way is called the intoxication of life.

Madā.
The intoxication of health.
The intoxication of youth.
The intoxication of life.

Ādhipateyyesu means that which has come from being an overlord is overlordship.
"I am so much in virtue, concentration, wisdom, and liberation, this is not suitable for me," thus, making oneself the chief, and making non-doing of evil the most important is called self-overlordship.
Making the world the overlord and making non-doing is called world-overlordship.
Making the supramundane dharma the overlord and making non-doing is called dharma-overlordship.
Ādhipateyyesu Self-overlordship World-overlordship Dharma-overlordship
Kathāvatthūnī means the causes of stories.
Atītaṃ vā addhāna means the past dharma, the meaning is the past aggregates.
Moreover, "O monks, whatever rūpa is past, ceased, and changed, the reckoning of it is 'it was', the designation of it is 'it was', the name of it is 'it was', the reckoning of it is not 'it is', the reckoning of it is not 'it will be'" (Saṃ.
Ni.
3.
62), thus, by the sutra that has come in this way in the path of expression, the meaning here should be illuminated.
Kathāvatthūnī Atītaṃ vā addhāna
Vijjā means wisdom by way of piercing the darkness.
Also, wisdom is by way of the state of making known.
For the knowledge of the recollection of previous existences pierces the darkness that is covering the previous existence that is arising, and because it makes the previous existence known, it is wisdom.
The knowledge of the passing away and rebirth pierces the darkness that is covering the passing away and rebirth, and because it makes that known, it is wisdom.
At the destruction of the āsavas, the knowledge pierces the darkness that is covering the four truths, and because it makes the four true dharmas known, it is wisdom.
Vijjā
Vihāresu the eight attainments are divine dwelling.
The four immeasurables are Brahmā dwelling.
The attainment of fruition is noble dwelling.

Vihāresu Divine dwelling.
Brahmā dwelling.
Noble dwelling.

The miracles are expanded in the Kevaṭṭa Sutta itself.

"These, friend," etc., should be connected in the same way as has been said.
Thus, while the elder was speaking about the eighty-hundred questions by way of the groups of three in the equal sixty, he showed the taste of harmony.

The explanation of the Tika is finished.

306. ‘‘Atthi kho, āvuso, tena bhagavatā jānatā passatā arahatā sammāsambuddhena cattāro dhammā sammadakkhātā. Tattha sabbeheva saṅgāyitabbaṃ, na vivaditabbaṃ…pe… atthāya hitāya sukhāya devamanussānaṃ. Katame cattāro? ‘‘Cattāro satipaṭṭhānā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu kāye kāyānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā, vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ. Vedanāsu vedanānupassī…pe… citte cittānupassī…pe… dhammesu dhammānupassī viharati ātāpī sampajāno satimā vineyya loke abhijjhādomanassaṃ. satipaṭṭhānā. ‘‘Cattāro sammappadhānā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu anuppannānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ anuppādāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati. Uppannānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati. Anuppannānaṃ kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ uppādāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati. Uppannānaṃ kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ ṭhitiyā asammosāya bhiyyobhāvāya vepullāya bhāvanāya pāripūriyā chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati. sammappadhānā. ‘‘Cattāro iddhipādā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu chandasamādhipadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgataṃ iddhipādaṃ bhāveti. Cittasamādhipadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgataṃ iddhipādaṃ bhāveti. Vīriyasamādhipadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgataṃ iddhipādaṃ bhāveti. Vīmaṃsāsamādhipadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgataṃ iddhipādaṃ bhāveti. iddhipādā. ‘‘Cattāri jhānāni. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ paṭhamajjhānaṃ (syā. kaṃ.) upasampajja viharati. Vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā ajjhattaṃ sampasādanaṃ cetaso ekodibhāvaṃ avitakkaṃ avicāraṃ samādhijaṃ pītisukhaṃ dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ dutiyajjhānaṃ (syā. kaṃ.) upasampajja viharati. Pītiyā ca virāgā upekkhako ca viharati sato ca sampajāno, sukhañca kāyena paṭisaṃvedeti, yaṃ taṃ ariyā ācikkhanti – ‘upekkhako satimā sukhavihārī’ti tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ tatiyajjhānaṃ (syā. kaṃ.) upasampajja viharati. Sukhassa ca pahānā dukkhassa ca pahānā, pubbeva somanassadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamā, adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ catutthaṃ jhānaṃ catutthajjhānaṃ (syā. kaṃ.) upasampajja viharati. jhānāni. paṭhamajjhānaṃ (syā. kaṃ.) dutiyajjhānaṃ (syā. kaṃ.) tatiyajjhānaṃ (syā. kaṃ.) catutthajjhānaṃ (syā. kaṃ.)
306. “There are, friends, four dharmas that are rightly expounded by the Blessed One—the one who knows, the one who sees, the Worthy One, rightly self-awakened. So it should all be recited together, without dispute… for the welfare, benefit, & happiness of devas and human beings. What are the four?

“The four smṛtyupasthānas. Here, friends, a bhikṣu abides, contemplating the body as body, ardent, fully aware, and mindful, after removing avarice and sorrow regarding the world. And when he dwells contemplating feelings in feelings… the mind in the mind… and mental objects in mental objects, earnestly, clearly comprehending, and mindfully, after having overcome desire and sorrow in regard to the world.

Smṛtyupasthānas.

“The four samyakprahāṇas. Here a bhikṣu generates desire, endeavors, activates persistence, upholds & exerts his intent for the sake of the non-arising of evil, unskillful qualities that have not yet arisen. He generates desire, endeavors, activates persistence, upholds & exerts his intent for the sake of the abandoning of evil, unskillful qualities that have arisen. He generates desire, endeavors, activates persistence, upholds & exerts his intent for the sake of the arising of skillful qualities that have not yet arisen. He generates desire, endeavors, activates persistence, upholds & exerts his intent for the maintenance, non-confusion, increase, plenitude, development, & culmination of skillful qualities that have arisen.

Samyakprahāṇas.

“The four ṛddhipādas. Here, friends, a bhikṣu develops the base of power endowed with concentration founded on desire and the fabrications of exertion (chandasamādhiprahānasaṅkhārasamannāgataṃ ṛddhipādaṃ bhāveti). He develops the base of power endowed with concentration founded on persistence and the fabrications of exertion (chittasamādhipadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgataṃ ṛddhipādaṃ bhāveti). He develops the base of power endowed with concentration founded on energy and the fabrications of exertion (vīryasamādhipadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgataṃ ṛddhipādaṃ bhāveti). He develops the base of power endowed with concentration founded on discrimination and the fabrications of exertion (vīmāṃsāsamādhipadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgataṃ ṛddhipādaṃ bhāveti).

Ṛddhipādas.

“The four dhyānas. Here, friends, a bhikṣu, quite secluded from sensuality, secluded from unskillful qualities, enters & remains in the first dhyāna: rapture & pleasure born of seclusion, accompanied by directed thought & evaluation (savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati). With the stilling of directed thoughts & evaluations, he enters & remains in the second dhyāna: rapture & pleasure born of concentration, unification of awareness free from directed thought & evaluation—internal assurance (avitakkaṃ avicāraṃ samādhijaṃ pītisukhaṃ dvitīyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati). With the fading of rapture, he remains equanimous, mindful, & alert, and senses pleasure with the body. He enters & remains in the third dhyāna, of which the noble ones declare, ‘Equanimous & mindful, one has a pleasant abiding’ (upekkhako satimā sukhavihārīti tṛtīyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati). With the abandoning of pleasure & pain—as with the earlier disappearance of elation & distress—he enters & remains in the fourth dhyāna: purity of equanimity & mindfulness, neither pleasure nor pain (aduḥkhamasukham upekṣāsatipāriśuddhiṃ caturthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati).
) Note: upasampajja viharati.

Jhānas.

306. Having shown the meaning of the aggregate by way of the triad, he now begins the discourse again in order to show [it] by way of the tetrad. There, the “tetrad of the foundations of mindfulness” has already been extensively explained.

In the tetrad of right efforts, chandaṃ janeti means: “whatever desire, will to do, wish to act, or wholesome desire for the Dhamma” -- thus, he generates the wish to act that was spoken of in that way. Vāyamati means he makes an effort. Vīriyaṃ ārabhati means he generates energy. Cittaṃ paggaṇhāti means he supports the mind. This is the summary here. But the detailed explanation has already come in the Sammappadhānavibhaṅga.

Chandaṃ janeti, vāyamati, vīriyaṃ ārabhati, cittaṃ paggaṇhāti.

In the foundations of success, the concentration that arises dependent on desire is chandasamādhi (concentration due to desire). The principal formative factors are padhānasaṅkhārā (principal formative factors). Samannāgatanti means endowed with those qualities. Iddhiyā pādaṃ, or iddhibhūtaṃ vā pādaṃ means iddhipādaṃ (base of power). So with the rest. This is the summary here, but the detailed explanation has already come in the Iddhipādavibhaṅga. But its meaning has been explained in the Visuddhimagga. The discourse on jhāna has also been extensively explained in the Visuddhimagga.

Chandasamādhi, padhānasaṅkhārā, samannāgata, iddhipādaṃ.
