It’s by reason of being consummate in virtue (śīlasampadā) and being consummate in views (dṛṣṭhisampadā) that beings—with the break-up of the body, after death—reappear in a good destination, a heavenly world.

“Five drawbacks of the unvirtuous person’s defect in virtue (dussīlassa sīlavipattiyā).
Here, friends, an unvirtuous person, defective in virtue, by reason of heedlessness undergoes the loss/confiscation of great wealth. This is the first drawback coming from an unvirtuous person’s defect in virtue (dussīlassa sīlavipattiyā).
And further, the bad reputation of the unvirtuous person, defective in virtue, gets spread about. This is the second drawback coming from an unvirtuous person’s defect in virtue (dussīlassa sīlavipattiyā).
And further, whatever assembly the unvirtuous person, defective in virtue, approaches—whether of noble warriors (kṣatriya), brāhmaṇas, householders (gṛhapati), or śramaṇas—he/she does so abashed & without confidence. This is the third drawback coming from an unvirtuous person’s defect in virtue (dussīlassa sīlavipattiyā).
And further, the unvirtuous person, defective in virtue, dies confused. This is the fourth drawback coming from an unvirtuous person’s defect in virtue (dussīlassa sīlavipattiyā).
And further, the unvirtuous person, defective in virtue—on the break-up of the body, after death—reappears in a plane of deprivation (apāya), a bad destination (durgati), a lower realm (vinipāta), hell (niraya). This is the fifth drawback coming from an unvirtuous person’s defect in virtue (dussīlassa sīlavipattiyā).

“Five rewards of the virtuous person’s consummation in virtue (ānisaṃsā sīlavato sīlasampadāya).
Here, friends, a virtuous person, consummate in virtue, by reason of heedfulness acquires a great mass of wealth. This is the first reward coming from a virtuous person’s consummation in virtue (sīlavato sīlasampadāya).
And further, the good reputation of the virtuous person, consummate in virtue, gets spread about. This is the second reward coming from a virtuous person’s consummation in virtue (sīlavato sīlasampadāya).
And further, whatever assembly the virtuous person, consummate in virtue, approaches—whether of noble warriors (kṣatriya), brāhmaṇas, householders (gṛhapati), or śramaṇas—he/she does so unabashed & with confidence. This is the third reward coming from a virtuous person’s consummation in virtue (sīlavato sīlasampadāya).
And further, the virtuous person, consummate in virtue, dies unconfused. This is the fourth reward coming from a virtuous person’s consummation in virtue (sīlavato sīlasampadāya).
And further, the virtuous person, consummate in virtue—on the break-up of the body, after death—reappears in a good destination (sugati), a heavenly world (svarga). This is the fifth reward coming from a virtuous person’s consummation in virtue (sīlavato sīlasampadāya).
“Friends, a monk making an accusation, when wanting to accuse another, may accuse the other after having established five qualities within himself: I will speak at the right time, not at the wrong time; I will speak truthfully, not falsely; I will speak gently, not harshly; I will speak in a beneficial way, not in a harmful way; I will speak with a mind of loving-kindness (maitrī-citta), not while harboring hatred.’

A monk making an accusation, wanting to accuse another, may accuse the other after having established these five qualities within himself.
316. ‘‘Abhabbo, āvuso, khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sañcicca pāṇa’’ntiādi desanāsīsameva, sotāpannādayopi pana abhabbā. Puthujjanakhīṇāsavānaṃ nindāpasaṃsatthampi evaṃ vuttaṃ. Puthujjano nāma gārayho, mātughātādīnipi karoti . Khīṇāsavo pana pāsaṃso, kunthakipillikaghātādīnipi na karotīti. Byasanesu viyassatīti byasanaṃ, hitasukhaṃ khipati viddhaṃsetīti attho. Ñātīnaṃ byasanaṃ ñātibyasanaṃ, corarogabhayādīhi ñātivināsoti attho. Bhogānaṃ byasanaṃ bhogabyasanaṃ, rājacorādivasena bhogavināsoti attho. Rogo eva byasanaṃ rogabyasanaṃ. Rogo hi ārogyaṃ byasati vināsetīti byasanaṃ, sīlassa byasanaṃ sīlabyasanaṃ. Dussīlyassetaṃ nāmaṃ. Sammādiṭṭhiṃ vināsayamānā uppannā diṭṭhi eva byasanaṃ diṭṭhibyasanaṃ. Ettha ca ñātibyasanādīni tīṇi neva akusalāni na tilakkhaṇāhatāni. Sīladiṭṭhibyasanadvayaṃ akusalaṃ tilakkhaṇāhataṃ. Teneva ‘‘nāvuso, sattā ñātibyasanahetu vā’’tiādimāha. ñātibyasanaṃ, bhogabyasanaṃ, rogabyasanaṃ. sīlabyasanaṃ. diṭṭhibyasanaṃ. Ñātisampadāti ñātīnaṃ sampadā pāripūrī bahubhāvo. Bhogasampadāyapi eseva nayo. Ārogyassa sampadā ārogyasampadā. Pāripūrī dīgharattaṃ arogatā. Sīladiṭṭhisampadāsupi eseva nayo . Idhāpi ñātisampadādayo no kusalā, na tilakkhaṇāhatā. Sīladiṭṭhisampadā kusalā, tilakkhaṇāhatā. Teneva ‘‘nāvuso, sattā ñātisampadāhetu vā’’tiādimāha. Ñātisampadā Bhogasampadāyapi ārogyasampadā. Sīladiṭṭhisampadāsupi Sīlavipattisīlasampattikathā mahāparinibbāne vitthāritāva. Codakenāti vatthusaṃsandassanā, āpattisaṃsandassanā, saṃvāsappaṭikkhepo, sāmīcippaṭikkhepoti catūhi codanāvatthūhi codayamānena. Kālena vakkhāmi no akālenāti ettha cuditakassa kālo kathito, na codakassa. Paraṃ codentena hi parisamajjhe vā uposathapavāraṇagge vā āsanasālābhojanasālādīsu vā na codetabbaṃ. Divāṭṭhāne nisinnakāle ‘‘karotāyasmā okāsaṃ, ahaṃ āyasmantaṃ vattukāmo’’ti evaṃ okāsaṃ kāretvā codetabbaṃ. Puggalaṃ pana upaparikkhitvā yo lolapuggalo abhūtaṃ vatvā bhikkhūnaṃ ayasaṃ āropeti, so okāsakammaṃ vināpi codetabbo. Bhūtenāti tacchena sabhāvena. Saṇhenāti maṭṭhena mudukena. Atthasañhitenāti atthakāmatāya hitakāmatāya upetena. Codakenā Kālena vakkhāmi no akālenā Bhūtenā Saṇhenā Atthasañhitenā Padhāniyaṅgapañcakavaṇṇanā
316. "A bhikkhu who is depleted of taints, reverend, is incapable of intentionally [taking] life," etc., is just the essence of the teaching. Moreover, even stream-enterers are incapable. Thus is spoken even blame and praise of ordinary people who are depleted of taints. An ordinary person is indeed blameworthy, he commits matricide, etc. But a khīṇāsava is praiseworthy, he does not commit killing lice and ants, etc.

By byasana, one is ruined; therefore, byasana. It throws down and destroys benefit and happiness; this is the meaning. Byasana of relatives is ñātibyasana; the meaning is destruction of relatives by thieves, disease, fear, etc. Byasana of possessions is bhogabyasana; the meaning is destruction of possessions due to kings, thieves, etc. Disease itself is byasana, [namely,] rogabyasana. For disease destroys health, it ruins [it]; therefore, byasana. Byasana of morality is sīlabyasana. This is a name for immorality. A view arisen destroying right view itself is byasana, [namely,] diṭṭhibyasana. And here, these three, ñātibyasana, etc., are neither unwholesome nor struck by the three characteristics. The pair, sīladiṭṭhibyasana, [are] unwholesome, struck by the three characteristics. Therefore, he said, "Reverend, beings [go] to ñātibyasana, etc."

Ñātibyasanaṃ,
bhogabyasanaṃ,
rogabyasanaṃ,
sīlabyasanaṃ,
diṭṭhibyasanaṃ.

"Ñātisampadā," that is, the perfection of relatives, abundance, numerousness. "Bhogasampadāyapi, even in the case of prosperity of possessions," this is the same method. Prosperity of health [is] ārogyasampadā. Perfection [is] long-lasting freedom from disease. "Sīladiṭṭhisampadāsupi, even in the case of prosperity of morality and views," this is the same method.

Here also, ñātisampadā, etc., [are] not wholesome, not struck by the three characteristics. Sīladiṭṭhisampadā [are] wholesome, struck by the three characteristics. Therefore, he said, "Reverend, beings [go] to ñātisampadā, etc."

Ñātisampadā,
bhogasampadāyapi,
ārogyasampadā.
sīladiṭṭhisampadāsupi

The talk of moral failure and moral success is extensively spoken of in the Great Passing Away Discourse.

"Codakenā," that is, by one who is rebuking with four reasons for rebuking: pointing out the object, pointing out the offense, objection to cohabitation, objection to proper conduct. "Kālena vakkhāmi no akālenā, I will speak at the right time, not at the wrong time," here the time is spoken of for the one being rebuked, not for the one rebuking. But further, one who is rebuking should not rebuke in the midst of an assembly, or at the uposatha or pavāraṇa, or in seat-halls, eating-halls, etc. When one is sitting during the day, [he should say,] "Venerable one, grant me leave, I wish to speak to the venerable one," thus, having caused [him to grant] leave, one should rebuke. But having carefully examined a person, if a greedy person speaks falsely and brings disgrace upon the bhikkhus, he should be rebuked even without a granting of leave. "Bhūtenā," that is, by what is true, by what is factual. "Saṇhenā," that is, by what is smooth, by what is gentle.
Atthasañhitenā means being endowed with the desire for meaning and the desire for benefit.

I will speak at the proper time to the questioner, not at the improper time, truthfully, gently, and meaningfully. Explanation of the five factors of effort.
317
317. ‘‘Pañca padhāniyaṅgāni. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu saddho hoti, saddahati tathāgatassa bodhiṃ – ‘itipi so bhagavā arahaṃ sammāsambuddho vijjācaraṇasampanno sugato, lokavidū anuttaro purisadammasārathi satthā devamanussānaṃ buddho bhagavā’ti. Appābādho hoti appātaṅko, samavepākiniyā gahaṇiyā samannāgato nātisītāya nāccuṇhāya majjhimāya padhānakkhamāya. Asaṭho hoti amāyāvī, yathābhūtaṃ attānaṃ āvikattā satthari vā viññūsu vā sabrahmacārīsu. Āraddhavīriyo viharati akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ upasampadāya thāmavā daḷhaparakkamo anikkhittadhuro kusalesu dhammesu. Paññavā hoti udayatthagāminiyā paññāya samannāgato ariyāya nibbedhikāya sammādukkhakkhayagāminiyā.
317. "Five factors of striving.

Here, friends, a bhikkhu has faith; he places faith in the Tathāgata's awakening: ‘Indeed, the Blessed One is worthy & rightly self-awakened, consummate in clear-knowing & conduct (vijjācaraṇa-sampanno), well-gone, an expert with regard to the cosmos, unexcelled trainer of people fit to be tamed (purisadammasārathi), teacher of devas & human beings, awakened, blessed.’

He is free from illness & discomfort (appābādho hoti appātaṅko), endowed with good digestion—not too cold, not too hot, of moderate strength—fit for exertion.

He is neither fraudulent nor deceitful (asaṭho hoti amāyāvī). He declares himself to the Teacher or to his observant companions in the holy life in line with what he actually is.

He keeps his persistence aroused for abandoning unskillful qualities & taking on skillful qualities (āraddhavīriyo viharati akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ upasampadāya). He is steadfast, solid in his effort, not shirking his duties with regard to skillful qualities.

He is wise; he possesses wisdom regarding rise and disappearance (udayatthagāminiyā paññāya) that is noble and penetrative and leads to the complete destruction of suffering.
317.Padhāniyaṅgānīti padhānaṃ vuccati padahanaṃ, padhānamassa atthīti padhāniyo, padhāniyassa bhikkhuno aṅgāni padhāniyaṅgāni. Saddhoti saddhāya samannāgato. Saddhā panesā āgamanasaddhā, adhigamanasaddhā, okappanasaddhā, pasādasaddhāti catubbidhā. Tattha sabbaññubodhisattānaṃ saddhā abhinīhārato āgatattā āgamanasaddhā nāma. Ariyasāvakānaṃ paṭivedhena adhigatattā adhigamanasaddhā nāma. Buddho dhammo saṅghoti vutte acalabhāvena okappanaṃ okappanasaddhā nāma. Pasāduppatti pasādasaddhā nāma. Idha okappanasaddhā adhippetā. Bodhinti catutthamaggañāṇaṃ. Taṃ suppaṭividdhaṃ tathāgatenāti saddahati. Desanāsīsameva cetaṃ, iminā pana aṅgena tīsupi ratanesu saddhā adhippetā. Yassa hi buddhādīsu pasādo balavā, tassa padhānavīriyaṃ ijjhati. Appābādhoti arogo. Appātaṅkoti niddukkho. Samavepākiniyāti samavipācanīyā. Gahaṇiyāti kammajatejodhātuyā. Nātisītāya nāccuṇhāyāti atisītagahaṇiko sītabhīrū hoti, accuṇhagahaṇiko uṇhabhīrū hoti, tesaṃ padhānaṃ na ijjhati. Majjhimagahaṇikassa ijjhati. Tenāha – ‘‘majjhimāya padhānakkhamāyā’’ti. Yathābhūtaṃ attānaṃ āvikattāti yathābhūtaṃ attano aguṇaṃ pakāsetā. Udayatthagāminiyāti udayañca atthaṅgamañca gantuṃ paricchindituṃ samatthāya, etena paññāsalakkhaṇapariggāhakaṃ udayabbayañāṇaṃ vuttaṃ . Ariyāyāti parisuddhāya. Nibbedhikāyāti anibbiddhapubbe lobhakkhandhādayo nibbijjhituṃ samatthāya. Sammā dukkhakkhayagāminiyāti tadaṅgavasena kilesānaṃ pahīnattā yaṃ yaṃ dukkhaṃ khīyati, tassa tassa dukkhassa khayagāminiyā. Iti sabbehi imehi padehi vipassanāpaññāva kathitā. Duppaññassa hi padhānaṃ na ijjhati. Suddhāvāsādipañcakavaṇṇanā
317. Padhāniyaṅgānī: padhānaṃ means exertion; one who has exertion is padhāniyo; the factors of a monk who has exertion are padhāniyaṅgāni.

Saddho: endowed with faith. This faith is fourfold: faith based on tradition (āgamanasaddhā), faith based on personal attainment (adhigamanasaddhā), faith consisting of conviction (okappanasaddhā), and faith consisting of serene joy (pasādasaddhā). Among these, the faith of the all-knowing Bodhisattas is called āgamanasaddhā, because it comes from aspiration. The faith of noble disciples (ariyasāvaka) is called adhigamanasaddhā because it is attained through penetration. Conviction in an unwavering manner when it is said “The Buddha, the Dharma, the Sangha” is called okappanasaddhā. The arising of serene joy is called pasādasaddhā. Here, okappanasaddhā is intended.

Bodhi means the fourth path knowledge (catutthamaggañāṇaṃ). He has faith that it has been well-penetrated by the Tathāgata. This is only the essence of the teaching; however, through this factor, faith in the three jewels is intended. For one in whom serene joy in the Buddha, etc., is strong, his exertion of energy succeeds.

Appābādho: without illness (arogo).

Appātaṅko: without suffering (niddukkho).

Samavepākiniyā: which digests well (samavipācanīyā).

Gahaṇiyā: by the element of heat born of karma (kammajatejodhātuyā).

Nātisītāya nāccuṇhāyā: one who takes what is too cold is fearful of cold; one who takes what is too hot is fearful of heat; their exertion does not succeed. One who takes what is moderate succeeds. Therefore, he said: “With moderate capacity for exertion.”

Yathābhūtaṃ attānaṃ āvikattā: one who reveals his own bad qualities as they actually are.

Udayatthagāminiyā: capable of going to and determining arising and passing away; by this, the knowledge of arising and passing away that grasps the characteristics of wisdom is stated.

Ariyāyā: perfectly pure (parisuddhāya).

Nibbedhikāyā: capable of penetrating the aggregates of greed, etc., which were not previously penetrated.

Sammā dukkhakkhayagāminiyā: because of the abandonment of defilements by means of its factors, it goes to the destruction of that suffering which is destroyed. Thus, by all these words, only insight wisdom (vipassanāpaññā) is spoken of. For one of poor wisdom, exertion does not succeed.

Explanation of the five Suddhāvāsas, etc.
318
318. ‘‘Pañca suddhāvāsā – avihā, atappā, sudassā, sudassī, akaniṭṭhā. ‘‘Pañca anāgāmino – antarāparinibbāyī, upahaccaparinibbāyī, asaṅkhāraparinibbāyī, sasaṅkhāraparinibbāyī, uddhaṃsotoakaniṭṭhagāmī. anāgāmino –
“Five Pure Abodes (śuddhāvāsa): Aviha, Atappa, Sudassa, Sudassī, Akaniṭṭha.

“Five non-returners (anāgāmin): one unbound in between (antarāparinibbāyī), one unbound on arrival (upahaccaparinibbāyī), one unbound without fabrication (asaṅkhāraparinibbāyī), one unbound with fabrication (sasaṅkhāraparinibbāyī), one going upstream to the Peerless (uddhaṃsotoakaniṭṭhagāmī).
318.Suddhāvāsāti suddhā idha āvasiṃsu āvasanti āvasissanti vāti suddhāvāsā. Suddhāti kilesamalarahitā anāgāmikhīṇāsavā. Avihātiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ, taṃ mahāpadāne vuttameva. Anāgāmīsu āyuno majjhaṃ anatikkamitvā antarāva kilesaparinibbānaṃ arahattaṃ patto antarāparinibbāyī nāma. Majjhaṃ upahacca atikkamitvā patto upahaccaparinibbāyī nāma. Asaṅkhārena appayogena akilamanto sukhena patto asaṅkhāraparinibbāyī nāma. Sasaṅkhārena sappayogena kilamanto dukkhena patto sasaṅkhāraparinibbāyī nāma. Ime cattāro pañcasupi suddhāvāsesu labbhanti. Uddhaṃsotoakaniṭṭhagāmīti ettha pana catukkaṃ veditabbaṃ. Yo hi avihāto paṭṭhāya cattāro devaloke sodhetvā akaniṭṭhaṃ gantvā parinibbāyati, ayaṃ uddhaṃsoto akaniṭṭhagāmī nāma. Yo avihāto dutiyaṃ vā tatiyaṃ vā catutthaṃ vā devalokaṃ gantvā parinibbāyati, ayaṃ uddhaṃsoto na akaniṭṭhagāmī nāma. Yo kāmabhavato akaniṭṭhesu nibbattitvā parinibbāyati, ayaṃ na uddhaṃsoto akaniṭṭhagāmī nāma. Yo heṭṭhā catūsu devalokesu tattha tattheva nibbattitvā parinibbāyati, ayaṃ na uddhaṃsoto na akaniṭṭhagāmī nāmāti. Anāgāmīsu antarāparinibbāyī upahaccaparinibbāyī asaṅkhāraparinibbāyī sasaṅkhāraparinibbāyī Uddhaṃsotoakaniṭṭhagāmī Cetokhilapañcakavaṇṇanā
318.

Śuddhāvāsa: śuddhā means "pure," idha means "here," āvasiṃsu, āvasanti, and āvasissanti mean "they dwelt," thus śuddhāvāsa.

Śuddhā means "free from the filth of defilements," anāgāmikhīṇāsavā means "non-returners whose outflows are destroyed."

What should be said regarding avihā, etc., has already been stated in the Mahāpadāna.

In regards to the anāgāmīs (non-returners):

One who, without overstepping the midpoint of their lifespan, attains arhatship and parinirvāṇa in between [rebirths] is called antarāparinirvāyī.

One who, having encountered obstacles and overcome them, attains [arhatship and parinirvāṇa] is called upahaccaparinirvāyī.

One who, without effort or exertion, easily attains [arhatship and parinirvāṇa] is called asaṅkhāraparinirvāyī.

One who, with effort and exertion, attains [arhatship and parinirvāṇa] with difficulty is called sasaṅkhāraparinirvāyī.

These four are found among the five śuddhāvāsas (pure abodes).

Regarding the term uddhaṃsotoakaniṭṭhagāmī, a fourfold distinction should be understood.

One who, starting from Aviha, purifies the four devalokas (god realms), goes to Akaniṣṭha, and attains parinirvāṇa, is called uddhaṃsoto akaniṭṭhagāmī (one who ascends and goes to Akaniṣṭha).

One who, starting from Aviha, goes to the second, third, or fourth devaloka and attains parinirvāṇa, is called uddhaṃsoto na akaniṭṭhagāmī (one who ascends but does not go to Akaniṣṭha).

One who, from the realm of desire, is reborn in Akaniṣṭha and attains parinirvāṇa, is na uddhaṃsoto akaniṭṭhagāmī (not one who ascends and goes to Akaniṣṭha).

One who, in the lower four devalokas, is reborn there and attains parinirvāṇa, is na uddhaṃsoto na akaniṭṭhagāmī (not one who ascends and does not go to Akaniṣṭha).

Anāgāmīsu

Antarāparinirvāyī Upahaccaparinirvāyī Asaṅkhāraparinirvāyī Sasaṅkhāraparinirvāyī Uddhaṃsotoakaniṭṭhagāmī

Explanation of the five mental hindrances (cetokhilapañcakavaṇṇanā)
319
319. ‘‘Pañca cetokhilā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu satthari kaṅkhati vicikicchati nādhimuccati na sampasīdati. Yo so, āvuso, bhikkhu satthari kaṅkhati vicikicchati nādhimuccati na sampasīdati, tassa cittaṃ na namati ātappāya anuyogāya sātaccāya padhānāya, yassa cittaṃ na namati ātappāya anuyogāya sātaccāya padhānāya, ayaṃ paṭhamo cetokhilo. Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu dhamme kaṅkhati vicikicchati…pe… saṅghe kaṅkhati vicikicchati… sikkhāya kaṅkhati vicikicchati… sabrahmacārīsu kupito hoti anattamano āhatacitto khilajāto. Yo so, āvuso, bhikkhu sabrahmacārīsu kupito hoti anattamano āhatacitto khilajāto, tassa cittaṃ na namati ātappāya anuyogāya sātaccāya padhānāya, yassa cittaṃ na namati ātappāya anuyogāya sātaccāya padhānāya, ayaṃ pañcamo cetokhilo.
319. The five mental blockages (cetokhilā).

Here, āvuso, a bhikkhu is doubtful (kaṅkhati) about the Teacher (satthari), is uncertain (vicikicchati), is undecided (nādhimuccati), and is unconfident (na sampasīdati).

When a monk (bhikkhu), āvuso, is doubtful (kaṅkhati) about the Teacher (satthari), is uncertain (vicikicchati), is undecided (nādhimuccati), and is not confident (na sampasīdati), then his mind (cittaṃ) does not incline (na namati) to ardency (ātappāya), commitment (anuyogāya), perseverance (sātaccāya), or exertion (padhānāya). When one's mind (cittaṃ) does not incline (na namati) to ardency (ātappāya), commitment (anuyogāya), perseverance (sātaccāya), or exertion (padhānāya), this is the first mental blockage (cetokhilo).

Again, āvuso, a bhikkhu is doubtful (kaṅkhati) about the Dhamma (dhamme), is uncertain (vicikicchati)…pe… is doubtful (kaṅkhati) about the Saṅgha (saṅghe), is uncertain (vicikicchati)… is doubtful (kaṅkhati) about the training (sikkhāya), is uncertain (vicikicchati)… is angered (kupito hoti) and unhappy (anattamano) with his fellows in the holy life (sabrahmacārīsu), his mind rigid and hostile (āhatacitto khilajāto).

When a monk (bhikkhu), āvuso, is angered (kupito hoti) and unhappy (anattamano) with his fellows in the holy life (sabrahmacārīsu), his mind rigid and hostile (āhatacitto khilajāto), then his mind (cittaṃ) does not incline (na namati) to ardency (ātappāya), commitment (anuyogāya), perseverance (sātaccāya), or exertion (padhānāya). When one's mind (cittaṃ) does not incline (na namati) to ardency (ātappāya), commitment (anuyogāya), perseverance (sātaccāya), or exertion (padhānāya), this is the fifth mental blockage (cetokhilo).
319.Cetokhilāti cittassa thaddhabhāvā. Satthari kaṅkhatīti satthu sarīre vā guṇe vā kaṅkhati. Sarīre kaṅkhamāno ‘‘dvattiṃsamahāpurisavaralakkhaṇapaṭimaṇḍitaṃ nāma sarīraṃ atthi nu kho natthī’’ti kaṅkhati. Guṇe kaṅkhamāno ‘‘atītānāgatapaccuppannajānanasamatthaṃ sabbaññutañāṇaṃ atthi nu kho natthī’’ti kaṅkhati. Ātappāyāti vīriyakaraṇatthāya. Anuyogāyāti punappunaṃ yogāya. Sātaccāyāti satatakiriyāya. Padhānāyāti padahanatthāya. Ayaṃ paṭhamo cetokhiloti ayaṃ satthari vicikicchāsaṅkhāto paṭhamo cittassa thaddhabhāvo. Dhammeti pariyattidhamme ca paṭivedhadhamme ca. Pariyattidhamme kaṅkhamāno ‘‘tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ caturāsītidhammakkhandhasahassānīti vadanti, atthi nu kho etaṃ natthī’’ti kaṅkhati. Paṭivedhadhamme kaṅkhamāno ‘‘vipassanānissando maggo nāma, magganissando phalaṃ nāma, sabbasaṅkhārapaṭinissaggo nibbānaṃ nāmāti vadanti, taṃ atthi nu kho natthī’’ti kaṅkhati. Saṅghe kaṅkhatīti ‘‘ujuppaṭipannotiādīnaṃ padānaṃ vasena evarūpaṃ paṭipadaṃ paṭipanno cattāro maggaṭṭhā cattāro phalaṭṭhāti aṭṭhannaṃ puggalānaṃ samūhabhūto saṅgho nāma atthi nu kho natthī’’ti kaṅkhati. Sikkhāya kaṅkhamāno ‘‘adhisīlasikkhā nāma, adhicittaadhipaññāsikkhā nāmāti vadanti , sā atthi nu kho natthī’’ti kaṅkhati. Ayaṃ pañcamoti ayaṃ sabrahmacārīsu kopasaṅkhāto pañcamo cittassa thaddhabhāvo kacavarabhāvo khāṇukabhāvo. Cetasovinibandhādipañcakavaṇṇanā
319. Cetokhilā means rigidity of mind (cittassa thaddhabhāvā).
Satthari kaṅkhatī means doubting the Teacher (satthu), either the Teacher's body or the Teacher's qualities (guṇe).
Doubting the body (sarīre), one doubts, "Is there indeed a body called 'endowed with the thirty-two marks of a great man' (dvattiṃsamahāpurisavaralakkhaṇa paṭimaṇḍitaṃ nāma sarīraṃ atthi nu kho natthī),"
Doubting the qualities (guṇe), one doubts, "Is there indeed the knowledge of omniscience capable of knowing the past, future and present (atītānāgatapaccuppanna jānanasamatthaṃ sabbaññutañāṇaṃ atthi nu kho natthī)?"
Ātappāyā means for the sake of making an effort (vīriyakaraṇatthāya).
Anuyogāyā means repeated practice (punappunaṃ yogāya).
Sātaccāyāti means continuous action (satatakiriyāya).
Padhānāyāti means for the sake of striving (padahanatthāya).
Ayaṃ paṭhamo cetokhilo means this is the first mental impediment (vicikicchāsaṅkhāto paṭhamo cittassa thaddhabhāvo) regarding the Teacher (satthari).
Dhamme means in the Dharma (pariyattidhamme) of learning and the Dharma of realization (paṭivedhadhamme).
Doubting the Dharma of learning (pariyattidhamme), one doubts, "They say there are eighty-four thousand collections of teachings, the word of the Buddha in the three baskets (tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ caturāsītidhammakkhandha sahassānīti vadanti), is this so or is it not (atthi nu kho etaṃ natthī)?"
Doubting the Dharma of realization (paṭivedhadhamme), one doubts, "They say the path is the outflow of insight (vipassanānissando maggo nāma), the fruit is the outflow of the path (magganissando phalaṃ nāma), the complete cessation of all formations is nirvāṇa (sabbasaṅkhāra paṭinissaggo nibbānaṃ nāmāti vadanti), is that so or is it not (taṃ atthi nu kho natthī)?"
Saṅghe kaṅkhatī means "Having entered the right practice" (ujuppaṭipannotiādīnaṃ padānaṃ vasena), having undertaken such a practice, there are four who are standing in the path (cattāro maggaṭṭhā), four who are standing in the fruit (cattāro phalaṭṭhāti). Is there indeed a saṅgha, a community which is a collection of eight types of individuals (aṭṭhannaṃ puggalānaṃ samūhabhūto saṅgho nāma atthi nu kho natthī)?" one doubts.
Sikkhāya kaṅkhamāno doubting the training, “There is higher training in morality (adhisīlasikkhā nāma), higher training in mind, and higher training in wisdom” (adhicitta adhipaññāsikkhā nāmāti vadanti), is that so or is it not (sā atthi nu kho natthī)?" one doubts.
Ayaṃ pañcamo means this is the fifth (pañcamo) - that is, anger (kopasaṅkhāto) among those who live the holy life together (sabrahmacārīsu)- the fifth rigidity of mind (cittassa thaddhabhāvo), the nature of rubbish (kacavarabhāvo), the nature of stubborness (khāṇukabhāvo).

Explanation of the five bondages of awareness, and so forth (cetasovinibandhādipañcakavaṇṇanā).
320
320. ‘‘Pañca cetasovinibandhā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu kāmesu avītarāgo hoti avigatacchando avigatapemo avigatapipāso avigatapariḷāho avigatataṇho. Yo so, āvuso, bhikkhu kāmesu avītarāgo hoti avigatacchando avigatapemo avigatapipāso avigatapariḷāho avigatataṇho, tassa cittaṃ na namati ātappāya anuyogāya sātaccāya padhānāya. Yassa cittaṃ na namati ātappāya anuyogāya sātaccāya padhānāya. Ayaṃ paṭhamo cetaso vinibandho. Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu kāye avītarāgo hoti…pe… rūpe avītarāgo hoti…pe… puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu yāvadatthaṃ udarāvadehakaṃ bhuñjitvā seyyasukhaṃ passasukhaṃ middhasukhaṃ anuyutto viharati…pe… puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu aññataraṃ devanikāyaṃ paṇidhāya brahmacariyaṃ carati – ‘imināhaṃ sīlena vā vatena vā tapena vā brahmacariyena vā devo vā bhavissāmi devaññataro vā’ti. Yo so, āvuso, bhikkhu aññataraṃ devanikāyaṃ paṇidhāya brahmacariyaṃ carati – ‘imināhaṃ sīlena vā vatena vā tapena vā brahmacariyena vā devo vā bhavissāmi devaññataro vā’ti, tassa cittaṃ na namati ātappāya anuyogāya sātaccāya padhānāya. Yassa cittaṃ na namati ātappāya anuyogāya sātaccāya padhānāya. Ayaṃ pañcamo cetaso vinibandho. ‘‘Pañcindriyāni – cakkhundriyaṃ, sotindriyaṃ, ghānindriyaṃ, jivhindriyaṃ, kāyindriyaṃ. ‘‘Pañcindriyāni – ‘‘Aparānipi pañcindriyāni – sukhindriyaṃ, dukkhindriyaṃ, somanassindriyaṃ, domanassindriyaṃ, upekkhindriyaṃ. pañcindriyāni – ‘‘Aparānipi pañcindriyāni – saddhindriyaṃ, vīriyindriyaṃ, satindriyaṃ, samādhindriyaṃ, paññindriyaṃ. pañcindriyāni –
320. The five bondages of awareness ( cetasovinibandhā ):

Here, friends, a monk is not free of passion, desire, love, thirst, fever, or craving for sensual pleasures ( kāmesu avītarāgo hoti avigatacchando avigatapemo avigatapipāso avigatapariḷāho avigatataṇho ). When a monk is not free of passion, desire, love, thirst, fever, or craving for sensual pleasures, then his mind doesn’t tend toward ardency ( ātappāya ), commitment ( anuyogāya ), perseverance ( sātaccāya ), or exertion ( padhānāya ). When one’s mind doesn’t tend toward ardency, commitment, perseverance, or exertion. This is the first bondage of awareness ( pathamo cetaso vinibandho ).

Again, friends, a monk is not free of passion for the body ( kāye avītarāgo hoti )…for forms ( rūpe avītarāgo hoti )… Again, friends, a monk, having eaten as much as his belly will hold, remains committed to the pleasure of lying down ( seyyasukhaṃ ), the pleasure of contact ( passasukhaṃ ), the pleasure of drowsiness ( middhasukhaṃ )… Again, friends, a monk practices the holy life intent on being born in one or another of the deva hosts, ‘By this virtue ( sīlena ) or practice ( vatena ) or abstinence ( tapena ) or holy life ( brahmacariyena ) I will be a deva ( devo ) of one sort or another ( devaññataro vā )’. When a monk practices the holy life intent on being born in one or another of the deva hosts, ‘By this virtue or practice or abstinence or holy life I will be a deva of one sort or another,’ then his mind doesn’t tend toward ardency, commitment, perseverance, or exertion. When one’s mind doesn’t tend toward ardency, commitment, perseverance, or exertion. This is the fifth bondage of awareness ( pañcamo cetaso vinibandho ).

The five faculties ( pañcindriyāni ): the faculty of the eye ( cakkhundriyaṃ ), the faculty of the ear ( sotindriyaṃ ), the faculty of the nose ( ghānindriyaṃ ), the faculty of the tongue ( jivhindriyaṃ ), the faculty of the body ( kāyindriyaṃ ).

The five faculties:

Again, the five faculties: the faculty of pleasure ( sukhindriyaṃ ), the faculty of pain ( dukkhindriyaṃ ), the faculty of happiness ( somanassindriyaṃ ), the faculty of distress ( domanassindriyaṃ ), the faculty of equanimity ( upekkhindriyaṃ ).

The five faculties:

Again, the five faculties: the faculty of conviction ( saddhindriyaṃ ), the faculty of persistence ( vīriyindriyaṃ ), the faculty of mindfulness ( satindriyaṃ ), the faculty of concentration ( samādhindriyaṃ ), the faculty of discernment ( paññindriyaṃ ).

The five faculties:
320.Cetasovinibandhāti cittaṃ bandhitvā muṭṭhiyaṃ katvā viya gaṇhantīti cetasovinibandhā. Kāmeti vatthukāmepi kilesakāmepi. Kāyeti attano kāye. Rūpeti bahiddhārūpe. Yāvadatthanti yattakaṃ icchati, tattakaṃ. Udarāvadehakanti udarapūraṃ. Tañhi udaraṃ avadehanato ‘‘udarāvadehaka’’nti vuccati. Seyyasukhanti mañcapīṭhasukhaṃ. Passasukhanti yathā samparivattakaṃ sayantassa dakkhiṇapassavāmapassānaṃ sukhaṃ hoti, evaṃ uppannaṃ sukhaṃ. Middhasukhanti niddāsukhaṃ. Anuyuttoti yuttappayutto viharati. Paṇidhāyāti patthayitvā. Brahmacariyenāti methunaviratibrahmacariyena. Devo vā bhavissāmīti mahesakkhadevo vā bhavissāmi. Devaññataro vāti appesakkhadevesu vā aññataro. Indriyesu paṭhamapañcake lokiyāneva kathitāni. Dutiyapañcake paṭhamadutiyacatutthāni lokiyāni, tatiyapañcamāni lokiyalokuttarāni. Tatiyapañcake samathavipassanāmaggavasena lokiyalokuttarāni. Indriyesu Nissaraṇiyapañcakavaṇṇanā
320.
"Cetasovinibandhā" means cittam having been bound, as if having been made into a fist, they grasp; thus, cetasovinibandhā.
"Kāme" means with regard to desire for objects and desire for defilements.
"Kāye" means in one's own body (kāye).
"Rūpe" means external forms.
"Yāvadattha" means as much as one wishes, so much.
"Udarāvadehaka" means filling the stomach (udarapūraṃ).
Indeed, that stomach, from filling, is called "Udarāvadehaka."
"Seyyasukha" means happiness on a couch or seat.
"Passasukha" means just as for one lying down alternating sides, happiness occurs on the right side or the left side, thus, arisen happiness.
"Middhasukha" means the happiness of sleep (niddāsukhaṃ).
"Anuyutto" means he dwells diligently and devotedly.
"Paṇidhāyā" means having desired.
"Brahmacariyenā" means by the practice of celibacy, abstaining from sexual intercourse (methunaviratibrahmacariyena).
"Devo vā bhavissāmī" means I will become a god of great power, or a god.
"Devaññataro vā" means either one among the gods of little power, or another.

In the indriya, only mundane things are spoken of in the first group of five.
In the second group of five, the first, second, and fourth are mundane; the third and fifth are mundane and supramundane.
In the third group of five, through the path of tranquility and insight (śamathavipaśyanā), there are both mundane and supramundane qualities.

In the indriya, description of the group of five that leads to liberation (nissaraṇiyapañcakavaṇṇanā).
321
321. ‘‘Pañca nissaraṇiyānissāraṇīyā (sī. syā. kaṃ. pī.) ṭīkā oloketabbādhātuyo. Idhāvuso, bhikkhuno kāme manasikaroto kāmesu cittaṃ na pakkhandati na pasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati. Nekkhammaṃ kho panassa manasikaroto nekkhamme cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati. Tassa taṃ cittaṃ sugataṃ subhāvitaṃ suvuṭṭhitaṃ suvimuttaṃ visaṃyuttaṃ kāmehi. Ye ca kāmapaccayā uppajjanti āsavā vighātā pariḷāhā vighātapariḷāhā (syā. kaṃ.), mutto so tehi, na so taṃ vedanaṃ vedeti. Idamakkhātaṃ kāmānaṃ nissaraṇaṃ. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhuno byāpādaṃ manasikaroto byāpāde cittaṃ na pakkhandati na pasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati. Abyāpādaṃ kho panassa manasikaroto abyāpāde cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati. Tassa taṃ cittaṃ sugataṃ subhāvitaṃ suvuṭṭhitaṃ suvimuttaṃ visaṃyuttaṃ byāpādena. Ye ca byāpādapaccayā uppajjanti āsavā vighātā pariḷāhā, mutto so tehi, na so taṃ vedanaṃ vedeti. Idamakkhātaṃ byāpādassa nissaraṇaṃ. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhuno vihesaṃ manasikaroto vihesāya cittaṃ na pakkhandati na pasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati. Avihesaṃ kho panassa manasikaroto avihesāya cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati. Tassa taṃ cittaṃ sugataṃ subhāvitaṃ suvuṭṭhitaṃ suvimuttaṃ visaṃyuttaṃ vihesāya. Ye ca vihesāpaccayā uppajjanti āsavā vighātā pariḷāhā, mutto so tehi, na so taṃ vedanaṃ vedeti. Idamakkhātaṃ vihesāya nissaraṇaṃ. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhuno rūpe manasikaroto rūpesu cittaṃ na pakkhandati na pasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati. Arūpaṃ kho panassa manasikaroto arūpe cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati. Tassa taṃ cittaṃ sugataṃ subhāvitaṃ suvuṭṭhitaṃ suvimuttaṃ visaṃyuttaṃ rūpehi. Ye ca rūpapaccayā uppajjanti āsavā vighātā pariḷāhā, mutto so tehi, na so taṃ vedanaṃ vedeti. Idamakkhātaṃ rūpānaṃ nissaraṇaṃ. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhuno sakkāyaṃ manasikaroto sakkāye cittaṃ na pakkhandati na pasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati. Sakkāyanirodhaṃ kho panassa manasikaroto sakkāyanirodhe cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati. Tassa taṃ cittaṃ sugataṃ subhāvitaṃ suvuṭṭhitaṃ suvimuttaṃ visaṃyuttaṃ sakkāyena. Ye ca sakkāyapaccayā uppajjanti āsavā vighātā pariḷāhā, mutto so tehi, na so taṃ vedanaṃ vedeti. Idamakkhātaṃ sakkāyassa nissaraṇaṃ.
321. "Five nissaraṇīya elements. Here, friends, when a monk attends to sensuality, his mind doesn’t leap up at sensuality, doesn’t grow confident, steadfast, or released in sensuality. But when attending to renunciation (naikṣammya), his mind leaps up at renunciation, grows confident, steadfast, & released in renunciation. When his mind is rightly-gone (sugata), rightly developed (subhāvita), has rightly risen above (suvuṭṭhita), gained release (suvimutta), & become disjoined from sensuality, then whatever effluents (āsava), torments (vighāta), & fevers (pariḷāha) there are that arise in dependence on sensuality, he is released from them. He doesn’t experience that feeling. This is expounded as the escape from sensuality (kāmānaṃ nissaraṇaṃ).

“And further, friends, when a monk attends to ill will (byāpāda), his mind doesn’t leap up at ill will, doesn’t grow confident, steadfast, or released in ill will. But when attending to non-ill will (abyāpāda), his mind leaps up at non-ill will, grows confident, steadfast, & released in non-ill will. When his mind is rightly-gone, rightly developed, has rightly risen above, gained release, & become disjoined from ill will, then whatever effluents, torments, & fevers there are that arise in dependence on ill will, he is released from them. He doesn’t experience that feeling. This is expounded as the escape from ill will (byāpādassa nissaraṇaṃ).

“And further, friends, when a monk attends to harmfulness (vihesā), his mind doesn’t leap up at harmfulness, doesn’t grow confident, steadfast, or released in harmfulness. But when attending to harmlessness (avihesā), his mind leaps up at harmlessness, grows confident, steadfast, & released in harmlessness. When his mind is rightly-gone, rightly developed, has rightly risen above, gained release, & become disjoined from harmfulness, then whatever effluents, torments, & fevers there are that arise in dependence on harmfulness, he is released from them. He doesn’t experience that feeling. This is expounded as the escape from harmfulness (vihesāya nissaraṇaṃ).

“And further, friends, when a monk attends to forms (rūpa), his mind doesn’t leap up at forms, doesn’t grow confident, steadfast, or released in forms. But when attending to the formless (arūpa), his mind leaps up at the formless, grows confident, steadfast, & released in the formless. When his mind is rightly-gone, rightly developed, has rightly risen above, gained release, & become disjoined from forms, then whatever effluents, torments, & fevers there are that arise in dependence on forms, he is released from them. He doesn’t experience that feeling. This is expounded as the escape from forms (rūpānaṃ nissaraṇaṃ).

“And further, friends, when a monk attends to self-identification (sakkāya), his mind doesn’t leap up at self-identification, doesn’t grow confident, steadfast, or released in self-identification. But when attending to the cessation of self-identification (sakkāyanirodha), his mind leaps up at the cessation of self-identification, grows confident, steadfast, & released in the cessation of self-identification. When his mind is rightly-gone, rightly developed, has rightly risen above, gained release, & become disjoined from self-identification, then whatever effluents, torments, & fevers there are that arise in dependence on self-identification, he is released from them.
This is expounded as the escape from self-identification ( sakkāyassa nissaraṇaṃ).
321.Nissaraṇiyāti nissaṭā visaññuttā. Dhātuyoti attasuññasabhāvā. Kāme manasikarototi kāme manasikarontassa, asubhajjhānato vuṭṭhāya agadaṃ gahetvā visaṃ vīmaṃsanto viya vīmaṃsanatthaṃ kāmābhimukhaṃ cittaṃ pesentassāti attho. Na pakkhandatīti na pavisati. Na pasīdatīti pasādaṃ nāpajjati. Nasantiṭṭhatīti na patiṭṭhati. Na vimuccatīti nādhimuccati. Yathā pana kukkuṭapattaṃ vā nhārudaddulaṃ vā aggimhi pakkhittaṃ patilīyati patikuṭati pativattati na sampasāriyati; evaṃ patilīyati na pasāriyati. Nekkhammaṃ kho panāti idha nekkhammaṃ nāma dasasu asubhesu paṭhamajjhānaṃ, tadassa manasikaroto cittaṃ pakkhandati. Tassa taṃ cittanti tassa taṃ asubhajjhānacittaṃ. Sugatanti gocare gatattā suṭṭhu gataṃ. Subhāvitanti ahānabhāgiyattā suṭṭhu bhāvitaṃ. Suvuṭṭhitanti kāmato suṭṭhu vuṭṭhitaṃ. Suvimuttanti kāmehi suṭṭhu vimuttaṃ. Kāmapaccayā āsavā nāma kāmahetukā cattāro āsavā. Vighātāti dukkhā. Pariḷāhāti kāmarāgapariḷāhā. Na so taṃ vedanaṃ vedetīti so taṃ kāmavedanaṃ vighātapariḷāhavedanañca na vedayati. Idamakkhātaṃ kāmānaṃ nissaraṇanti idaṃ asubhajjhānaṃ kāmehi nissaṭattā kāmānaṃ nissaraṇanti akkhātaṃ. Yo pana taṃ jhānaṃ pādakaṃ katvā saṅkhāre sammasanto tatiyaṃ maggaṃ patvā anāgāmiphalena nibbānaṃ disvā puna kāmā nāma natthīti jānāti, tassa cittaṃ accantanissaraṇameva. Sesapadesupi eseva nayo. Ayaṃ pana viseso, dutiyavāre mettājhānāni byāpādassa nissaraṇaṃ nāma. Tatiyavāre karuṇājhānāni vihiṃsāya nissaraṇaṃ nāma. Catutthavāre arūpajjhānāni rūpānaṃ nissaraṇaṃ nāma. Accantanissaraṇe cettha arahattaphalaṃ yojetabbaṃ. Pañcamavāre sakkāyaṃ manasikarototi suddhasaṅkhāre pariggaṇhitvā arahattaṃ pattassa sukkhavipassakassa phalasamāpattito vuṭṭhāya vīmaṃsanatthaṃ pañcupādānakkhandhābhimukhaṃ cittaṃ pesentassa. Idamakkhātaṃ sakkāyanissaraṇanti idaṃ arahattamaggena ca phalena ca nibbānaṃ disvā ṭhitassa bhikkhuno puna sakkāyo natthīti uppannaṃ arahattaphalasamāpatticittaṃ sakkāyassa nissaraṇanti akkhātaṃ. sakkāyaṃ manasikaroto Idamakkhātaṃ sakkāyanissaraṇa Vimuttāyatanapañcakavaṇṇanā
321. Nissaraṇīyā means escaped, disconnected.

Dhātuyo means having the nature of emptiness of self ( attasuññasabhāvā).

Kāme manasikaroto means to one who attends to sensual pleasures, to one who, having arisen from contemplation of the unattractive, having taken an antidote, examines the poison, as if sending the mind towards sensual pleasures for the purpose of investigation, this is the meaning.

Na pakkhandatī means does not flow into.

Na pasīdatī means does not attain clarity.

Na santiṭṭhatī means does not become established.

Na vimuccatī means does not become liberated.

Just as a cock's feather or a strip of sinew, thrown into a fire, shrinks away from it, turns back from it, recoils and is not drawn toward it; so it shrinks away, it is not drawn near.

Nekkhammaṃ kho panā here nekkhamma means the first jhāna among the ten kinds of the unattractive; the mind of one who attends to that, flows into it.

Tassa taṃ cittanti that mind, that unattractive jhāna mind.

Sugata means well gone, because of having gone to its object.

Subhāvita means well developed, because of being conducive to abandonment.

Suvuṭṭhita means well arisen, having well arisen from sensuality.

Suvimutta means well liberated, well liberated from sensual pleasures.

Kāmapaccayā āsavā means the four āsavas caused by sensual pleasure.

Vighātā means suffering.

Pariḷāhā means the burning of sensual lust.

Na so taṃ vedanaṃ vedetī means he does not experience that feeling of sensual pleasure, or the feeling of suffering and burning.

Idamakkhātaṃ kāmānaṃ nissaraṇanti this contemplation of the unattractive, because it is escaped from sensual pleasures, is said to be the escape from sensual pleasures.

But whoever, having made that jhāna a basis, contemplates conditioned things, having attained the third path, having seen nirvāṇa with the fruit of non-returning, knows that there are no sensual pleasures again, for him the mind is truly and completely escaped.

The same method of explanation applies to the other clauses too.

But this is the difference: in the second instance, the mettājhānas are said to be the escape from ill-will.

In the third instance, the karuṇājhānas are said to be the escape from harmfulness.

In the fourth instance, the arūpajjhānas are said to be the escape from forms.

In this complete escape, the fruit of arahatship should be connected.

In the fifth instance, sakkāyaṃ manasikaroto means to one who, having grasped pure conditioned things, having attained arahatship, having arisen from the fruition attainment, sends his mind towards the five aggregates of clinging for the purpose of investigation.

Idamakkhātaṃ sakkāyanissaraṇanti this fruition attainment mind of arahatship, which arises in a bhikkhu who, having seen nirvāṇa by the path and fruit of arahatship, [and knowing that] there is no self-identity again, is said to be the escape from self-identity.

Sakkāyaṃ manasikaroto idamakkhātaṃ sakkāyanissaraṇa

Explanation of the five abodes of liberation.
322
322. ‘‘Pañca vimuttāyatanāni. Idhāvuso, bhikkhuno satthā dhammaṃ deseti aññataro vā garuṭṭhāniyo sabrahmacārī. Yathā yathā, āvuso, bhikkhuno satthā dhammaṃ deseti aññataro vā garuṭṭhāniyo sabrahmacārī . Tathā tathā so tasmiṃ dhamme atthapaṭisaṃvedī ca hoti dhammapaṭisaṃvedī ca. Tassa atthapaṭisaṃvedino dhammapaṭisaṃvedino pāmojjaṃ jāyati, pamuditassa pīti jāyati, pītimanassa kāyo passambhati, passaddhakāyo sukhaṃ vedeti, sukhino cittaṃ samādhiyati. Idaṃ paṭhamaṃ vimuttāyatanaṃ. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhuno na heva kho satthā dhammaṃ deseti aññataro vā garuṭṭhāniyo sabrahmacārī, api ca kho yathāsutaṃ yathāpariyattaṃ dhammaṃ vitthārena paresaṃ deseti…pe… api ca kho yathāsutaṃ yathāpariyattaṃ dhammaṃ vitthārena sajjhāyaṃ karoti…pe… api ca kho yathāsutaṃ yathāpariyattaṃ dhammaṃ cetasā anuvitakketi anuvicāreti manasānupekkhati…pe… api ca khvassa aññataraṃ samādhinimittaṃ suggahitaṃ hoti sumanasikataṃ sūpadhāritaṃ suppaṭividdhaṃ paññāya. Yathā yathā, āvuso, bhikkhuno aññataraṃ samādhinimittaṃ suggahitaṃ hoti sumanasikataṃ sūpadhāritaṃ suppaṭividdhaṃ paññāya tathā tathā so tasmiṃ dhamme atthapaṭisaṃvedī ca hoti dhammapaṭisaṃvedī ca. Tassa atthapaṭisaṃvedino dhammapaṭisaṃvedino pāmojjaṃ jāyati, pamuditassa pīti jāyati, pītimanassa kāyo passambhati, passaddhakāyo sukhaṃ vedeti , sukhino cittaṃ samādhiyati. Idaṃ pañcamaṃ vimuttāyatanaṃ. ‘‘Pañca vimuttiparipācanīyā saññā – aniccasaññā, anicce dukkhasaññā, dukkhe anattasaññā, pahānasaññā, virāgasaññā. vimuttiparipācanīyā saññā – ‘‘Ime kho, āvuso, tena bhagavatā jānatā passatā arahatā sammāsambuddhena pañca dhammā sammadakkhātā; tattha sabbeheva saṅgāyitabbaṃ…pe… atthāya hitāya sukhāya devamanussānaṃ saṅgitiyapañcakaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ (syā. kaṃ.). saṅgitiyapañcakaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ (syā. kaṃ.) Chakkaṃ
322. "Five openings to release (pañca vimuttāyatanāni). Here, friends, the Teacher or a fellow person of venerable status in the holy life teaches the Dhamma to a monk. And in whatever way the Teacher or a fellow person of venerable status in the holy life teaches the Dhamma to the monk, in just that way the monk, with regard to that Dhamma, is sensitive to the meaning (attha), is sensitive to the Dhamma (dhamma). In him—sensitive to the meaning, sensitive to the Dhamma—joy (pāmojjaṃ) is born. When he is joyful (pamuditassa), rapture (pīti) is born. In one who is enraptured at heart (pītimanassa), the body grows calm (passambhati). When the body is calm (passaddhakāyo), one feels pleasure (sukhaṃ vedeti). Feeling pleasure (sukhino), the mind becomes concentrated (samādhiyati). This is the first opening to release.

"And further, friends, it’s not that the Teacher or a fellow person of venerable status in the holy life teaches the Dhamma to the monk; but the monk teaches the Dhamma in detail to others, as he has heard it, as he has learned it...or he recites the Dhamma in detail, as he has heard it, as he has learned it...or he, in his awareness, directs his thoughts to the Dhamma in detail, as he has heard it, as he has learned it, evaluates it, & mentally examines it...or he has well grasped a certain theme of concentration (samādhinimittaṃ), has attended to it well (sumanasikataṃ), sustained it well (sūpadhāritaṃ), has penetrated it well with discernment (suppaṭividdhaṃ paññāya). In whatever way, friends, the monk has well grasped a certain theme of concentration, has attended to it well, sustained it well, has penetrated it well with discernment, in just that way the monk, with regard to that Dhamma, is sensitive to the meaning, is sensitive to the Dhamma. In him—sensitive to the meaning, sensitive to the Dhamma—joy is born. When he is joyful, rapture is born. In one who is enraptured at heart, the body grows calm. When the body is calm, one feels pleasure, feeling pleasure, the mind becomes concentrated. This is the fifth opening to release.

"Five perceptions that ripen liberation (vimuttiparipācanīyā saññā) – the perception of impermanence (aniccasaññā), the perception of suffering in impermanence (anicce dukkhasaññā), the perception of not-self in suffering (dukkhe anattasaññā), the perception of abandonment (pahānasaññā), the perception of dispassion (virāgasaññā).

"These five things, friends, have been rightly explained by that Blessed One—knowing, seeing, worthy, rightly self-awakened; there should be a complete recitation by all there…for the benefit, welfare and happiness of gods and humans.

The Sangiti Five is Finished.

The Sixes
322.Vimuttāyatanānīti vimuccanakāraṇāni. Atthapaṭisaṃvedinoti pāḷiatthaṃ jānantassa. Dhammapaṭisaṃvedinoti pāḷiṃ jānantassa. Pāmojjanti taruṇapīti. Pītīti tuṭṭhākārabhūtā balavapīti. Kāyoti nāmakāyo paṭipassambhati. Sukhaṃ vedayatīti sukhaṃ paṭilabhati. Cittaṃ samādhiyatīti arahattaphalasamādhinā samādhiyati. Ayañhi taṃ dhammaṃ suṇanto āgatāgataṭṭhāne jhānavipassanāmaggaphalāni jānāti, tassa evaṃ jānato pīti uppajjati. So tassā pītiyā antarā osakkituṃ na dento upacārakammaṭṭhāniko hutvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. Taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ – ‘‘cittaṃ samādhiyatī’’ti. Sesesupi eseva nayo . Ayaṃ pana viseso, samādhinimittanti aṭṭhatiṃsāya ārammaṇesu aññataro samādhiyeva samādhinimittaṃ. Suggahitaṃ hotītiādīsu ācariyasantike kammaṭṭhānaṃ uggaṇhantena suṭṭhu gahitaṃ hoti. Suṭṭhu manasikatanti suṭṭhu upadhāritaṃ. Suppaṭividdhaṃ paññāyāti paññāya suṭṭhu paccakkhaṃ kataṃ. Tasmiṃ dhammeti tasmiṃ kammaṭṭhānapāḷidhamme. Vimuttiparipācanīyāti vimutti vuccati arahattaṃ, taṃ paripācentīti vimuttiparipācanīyā. Aniccasaññāti aniccānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā. Anicce dukkhasaññāti dukkhānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā. Dukkhe anattasaññāti anattānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā. Pahānasaññāti pahānānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā. Virāgasaññāti virāgānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā. Vimuttiparipācanīyā vimuttiparipācanīyā. Aniccasaññā Anicce dukkhasaññā Dukkhe anattasaññā Pahānasaññā Virāgasaññā ‘‘Ime kho āvuso’’tiādi vuttanayeneva yojetabbaṃ. Iti chabbīsatiyā pañcakānaṃ vasena tiṃsasatapañhe kathento thero sāmaggirasaṃ dassesīti. Pañcakavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Chakkavaṇṇanā
322. Vimuttāyatanānī means the causes for liberation. Atthapaṭisaṃvedino means for one who knows the meaning of the text. Dhammapaṭisaṃvedino means for one who knows the text. Pāmojja means fresh joy. Pītī means strong joy that is the nature of satisfaction. Kāyo means the mental body is calmed. Sukhaṃ vedayatī means obtains happiness. Cittaṃ samādhiyatī means the mind is concentrated with the concentration of the fruit of arahantship.

For, while listening to that Dharma, he knows the jhānas, insight, path, and fruits in the places where he arrives, and joy arises in him as he knows thus. Not allowing anything to interrupt that joy, he becomes one who has taken a meditation subject for access (upacārakammaṭṭhāniko), develops insight, and attains arahantship. With reference to that, it was said, "The mind is concentrated." The same method applies in the remaining [cases] as well.

But this is the distinction: samādhinimitta means concentration itself is the sign of concentration, [which is] one of the thirty-eight objects.

In suggahitaṃ hotī etc., [it means] it is well grasped by one who is learning a meditation subject from a teacher. Suṭṭhu manasikata means well attended to. Suppaṭividdhaṃ paññāyā means well understood by wisdom. Tasmiṃ dhamme means in that meditation subject's text (kammaṭṭhānapāḷidhamme).

Vimuttiparipācanīyā means liberation is called arahantship, that which ripens that is vimuttiparipācanīyā. Aniccasaññā means the perception that has arisen in the knowledge of contemplating impermanence (aniccānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā). Anicce dukkhasaññā means the perception that has arisen in the knowledge of contemplating suffering (dukkhānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā). Dukkhe anattasaññā means the perception that has arisen in the knowledge of contemplating not-self (anattānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā). Pahānasaññā means the perception that has arisen in the knowledge of contemplating abandonment (pahānānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā). Virāgasaññā means the perception that has arisen in the knowledge of contemplating dispassion (virāgānupassanāñāṇe uppannasaññā).

Vimuttiparipācanīyā aniccasaññā anicce dukkhasaññā dukkhe anattasaññā pahānasaññā virāgasaññā “These, friend” etc., should be construed in the same way as stated above.

Thus, in explaining the thirty-five questions by way of the five sets, the elder showed the flavor of harmony.

The Pañcakavaṇṇanā is finished.

Chakkavaṇṇanā
323
323. ‘‘Atthi kho, āvuso, tena bhagavatā jānatā passatā arahatā sammāsambuddhena cha dhammā sammadakkhātā; tattha sabbeheva saṅgāyitabbaṃ…pe… atthāya hitāya sukhāya devamanussānaṃ. Katame cha? ‘‘Cha ajjhattikāniāyatanāni – cakkhāyatanaṃ, sotāyatanaṃ, ghānāyatanaṃ, jivhāyatanaṃ, kāyāyatanaṃ, manāyatanaṃ. ajjhattikāni āyatanāni – ‘‘Cha bāhirāni āyatanāni – rūpāyatanaṃ, saddāyatanaṃ, gandhāyatanaṃ, rasāyatanaṃ, phoṭṭhabbāyatanaṃ, dhammāyatanaṃ. bāhirāni āyatanāni – ‘‘Cha viññāṇakāyā – cakkhuviññāṇaṃ, sotaviññāṇaṃ, ghānaviññāṇaṃ, jivhāviññāṇaṃ, kāyaviññāṇaṃ, manoviññāṇaṃ. viññāṇakāyā – ‘‘Cha phassakāyā – cakkhusamphasso, sotasamphasso, ghānasamphasso, jivhāsamphasso, kāyasamphasso, manosamphasso. phassakāyā – ‘‘Cha vedanākāyā – cakkhusamphassajā vedanā, sotasamphassajā vedanā, ghānasamphassajā vedanā, jivhāsamphassajā vedanā, kāyasamphassajā vedanā, manosamphassajā vedanā. vedanākāyā – ‘‘Cha saññākāyā – rūpasaññā, saddasaññā, gandhasaññā, rasasaññā, phoṭṭhabbasaññā, dhammasaññā. saññākāyā – ‘‘Cha sañcetanākāyā – rūpasañcetanā, saddasañcetanā, gandhasañcetanā, rasasañcetanā, phoṭṭhabbasañcetanā, dhammasañcetanā. sañcetanākāyā – ‘‘Cha taṇhākāyā – rūpataṇhā, saddataṇhā, gandhataṇhā, rasataṇhā, phoṭṭhabbataṇhā, dhammataṇhā. taṇhākāyā –
“There are, friends, six dhammas that are rightly expounded by the Blessed One—the one who knows, the one who sees, the Worthy One (arhat), rightly self-awakened (sammāsambuddha); those should all be recited together… for the benefit & happiness of devas & human beings.

What are the six?

“Six internal sense spheres (ajjhattikāni āyatanāni): the eye sphere (cakkhāyatanaṃ), the ear sphere (sotāyatanaṃ), the nose sphere (ghānāyatanaṃ), the tongue sphere (jivhāyatanaṃ), the body sphere (kāyāyatanaṃ), and the mind sphere (manāyatanaṃ).

Six external sense spheres (bāhirāni āyatanāni): the form sphere (rūpāyatanaṃ), the sound sphere (saddāyatanaṃ), the smell sphere (gandhāyatanaṃ), the taste sphere (rasāyatanaṃ), the touch sphere (phoṭṭhabbāyatanaṃ), and the phenomena sphere (dhammāyatanaṃ).

Six aggregates of consciousness (viññāṇakāyā): eye-consciousness (cakkhuviññāṇaṃ), ear-consciousness (sotaviññāṇaṃ), nose-consciousness (ghānaviññāṇaṃ), tongue-consciousness (jivhāviññāṇaṃ), body-consciousness (kāyaviññāṇaṃ), and mind-consciousness (manoviññāṇaṃ).

Six aggregates of contact (phassakāyā): eye-contact (cakkhusamphasso), ear-contact (sotasamphasso), nose-contact (ghānasamphasso), tongue-contact (jivhāsamphasso), body-contact (kāyasamphasso), and mind-contact (manosamphasso).

Six aggregates of feeling (vedanākāyā): feeling born of eye-contact (cakkhusamphassajā vedanā), feeling born of ear-contact (sotasamphassajā vedanā), feeling born of nose-contact (ghānasamphassajā vedanā), feeling born of tongue-contact (jivhāsamphassajā vedanā), feeling born of body-contact (kāyasamphassajā vedanā), and feeling born of mind-contact (manosamphassajā vedanā).

Six aggregates of perception (saññākāyā): perception of form (rūpasaññā), perception of sound (saddasaññā), perception of smell (gandhasaññā), perception of taste (rasasaññā), perception of touch (phoṭṭhabbasaññā), and perception of phenomena (dhammasaññā).

Six aggregates of volition (sañcetanākāyā): volition regarding form (rūpasañcetanā), volition regarding sound (saddasañcetanā), volition regarding smell (gandhasañcetanā), volition regarding taste (rasasañcetanā), volition regarding touch (phoṭṭhabbasañcetanā), and volition regarding phenomena (dhammasañcetanā).

Six aggregates of craving (taṇhākāyā): craving for form (rūpataṇhā), craving for sound (saddataṇhā), craving for smell (gandhataṇhā), craving for taste (rasataṇhā), craving for touch (phoṭṭhabbataṇhā), and craving for phenomena (dhammataṇhā).
323. Iti pañcakavasena sāmaggirasaṃ dassetvā idāni chakkavasena dassetuṃ puna desanaṃ ārabhi. Tattha ajjhattikānīti ajjhattajjhattikāni. Bāhirānīti tato ajjhattajjhattato bahibhūtāni. Vitthārato pana āyatanakathā visuddhimagge kathitāva. Viññāṇakāyāti viññāṇasamūhā. Cakkhuviññāṇanti cakkhupasādanissitaṃ kusalākusalavipākaviññāṇaṃ. Esa nayo sabbattha. Cakkhusamphassoti cakkhunissito samphasso. Sotasamphassādīsupi eseva nayo. Manosamphassoti ime dasa samphasse ṭhapetvā seso sabbo manosamphasso nāma. Vedanāchakkampi eteneva nayena veditabbaṃ. Rūpasaññāti rūpaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā uppannā saññā. Etenupāyena sesāpi veditabbā. Cetanāchakkepi eseva nayo. Tathā taṇhāchakke. Agāravoti gāravavirahito. Appatissoti appatissayo anīcavutti. Ettha pana yo bhikkhu satthari dharamāne tīsu kālesu upaṭṭhānaṃ na yāti. Satthari anupāhane caṅkamante saupāhano caṅkamati, nīce caṅkamante ucce caṅkamati, heṭṭhā vasante upari vasati, satthudassanaṭṭhāne ubho aṃse pārupati, chattaṃ dhāreti, upāhanaṃ dhāreti, nahāyati, uccāraṃ vā passāvaṃ vā karoti. Parinibbute pana cetiyaṃ vandituṃ na gacchati, cetiyassa paññāyanaṭṭhāne satthudassanaṭṭhāne vuttaṃ sabbaṃ karoti, ayaṃ satthari agāravo nāma. Yo pana dhammassavane saṃghuṭṭhe sakkaccaṃ na gacchati, sakkaccaṃ dhammaṃ na suṇāti, samullapanto nisīdati, sakkaccaṃ na gaṇhāti, na vāceti, ayaṃ dhamme agāravo nāma. Yo pana therena bhikkhunā anajjhiṭṭho dhammaṃ deseti, nisīdati, pañhaṃ katheti, vuḍḍhe bhikkhū ghaṭṭento gacchati, tiṭṭhati, nisīdati, dussapallatthikaṃ vā hatthapallatthikaṃ vā karoti, saṅghamajjhe ubho aṃse pārupati, chattupāhanaṃ dhāreti, ayaṃ saṅghe agāravo nāma. Ekabhikkhusmimpi hi agārave kate saṅghe agāravo katova hoti. Tisso sikkhā pana apūrayamānova sikkhāya agāravo nāma. Appamādalakkhaṇaṃ ananubrūhayamāno appamāde agāravo nāma. Duvidhampi paṭisanthāraṃ akaronto paṭisanthāre agāravo nāma. Cha gāravā vuttappaṭipakkhavasena veditabbā. Agāravo Appatisso satthari agāravo dhamme agāravo saṅghe agāravo sikkhāya agāravo appamāde agāravo paṭisanthāraṃ paṭisanthāre agāravo Somanassūpavicārāti somanassasampayuttā vicārā. Somanassaṭṭhāniyanti somanassakāraṇabhūtaṃ. Upavicaratīti vitakkena vitakketvā vicārena paricchindati. Esa nayo sabbattha. Domanassūpavicārāpi evameva veditabbā. Tathā upekkhūpavicārā. Sāraṇīyadhammā heṭṭhā vitthāritā. Diṭṭhisāmaññagatoti iminā pana padena kosambakasutte paṭhamamaggo kathito. Idha cattāropi maggā. Somanassūpavicārā Somanassaṭṭhāniya Upavicaratī Domanassūpavicārā upekkhūpavicārā. Sāraṇīyadhammā Diṭṭhisāmaññagato Vivādamūlachakkavaṇṇanā
323. Having shown the complete meaning by way of the five aggregates, now, in order to show [it] by way of the six aggregates, [the text] again begins the teaching.

There, ajjhattikānī means internal-internal.

bāhirānī means external, that is, external to the internal-internal.

However, the exposition of the āyatana is already stated in detail in the Visuddhimagga (Path of Purification).

viññāṇakāyā means aggregates of consciousness.

cakkhuviññāṇa means consciousness—wholesome, unwholesome, or the result [of karma]—that is dependent on the sensitive part of the eye.

This method applies to all [the aggregates].

cakkhusamphasso means contact dependent on the eye.

The same method applies also to sotasamphassā, and so forth.

manosamphasso means that, having set aside these ten contacts, all the remaining [contacts] are called manosamphasso.

The group of six feelings should be understood in the same way by this method.

rūpasaññā means perception that has arisen having made form an object.

The remaining [perceptions] should be understood by this method.

The same method applies also to the group of six volitions.

Likewise, to the group of six cravings.

agāravo means devoid of respect.

appatisso means without dependence, unsteady behavior.

Here, a bhikkhu who, while the Teacher is living, does not go to serve [him] at the three times [of day];

when the Teacher is walking without shoes, he walks with shoes; when [the Teacher] is walking on low ground, he walks on high ground; when [the Teacher] is dwelling below, he dwells above; at the place of seeing the Teacher, he covers both shoulders, holds an umbrella, wears shoes, bathes, or excretes urine or feces.

However, when [the Teacher] has passed away, he does not go to venerate the cetiya; at the place of making offerings to the cetiya, at the place of seeing the Teacher, he does everything that has been said [above]; this is called disrespect toward the Teacher (satthari agāravo).

A bhikkhu who, when the announcement of the Dharma is made, does not go respectfully to hear the Dharma, does not respectfully listen to the Dharma, sits down talking, does not respectfully take [it] up, nor recite [it], is called disrespect toward the Dharma (dhamme agāravo).

A bhikkhu who, without being requested by an elder bhikkhu, teaches the Dharma, sits down, tells a story, goes jostling elderly bhikkhus, stands, sits, makes a robe-support or a hand-support, in the midst of the saṅgha covers both shoulders, holds an umbrella and shoes, is called disrespect toward the saṅgha (saṅghe agāravo).

For even if disrespect is done to one bhikkhu, disrespect is done to the saṅgha.

Only if he does not fulfill the three trainings is he called disrespect toward the training (sikkhāya agāravo).

If he does not cultivate the characteristic of diligence, he is called disrespect toward diligence (appamāde agāravo).

If he does not do even the two kinds of cordiality, he is called disrespect toward cordiality (paṭisanthāre agāravo).

The six kinds of disrespect should be understood by way of the opposites that have been stated.

agāravo, appatisso, disrespect toward the Teacher (satthari agāravo), disrespect toward the Dharma (dhamme agāravo), disrespect toward the saṅgha (saṅghe agāravo), disrespect toward the training (sikkhāya agāravo), disrespect toward diligence (appamāde agāravo), cordiality (paṭisanthāraṃ), disrespect toward cordiality (paṭisanthāre agāravo).

somanassūpavicārā means investigations associated with joy.

somanassaṭṭhāniya means the cause of joy.

upavicaratī means, having thought with thought, he discriminates with investigation.

This method applies to all [the investigations].

domanassūpavicārā should also be understood in just the same way.

Likewise, upekkhūpavicārā.

The sāraṇīyadhammā have been explained in detail below.
"Being endowed with agreement in views ( diṭṭhisāmaññagato)"—with this word, the first path is spoken of in the Kosambaka Sutta. Here, however, are all four paths.

"Investigating with joy ( somanassūpavicārā), investigating in a state of joy (somanassaṭṭhāniya upavicaratī), investigating with displeasure (domanassūpavicārā), investigating with equanimity (upekkhūpavicārā). sāraṇīyadhammā diṭṭhisāmaññagato."

Explanation of the six roots of quarrel ( vivādamūlachakkavaṇṇanā).
324
324. ‘‘Cha agāravā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu satthari agāravo viharati appatisso; dhamme agāravo viharati appatisso; saṅghe agāravo viharati appatisso; sikkhāya agāravo viharati appatisso; appamāde agāravo viharati appatisso; paṭisanthāre paṭisandhāre (ka.) agāravo viharati appatisso. ‘‘Cha gāravā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu satthari sagāravo viharati sappatisso; dhamme sagāravo viharati sappatisso; saṅghe sagāravo viharati sappatisso; sikkhāya sagāravo viharati sappatisso; appamāde sagāravo viharati sappatisso; paṭisanthāre sagāravo viharati sappatisso. gāravā. ‘‘Cha somanassūpavicārā. Cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā somanassaṭṭhāniyaṃ rūpaṃ upavicarati; sotena saddaṃ sutvā… ghānena gandhaṃ ghāyitvā… jivhāya rasaṃ sāyitvā… kāyena phoṭṭhabbaṃ phusitvā. Manasā dhammaṃ viññāya somanassaṭṭhāniyaṃ dhammaṃ upavicarati. somanassūpavicārā ‘‘Cha domanassūpavicārā. Cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā domanassaṭṭhāniyaṃ rūpaṃ upavicarati…pe… manasā dhammaṃ viññāya domanassaṭṭhāniyaṃ dhammaṃ upavicarati. domanassūpavicārā. ‘‘Cha upekkhūpavicārā. Cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā upekkhāṭṭhāniyaṃ upekkhāṭhāniyaṃ (ka.) rūpaṃ upavicarati…pe… manasā dhammaṃ viññāya upekkhāṭṭhāniyaṃ dhammaṃ upavicarati. upekkhūpavicārā. upekkhāṭhāniyaṃ (ka.) ‘‘Cha sāraṇīyā dhammā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhuno mettaṃ kāyakammaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hoti sabrahmacārīsu āvi āvī (ka. sī. pī. ka.) ceva raho ca. Ayampi dhammo sāraṇīyo piyakaraṇo garukaraṇo saṅgahāya avivādāya sāmaggiyā ekībhāvāya saṃvattati. sāraṇīyā dhammā. āvī (ka. sī. pī. ka.) ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhuno mettaṃ vacīkammaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hoti sabrahmacārīsu āvi ceva raho ca. Ayampi dhammo sāraṇīyo…pe… ekībhāvāya saṃvattati. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhuno mettaṃ manokammaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hoti sabrahmacārīsu āvi ceva raho ca. Ayampi dhammo sāraṇīyo…pe… ekībhāvāya saṃvattati. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu ye te lābhā dhammikā dhammaladdhā antamaso pattapariyāpannamattampi, tathārūpehi lābhehi appaṭivibhattabhogī hoti sīlavantehi sabrahmacārīhi sādhāraṇabhogī. Ayampi dhammo sāraṇīyo…pe… ekībhāvāya saṃvattati. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu yāni tāni sīlāni akhaṇḍāni acchiddāni asabalāni akammāsāni bhujissāni viññuppasatthāni aparāmaṭṭhāni samādhisaṃvattanikāni, tathārūpesu sīlesu sīlasāmaññagato viharati sabrahmacārīhi āvi ceva raho ca. Ayampi dhammo sāraṇīyo…pe… ekībhāvāya saṃvattati. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu yāyaṃ diṭṭhi ariyā niyyānikā niyyāti takkarassa sammā dukkhakkhayāya, tathārūpāya diṭṭhiyā diṭṭhisāmaññagato viharati sabrahmacārīhi āvi ceva raho ca. Ayampi dhammo sāraṇīyo piyakaraṇo garukaraṇo saṅgahāya avivādāya sāmaggiyā ekībhāvāya saṃvattati.
324. "Six kinds of lack of respect. Here, friends, a monk dwells without respect or deference for the Teacher ( satthari agāravo viharati appatisso), for the Dhamma, for the Saṅgha, for the training ( sikkhāya agāravo viharati appatisso), for heedfulness ( appamāde agāravo viharati appatisso), for welcoming manners (paṭisanthāre agāravo viharati appatisso).

"Six kinds of respect. Here, friends, a monk dwells with respect and deference for the Teacher ( satthari sagāravo viharati sappatisso), for the Dhamma, for the Saṅgha, for the training, for heedfulness, for welcoming manners.

"Six kinds of explorations productive of joy ( somanassūpavicārā). Having seen a form with the eye, one explores a form productive of joy ( cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā somanassaṭṭhāniyaṃ rūpaṃ upavicarati); on hearing a sound with the ear… on smelling an odor with the nose… on tasting a flavor with the tongue… on touching a tangible with the body. On cognizing an idea via the intellect, one explores an idea that can act as the basis for happiness ( manasā dhammaṃ viññāya somanassaṭṭhāniyaṃ dhammaṃ upavicarati).

"Six kinds of explorations productive of grief (domanassūpavicārā). Having seen a form with the eye, one explores a form productive of grief ( cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā domanassaṭṭhāniyaṃ rūpaṃ upavicarati)… On cognizing an idea via the intellect, one explores an idea that can act as the basis for distress ( manasā dhammaṃ viññāya domanassaṭṭhāniyaṃ dhammaṃ upavicarati).

"Six kinds of explorations productive of equanimity (upekkhūpavicārā). Having seen a form with the eye, one explores a form productive of equanimity (cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā upekkhāṭṭhāniyaṃ rūpaṃ upavicarati)… On cognizing an idea via the intellect, one explores an idea that can act as the basis for equanimity ( manasā dhammaṃ viññāya upekkhāṭṭhāniyaṃ dhammaṃ upavicarati).

"Six memorable qualities ( sāraṇīyā dhammā). Here, friends, a monk maintains bodily acts of loving-kindness both in public and in private towards his companions in the holy life ( mettāṃ kāyakammaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hoti sabrahmacārīsu āvi ceva raho ca). This is a memorable quality that creates love and respect, and conduces to helpfulness, to non-dispute, to concord, and to unity ( ayampi dhammo sāraṇīyo piyakaraṇo garukaraṇo saṅgahāya avivādāya sāmaggiyā ekībhāvāya saṃvattati).

"Furthermore, friends, a monk maintains verbal acts of loving-kindness both in public and in private towards his companions in the holy life ( mettāṃ vacīkammaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hoti sabrahmacārīsu āvi ceva raho ca). This too is a memorable quality…and conduces to unity.
“And further, the monk is set on mental acts of goodwill (mettā manokamma) with regard to his companions in the holy life (sabrahmacārī), to their faces and behind their backs. This too is a condition that is conducive to amiability…leading to a state of unity (ekībhāvāya saṃvattati).

“And further, whatever righteous gains the monk may obtain in a righteous way—even if only the alms in his bowl—he does not consume them alone. He consumes them after sharing them in common with his virtuous companions in the holy life (sīlavantehi sabrahmacārīhi sādhāraṇabhogī). This too is a condition that is conducive to amiability…leading to a state of unity (ekībhāvāya saṃvattati).

“And further—with reference to the virtues (sīlāni) that are untorn, unbroken, unspotted, unsplattered, liberating, praised by the observant, ungrasped at, leading to concentration—the monk dwells with his virtue (sīla) in tune with that of his companions in the holy life, to their faces and behind their backs. This, too, is a condition that is conducive to amiability…leading to a state of unity (ekībhāvāya saṃvattati).

“And further, the monk dwells possessing in common with his companions in the holy life (sabrahmacārī), that noble (ariyā) view (diṭṭhi) which leads outwards, which leads the one who acts accordingly to the ending of suffering (dukkhakkhayāya); he dwells possessing a commonality of view. This is a memorable quality that creates love and respect, and conduces to helpfulness, to non-dispute, to concord, and to unity (ekībhāvāya saṃvattati).
325
325. Cha vivādamūlāni. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu kodhano hoti upanāhī. Yo so, āvuso, bhikkhu kodhano hoti upanāhī, so sattharipi agāravo viharati appatisso, dhammepi agāravo viharati appatisso, saṅghepi agāravo viharati appatisso, sikkhāyapi na paripūrakārī paripūrīkārī (syā. kaṃ.) hoti. Yo so, āvuso, bhikkhu satthari agāravo viharati appatisso, dhamme agāravo viharati appatisso, saṅghe agāravo viharati appatisso, sikkhāya na paripūrakārī, so saṅghe vivādaṃ janeti. Yo hoti vivādo bahujanaahitāya bahujanaasukhāya anatthāya ahitāya dukkhāya devamanussānaṃ. Evarūpaṃ ce tumhe, āvuso, vivādamūlaṃ ajjhattaṃ vā bahiddhā vā samanupasseyyātha. Tatra tumhe, āvuso, tasseva pāpakassa vivādamūlassa pahānāya vāyameyyātha. Evarūpaṃ ce tumhe, āvuso, vivādamūlaṃ ajjhattaṃ vā bahiddhā vā na samanupasseyyātha. Tatra tumhe, āvuso, tasseva pāpakassa vivādamūlassa āyatiṃ anavassavāya paṭipajjeyyātha. Evametassa pāpakassa vivādamūlassa pahānaṃ hoti. Evametassa pāpakassa vivādamūlassa āyatiṃ anavassavo hoti. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu makkhī hoti paḷāsī…pe… issukī hoti maccharī…pe… saṭho hoti māyāvī… pāpiccho hoti micchādiṭṭhī… sandiṭṭhiparāmāsī hoti ādhānaggāhī duppaṭinissaggī…pe… yo so, āvuso, bhikkhu sandiṭṭhiparāmāsī hoti ādhānaggāhī duppaṭinissaggī, so sattharipi agāravo viharati appatisso, dhammepi agāravo viharati appatisso, saṅghepi agāravo viharati appatisso, sikkhāyapi na paripūrakārī hoti. Yo so, āvuso, bhikkhu satthari agāravo viharati appatisso, dhamme agāravo viharati appatisso, saṅghe agāravo viharati appatisso , sikkhāya na paripūrakārī, so saṅghe vivādaṃ janeti. Yo hoti vivādo bahujanaahitāya bahujanaasukhāya anatthāya ahitāya dukkhāya devamanussānaṃ. Evarūpaṃ ce tumhe, āvuso, vivādamūlaṃ ajjhattaṃ vā bahiddhā vā samanupasseyyātha. Tatra tumhe, āvuso, tasseva pāpakassa vivādamūlassa pahānāya vāyameyyātha. Evarūpaṃ ce tumhe, āvuso, vivādamūlaṃ ajjhattaṃ vā bahiddhā vā na samanupasseyyātha. Tatra tumhe, āvuso, tasseva pāpakassa vivādamūlassa āyatiṃ anavassavāya paṭipajjeyyātha. Evametassa pāpakassa vivādamūlassa pahānaṃ hoti. Evametassa pāpakassa vivādamūlassa āyatiṃ anavassavo hoti. ‘‘Cha dhātuyo – pathavīdhātu, āpodhātu, tejodhātu, vāyodhātu, ākāsadhātu, viññāṇadhātu. dhātuyo
325. Six roots of dispute.

Here, friends, a bhikkhu is angry and spiteful.

When a monk is angry and resentful, he dwells without respect or deference for the Teacher ( satthari ), for the Dhamma ( dhamma ), for the Saṅgha ( saṅgha ), and he does not fulfill the training.

When a bhikkhu dwells without respect or deference for the Teacher, for the Dhamma, for the Saṅgha, and does not fulfill the training, he creates a dispute ( vivādaṃ janeti ) in the Saṅgha ( saṅghe ).

That dispute is for the harm and unhappiness of many, for the loss, harm, and suffering of gods and humans ( devamanussānaṃ ).

If you see this sort of root of dispute within you or without, friends,

you should make an effort for the abandoning ( pahānāya vāyameyyātha ) of that evil root of dispute ( pāpakassa vivādamūlassa ).

If you don’t see this sort of root of dispute within you or without,

you should practice for the future non-arising of that evil root of dispute.

In this way that bad root of disputes is abandoned.

In this way that bad source of disputes has no future effect.

“And further, friends, a bhikkhu is dismissive and antagonistic… envious and selfish… deceitful and pretentious… has evil desires and wrong views… is opinionated, tenacious, and obstinate… When a bhikkhu is opinionated, tenacious, and obstinate, he dwells without respect or deference for the Teacher, for the Dhamma, for the Saṅgha, and does not fulfill the training.

When a bhikkhu dwells without respect or deference for the Teacher, for the Dhamma, for the Saṅgha, he creates a dispute in the Saṅgha.

That dispute is for the harm and unhappiness of many, for the loss, harm, and suffering of gods and humans.

If you see this sort of root of dispute within you or without, friends,

you should make an effort for the abandoning of that evil root of dispute.

If you don’t see this sort of root of dispute within you or without,

you should practice for the future non-arising of that evil root of dispute.

In this way that bad root of disputes is abandoned.

In this way that bad source of disputes has no future effect.

Six elements: earth element ( pathavīdhātu ), water element ( āpodhātu ), fire element ( tejodhātu ), air element ( vāyodhātu ), space element ( ākāsadhātu ), and consciousness element ( viññāṇadhātu ).

Elements
325.Vivādamūlānīti vivādassa mūlāni. Kodhanoti kujjhanalakkhaṇena kodhena samannāgato. Upanāhīti veraappaṭinissaggalakkhaṇena upanāhena samannāgato. Ahitāya dukkhāya devamanussānanti dvinnaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ vivādo kathaṃ devamanussānaṃ ahitāya dukkhāya saṃvattati. Kosambakakkhandhake viya dvīsu bhikkhūsu vivādaṃ āpannesu tasmiṃ vihāre tesaṃ antevāsikā vivadanti. Tesaṃ ovādaṃ gaṇhanto bhikkhunisaṅgho vivadati. Tato tesaṃ upaṭṭhākā vivadanti. Atha manussānaṃ ārakkhadevatā dve koṭṭhāsā honti. Tattha dhammavādīnaṃ ārakkhadevatā dhammavādiniyo honti adhammavādīnaṃ adhammavādiniyo. Tato ārakkhadevatānaṃ mittā bhummā devatā bhijjanti. Evaṃ paramparā yāva brahmalokā ṭhapetvā ariyasāvake sabbe devamanussā dve koṭṭhāsā honti. Dhammavādīhi pana adhammavādinova bahutarā honti. Tato ‘‘yaṃ bahukehi gahitaṃ, taṃ taccha’’nti dhammaṃ vissajjetvā bahutarāva adhammaṃ gaṇhanti. Te adhammaṃ purakkhatvā vadantā apāyesu nibbattanti. Evaṃ dvinnaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ vivādo devamanussānaṃ ahitāya dukkhāya hoti. Ajjhattaṃ vāti tumhākaṃ abbhantaraparisāya. Bahiddhā vāti paresaṃ parisāya. Ajjhattaṃ vā Bahiddhā vā Makkhīti paresaṃ guṇamakkhanalakkhaṇena makkhena samannāgato. Paḷāsīti yugaggāhalakkhaṇena paḷāsena samannāgato. Issukīti parasakkārādīni issāyanalakkhaṇāya issāya samannāgato. Maccharīti āvāsamacchariyādīhi samannāgato. Saṭhoti kerāṭiko. Māyāvīti katapāpapaṭicchādako. Pāpicchoti asantasambhāvanicchako dussīlo. Micchādiṭṭhīti natthikavādī ahetukavādī akiriyavādī. Sandiṭṭhiparāmāsīti sayaṃ diṭṭhimeva parāmasati. Ādhānaggāhīti daḷhaggāhī. Duppaṭinissaggīti na sakkā hoti gahitaṃ vissajjāpetuṃ. Makkhī Paḷāsī Issukī Maccharī Saṭho Māyāvī Pāpiccho Micchādiṭṭhī Sandiṭṭhiparāmāsī Ādhānaggāhī Duppaṭinissaggī Pathavīdhātūti patiṭṭhādhātu. Āpodhātūti ābandhanadhātu. Tejodhātūti paripācanadhātu. Vāyodhātūti vitthambhanadhātu. Ākāsadhātūti asamphuṭṭhadhātu. Viññāṇadhātūti vijānanadhātu. Pathavīdhātū Āpodhātū Tejodhātū Vāyodhātū Ākāsadhātū Viññāṇadhātū Nissaraṇiyachakkavaṇṇanā
325. Vivādamūlānī: the roots of quarrel.

Kodhano: one endowed with anger (kodha) characterized by irritability (kujjhanalakkhaṇena).

Upanāhī: one endowed with hatred (upanāhena) characterized by the non-abandonment of enmity (veraappaṭinissaggalakkhaṇena).

"For the disadvantage and suffering of gods and humans": how does the quarrel of two monks lead to the disadvantage and suffering of gods and humans?

When a quarrel arises between two monks, as in the Kosambaka Khandhaka, in that monastery, their residents quarrel.

The bhikkhunīsaṅgha (community of nuns) quarrels, taking their advice.

Then their attendants quarrel.

Then the guardian deities of humans are divided into two factions.

There, the guardian deities of those who advocate the Dharma are Dharma advocates, and the guardian deities of those who do not advocate the Dharma are non-Dharma advocates.

Then the friendly, earthly deities of the guardian deities are split.

Thus, in succession, as far as the Brahma world, except for the noble disciples, all gods and humans are divided into two factions.

However, those who do not advocate the Dharma are more numerous than those who advocate the Dharma.

Then, rejecting the Dharma, [thinking,] "That which is accepted by many is true," most people accept adhamma (non-dharma).

Those who uphold adhamma, speaking, are reborn in the apāyas (realms of misery).

Thus, the quarrel of two monks is for the disadvantage and suffering of gods and humans.

Ajjhattaṃ vā: in your inner circle.

Bahiddhā vā: in the circle of others.

Either internal or external.

Makkhī: one endowed with makkha, characterized by disparaging the virtues of others (paresaṃ guṇamakkhanalakkhaṇena).

Paḷāsī: one endowed with paḷāsa, characterized by seizing quarrels (yugaggāhalakkhaṇena).

Issukī: one endowed with envy (issāya) characterized by envy of the gains of others (parasakkārādīni issāyanalakkhaṇāya).

Maccharī: one endowed with stinginess (macchariya) regarding dwellings, etc. (āvāsamacchariyādīhi).

Saṭho: a cheat (kerāṭiko).

Māyāvī: one who conceals committed evils (katapāpapaṭicchādako).

Pāpiccho: one of evil desires, an immoral person who desires what is not (asantasambhāvanicchako dussīlo).

Micchādiṭṭhī: a nihilist (natthikavādī), a hetuvadin, an akiriyavādī.

Sandiṭṭhiparāmāsī: one who grasps only what he himself has seen (sayaṃ diṭṭhimeva parāmasati).

Ādhānaggāhī: one who grasps firmly (daḷhaggāhī).

Duppaṭinissaggī: it is not possible to give up what has been grasped (na sakkā hoti gahitaṃ vissajjāpetuṃ).

Makkhī, paḷāsī, issukī, maccharī, saṭho, māyāvī, pāpiccho, micchādiṭṭhī, sandiṭṭhiparāmāsī, ādhānaggāhī, duppaṭinissaggī.

Pathavīdhātu: the element of earth is the element of foundation (patiṭṭhādhātu).

Āpodhātu: the water element is the element of cohesion (ābandhanadhātu).

Tejodhātu: the fire element is the element of maturing (paripācanadhātu).

Vāyodhātu: the air element is the element of support (vitthambhanadhātu).

Ākāsadhātu: the space element is the element of non-obstruction (asamphuṭṭhadhātu).

Viññāṇadhātu: the consciousness element is the element of cognition (vijānanadhātu).

Pathavīdhātu, āpodhātu, tejodhātu, vāyodhātu, ākāsadhātu, viññāṇadhātu.

Explanation of the six-spoked chariot of deliverance (nissaraṇiyachakkavaṇṇanā).
326
326. ‘‘Cha nissaraṇiyā dhātuyo. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu evaṃ vadeyya – ‘mettā hi kho me cetovimutti bhāvitā bahulīkatā yānīkatā vatthukatā anuṭṭhitā paricitā susamāraddhā, atha ca pana me byāpādo cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhatī’ti. So ‘mā hevaṃ’, tissa vacanīyo, ‘māyasmā evaṃ avaca, mā bhagavantaṃ abbhācikkhi, na hi sādhu bhagavato abbhakkhānaṃ, na hi bhagavā evaṃ vadeyya. Aṭṭhānametaṃ, āvuso, anavakāso, yaṃ mettāya cetovimuttiyā bhāvitāya bahulīkatāya yānīkatāya vatthukatāya anuṭṭhitāya paricitāya susamāraddhāya. Atha ca panassa byāpādo cittaṃ pariyādāya ṭhassati, netaṃ ṭhānaṃ vijjati. Nissaraṇaṃ hetaṃ, āvuso, byāpādassa, yadidaṃ mettā cetovimuttī’ti. ‘‘Idha panāvuso, bhikkhu evaṃ vadeyya – ‘karuṇā hi kho me cetovimutti bhāvitā bahulīkatā yānīkatā vatthukatā anuṭṭhitā paricitā susamāraddhā. Atha ca pana me vihesā cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhatī’ti, so ‘mā hevaṃ’ tissa vacanīyo ‘māyasmā evaṃ avaca, mā bhagavantaṃ abbhācikkhi, na hi sādhu bhagavato abbhakkhānaṃ, na hi bhagavā evaṃ vadeyya. Aṭṭhānametaṃ āvuso, anavakāso, yaṃ karuṇāya cetovimuttiyā bhāvitāya bahulīkatāya yānīkatāya vatthukatāya anuṭṭhitāya paricitāya susamāraddhāya, atha ca panassa vihesā cittaṃ pariyādāya ṭhassati, netaṃ ṭhānaṃ vijjati. Nissaraṇaṃ hetaṃ, āvuso, vihesāya, yadidaṃ karuṇā cetovimuttī’ti. ‘‘Idha panāvuso, bhikkhu evaṃ vadeyya – ‘muditā hi kho me cetovimutti bhāvitā bahulīkatā yānīkatā vatthukatā anuṭṭhitā paricitā susamāraddhā. Atha ca pana me arati cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhatī’ti, so ‘mā hevaṃ’ tissa vacanīyo ‘‘māyasmā evaṃ avaca, mā bhagavantaṃ abbhācikkhi, na hi sādhu bhagavato abbhakkhānaṃ, na hi bhagavā evaṃ vadeyya. Aṭṭhānametaṃ, āvuso, anavakāso, yaṃ muditāya cetovimuttiyā bhāvitāya bahulīkatāya yānīkatāya vatthukatāya anuṭṭhitāya paricitāya susamāraddhāya, atha ca panassa arati cittaṃ pariyādāya ṭhassati, netaṃ ṭhānaṃ vijjati. Nissaraṇaṃ hetaṃ, āvuso, aratiyā, yadidaṃ muditā cetovimuttī’ti. ‘‘Idha panāvuso, bhikkhu evaṃ vadeyya – ‘upekkhā hi kho me cetovimutti bhāvitā bahulīkatā yānīkatā vatthukatā anuṭṭhitā paricitā susamāraddhā. Atha ca pana me rāgo cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhatī’ti. So ‘mā hevaṃ’ tissa vacanīyo ‘māyasmā evaṃ avaca, mā bhagavantaṃ abbhācikkhi, na hi sādhu bhagavato abbhakkhānaṃ, na hi bhagavā evaṃ vadeyya.
