342. ‘‘Nava akkhaṇā asamayā brahmacariyavāsāya. Idhāvuso , tathāgato ca loke uppanno hoti arahaṃ sammāsambuddho, dhammo ca desiyati opasamiko parinibbāniko sambodhagāmī sugatappavedito. Ayañca puggalo nirayaṃ upapanno hoti. Ayaṃ paṭhamo akkhaṇo asamayo brahmacariyavāsāya. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, tathāgato ca loke uppanno hoti arahaṃ sammāsambuddho, dhammo ca desiyati opasamiko parinibbāniko sambodhagāmī sugatappavedito. Ayañca puggalo tiracchānayoniṃ upapanno hoti. Ayaṃ dutiyo akkhaṇo asamayo brahmacariyavāsāya. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ…pe… pettivisayaṃ upapanno hoti. Ayaṃ tatiyo akkhaṇo asamayo brahmacariyavāsāya. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ…pe… asurakāyaṃ upapanno hoti. Ayaṃ catuttho akkhaṇo asamayo brahmacariyavāsāya. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ…pe… aññataraṃ dīghāyukaṃ devanikāyaṃ upapanno hoti. Ayaṃ pañcamo akkhaṇo asamayo brahmacariyavāsāya. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ…pe… paccantimesu janapadesu paccājāto hoti milakkhesu milakkhakesu (syā. kaṃ.) milakkhūsu (ka.) aviññātāresu, yattha natthi gati bhikkhūnaṃ bhikkhunīnaṃ upāsakānaṃ upāsikānaṃ. Ayaṃ chaṭṭho akkhaṇo asamayo brahmacariyavāsāya. milakkhakesu (syā. kaṃ.) milakkhūsu (ka.) ‘‘Puna caparaṃ…pe… majjhimesu janapadesu paccājāto hoti. So ca hoti micchādiṭṭhiko viparītadassano – ‘natthi dinnaṃ, natthi yiṭṭhaṃ, natthi hutaṃ, natthi sukatadukkaṭānaṃ sukaṭa dukkaṭānaṃ (sī. pī.) kammānaṃ phalaṃ vipāko, natthi ayaṃ loko, natthi paro loko, natthi mātā, natthi pitā, natthi sattā opapātikā, natthi loke samaṇabrāhmaṇā sammaggatā sammāpaṭipannā ye imañca lokaṃ parañca lokaṃ sayaṃ abhiññā sacchikatvā pavedentī’ti. Ayaṃ sattamo akkhaṇo asamayo brahmacariyavāsāya. sukaṭa dukkaṭānaṃ (sī. pī.) ‘‘Puna caparaṃ…pe… majjhimesu janapadesu paccājāto hoti. So ca hoti duppañño jaḷo eḷamūgo, nappaṭibalo subhāsitadubbhāsitānamatthamaññātuṃ. Ayaṃ aṭṭhamo akkhaṇo asamayo brahmacariyavāsāya. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, tathāgato ca loke na katthaci nakāro na dissati uppanno hoti arahaṃ sammāsambuddho, dhammo ca na desiyati opasamiko parinibbāniko sambodhagāmī sugatappavedito. Ayañca puggalo majjhimesu janapadesu paccājāto hoti, so ca hoti paññavā ajaḷo aneḷamūgo, paṭibalo subhāsita-dubbhāsitānamatthamaññātuṃ. Ayaṃ navamo akkhaṇo asamayo brahmacariyavāsāya. katthaci nakāro na dissati
342. Nine inopportune, untimely situations for leading the holy life.

Friends, there is the case where a Tathāgata appears in the world, worthy & rightly self-awakened ( arahaṃ sammāsambuddho). The Dharma is taught that leads to stilling, to total unbinding, to self-awakening as declared by One Well-gone (sugatappavedito). But this person is reborn in hell. This is the first inopportune, untimely situation for leading the holy life.

“And further, friends, there is the case where a Tathāgata appears in the world, worthy & rightly self-awakened. The Dharma is taught that leads to stilling, to total unbinding, to self-awakening as declared by One Well-gone. but this person is reborn in an animal womb… This is the second inopportune, untimely situation for leading the holy life.

“And further, …he is reborn in the realm of the hungry ghosts… This is the third inopportune, untimely situation for leading the holy life.

“And further, … in the host of the asuras… This is the fourth inopportune, untimely situation for leading the holy life.

“And further, … in a certain host of long-lived devas… This is the fifth inopportune, untimely situation for leading the holy life.

“And further, … he is born in a border region among ignorant barbarians where there is no opening for monks, nuns, male lay followers, or female lay followers to go.… This is the sixth inopportune, untimely situation for leading the holy life.

“And further, … Or he is born in the Middle Country (majjhimesu janapadesu paccājāto hoti). but he has wrong views & skewed vision: ‘There is nothing given (natthi dinnaṃ), nothing offered, nothing sacrificed (natthi hutaṃ). There is no fruit or result of good or bad actions. There is no this world, no next world, no mother, no father; there are no spontaneously reborn beings; there are no contemplatives or brāhmaṇas who, faring rightly & practicing rightly, proclaim this world & the next after having directly known & realized it for themselves.’ This is the seventh inopportune, untimely situation for leading the holy life.

“And further, … Or he is born in the Middle Country (majjhimesu janapadesu paccājāto hoti). but he is undiscerning, dull, drooling, incapable of knowing the meaning of what is well-said or ill-said.
This is the eighth inopportune, untimely situation for leading the holy life.

“And further, friend, a Tathāgata does not arise in the world, a worthy (arhat) and rightly self-awakened one (sammāsambuddha), and the dharma is not taught, which leads to stilling (opaśamika), to complete liberation (parinirvāṇika), to awakening (sambodhi-gāmī), proclaimed by the Well-Gone (sugata).

This person is reborn in the Middle Country and is discerning (paññavā), not dull (ajaḷa), not drooling and mute (aneḷamūga), capable of understanding the meaning of what is well-spoken (subhāṣita) and poorly-spoken (dubbhāṣita).

This is the ninth inopportune, untimely situation for leading the holy life.
342. Akkhaṇesu dhammo ca desiyatīti catusaccadhammo desiyati. Opasamikoti kilesūpasamakaro. Parinibbānikoti kilesaparinibbānena parinibbānāvaho. Sambodhagāmīti catumaggañāṇapaṭivedhagāmī. Aññataranti asaññabhavaṃ vā arūpabhavaṃ vā.
342. "And the dharma is taught at the right times" means the dharma of the four noble truths is taught.

"Opasamiko" means the one who pacifies the defilements (kleśa).

"Parinibbāniko" means bringing about parinirvāṇa through the parinirvāṇa of the defilements.

"Sambodhagāmī" means leading to the realization of the knowledge of the four paths (catumaggañāṇa).

"Aññatara" means either the realm of non-perception (asaññabhava) or the formless realm (arūpabhava).
343
343. ‘‘Nava anupubbavihārā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā…pe… dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Pītiyā ca virāgā…pe… tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Sukhassa ca pahānā …pe… catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā…pe… ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘anantaṃ viññāṇa’nti viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘natthi kiñcī’ti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Sabbaso ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Sabbaso nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ upasampajja viharati.
343. “Nine step-by-step dwellings: Here, a bhikṣu, quite secluded from sense pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states, enters and dwells in the first jhāna, which is accompanied by thought and examination, born of seclusion, filled with joy and happiness. With the stilling of thought and examination, he enters and dwells in the second jhāna. With the fading away of joy, he enters and dwells in the third jhāna. With the abandoning of pleasure, he enters and dwells in the fourth jhāna. With the complete transcending of perceptions of form, he enters and dwells in the dimension of the infinitude of space (ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ). With the complete transcending of the dimension of the infinitude of space, aware that ‘consciousness is infinite,’ he enters and dwells in the dimension of the infinitude of consciousness (viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ). With the complete transcending of the dimension of the infinitude of consciousness, aware that ‘there is nothing,’ he enters and dwells in the dimension of nothingness (ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ). With the complete transcending of the dimension of nothingness, he enters and dwells in the dimension of neither perception nor non-perception (nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ). With the complete transcending of the dimension of neither perception nor non-perception, he enters and dwells in the cessation of perception and feeling (saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ).
343.Anupubbavihārāti anupaṭipāṭiyā samāpajjitabbavihārā.
343. Anupubbavihārā means vihārā to be attained in due order.
344
344. ‘‘Nava anupubbanirodhā. Paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ samāpannassa kāmasaññā niruddhā hoti. Dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ samāpannassa vitakkavicārā niruddhā honti. Tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ samāpannassa pīti niruddhā hoti. Catutthaṃ jhānaṃ samāpannassa assāsapassāssā niruddhā honti. Ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samāpannassa rūpasaññā niruddhā hoti. Viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samāpannassa ākāsānañcāyatanasaññā niruddhā hoti. Ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samāpannassa viññāṇañcāyatanasaññā niruddhā hoti. Nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samāpannassa ākiñcaññāyatanasaññā niruddhā hoti. Saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ samāpannassa saññā ca vedanā ca niruddhā honti. ‘‘Ime kho, āvuso, tena bhagavatā jānatā passatā arahatā sammāsambuddhena nava dhammā sammadakkhātā. Tattha sabbeheva saṅgāyitabbaṃ…pe… atthāya hitāya sukhāya devamanussānaṃ. Dasakaṃ
344. "Nine step-by-step cessations:
When one has attained the first jhāna (first dhyāna), the perception of sensuality has ceased.
When one has attained the second jhāna (second dhyāna), directed thoughts & evaluations have ceased.
When one has attained the third jhāna (third dhyāna), rapture has ceased.
When one has attained the fourth jhāna (fourth dhyāna), in-&-out breaths have ceased.
When one has attained the dimension of the infinitude of space (ākāsānañcāyatana), the perception of forms has ceased.
When one has attained the dimension of the infinitude of consciousness (viññāṇañcāyatana), the perception of the dimension of the infinitude of space has ceased.
When one has attained the dimension of nothingness (ākiñcaññāyatana), the perception of the dimension of the infinitude of consciousness has ceased.
When one has attained the dimension of neither perception nor non-perception (nevasaññānāsaññāyatana), the perception of the dimension of nothingness has ceased.
When one has attained the cessation of perception & feeling (saññāvedayitanirodha), perceptions & feelings have ceased.

"These, friends, are the nine dharmas (nine things) that are rightly expounded by the Blessed One—the one who knows, the one who sees, the Worthy One, rightly self-awakened (sammāsambuddha).
There all should sing together… for the benefit, welfare, and happiness of gods and humans.

The tenth.
344.Anupubbanirodhāti anupaṭipāṭiyā nirodhā. ‘‘Ime, kho āvuso’’tiādi vuttanayeneva yojetabbaṃ. Iti channaṃ navakānaṃ vasena catupaṇṇāsa pañhe kathento thero sāmaggirasaṃ dassesīti. Navakavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Dasakavaṇṇanā
344. Anupubbanirodhā means cessation in due order.

“These, friend,” etc., should be connected in the manner previously stated. Thus, while explaining forty-four questions based on six groups of nine, the elder showed the taste of concord.

The Commentary on the Nines is finished.

Commentary on the Tens
345
345. ‘‘Atthi kho, āvuso, tena bhagavatā jānatā passatā arahatā sammāsambuddhena dasa dhammā sammadakkhātā. Tattha sabbeheva saṅgāyitabbaṃ…pe… atthāya hitāya sukhāya devamanussānaṃ. Katame dasa? ‘‘Dasa nāthakaraṇā dhammā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu sīlavā hoti. Pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvuto viharati ācāragocarasampanno, aṇumattesu vajjesu bhayadassāvī samādāya sikkhati sikkhāpadesu. Yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu sīlavā hoti, pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvuto viharati, ācāragocarasampanno, aṇumattesu vajjesu bhayadassāvī samādāya sikkhati sikkhāpadesu. Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo. nāthakaraṇā dhammā. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu bahussuto hoti sutadharo sutasannicayo. Ye te dhammā ādikalyāṇā majjhekalyāṇā pariyosānakalyāṇā sātthā sabyañjanā sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ (sī. syā. pī.) kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ abhivadanti, tathārūpāssa dhammā bahussutā honti dhatā (ka. sī. syā. kaṃ.) dhātā vacasā paricitā manasānupekkhitā diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā, yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu bahussuto hoti…pe… diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā. Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo. sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ (sī. syā. pī.) dhatā (ka. sī. syā. kaṃ.) ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu kalyāṇamitto hoti kalyāṇasahāyo kalyāṇasampavaṅko. Yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu kalyāṇamitto hoti kalyāṇasahāyo kalyāṇasampavaṅko. Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu suvaco hoti sovacassakaraṇehi dhammehi samannāgato khamo padakkhiṇaggāhī anusāsaniṃ. Yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu suvaco hoti…pe… padakkhiṇaggāhī anusāsaniṃ. Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu yāni tāni sabrahmacārīnaṃ uccāvacāni kiṃkaraṇīyāni, tattha dakkho hoti analaso tatrupāyāya vīmaṃsāya samannāgato, alaṃ kātuṃ alaṃ saṃvidhātuṃ. Yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu yāni tāni sabrahmacārīnaṃ…pe… alaṃ saṃvidhātuṃ. Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu dhammakāmo hoti piyasamudāhāro, abhidhamme abhivinaye uḷārapāmojjo uḷārapāmujjo (sī. pī.), oḷārapāmojjo (syā. kaṃ.). Yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu dhammakāmo hoti…pe… uḷārapāmojjo uḷārapāmujjo (sī. pī.), oḷārapāmojjo (syā. kaṃ.). Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo. uḷārapāmujjo (sī. pī.), oḷārapāmojjo (syā. kaṃ.) uḷārapāmujjo (sī. pī.), oḷārapāmojjo (syā. kaṃ.) ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso , bhikkhu santuṭṭho hoti itarītarehi cīvarapiṇḍapātasenāsanagilānappaccayabhesajjaparikkhārehi . Yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu santuṭṭho hoti…pe… parikkhārehi. Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu āraddhavīriyo viharati akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ upasampadāya, thāmavā daḷhaparakkamo anikkhittadhuro kusalesu dhammesu. Yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu āraddhavīriyo viharati…pe… anikkhittadhuro kusalesu dhammesu. Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu satimā hoti paramena satinepakkena samannāgato cirakatampi cirabhāsitampi saritā anussaritā. Yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu satimā hoti…pe… saritā anussaritā. Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo. ‘‘Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu paññavā hoti, udayatthagāminiyā paññāya samannāgato ariyāya nibbedhikāya sammādukkhakkhayagāminiyā. Yaṃpāvuso, bhikkhu paññavā hoti…pe… sammādukkhakkhayagāminiyā. Ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo.
345. “There are, friends, ten dharmas that are rightly expounded by the Blessed One—the one who knows, the one who sees, the Worthy One, rightly self-awakened.

There, all should be chanted together… for the benefit, for the happiness of gods and humans.
What are the ten?

“Ten qualities creating a protector (nāthakaraṇā dhammā).
Here, friends, a monk (bhikkhu) is virtuous (sīlavā hoti).
He dwells restrained in accordance with the Pāṭimokkha (pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvuto), consummate in his behavior & sphere of activity (ācāragocarasampanno). He trains himself, having undertaken the training rules, seeing danger in the slightest faults (aṇumattesu vajjesu bhayadassāvī samādāya sikkhati sikkhāpadesu).
And the fact that he is virtuous, friends, a monk is virtuous, he dwells restrained in accordance with the Pāṭimokkha, consummate in his behavior & sphere of activity. He trains himself, having undertaken the training rules, seeing danger in the slightest faults.
This quality also creates a protector (ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo).

Qualities creating a protector (nāthakaraṇā dhammā).

“And further, friends, a monk has heard much (bahussuto hoti), has retained what he has heard (sutadharo), has stored what he has heard (sutasannicayo).
Those dharmas that are admirable in the beginning (ādikalyāṇā), admirable in the middle (majjhekalyāṇā), admirable in the end (pariyosānakalyāṇā), that—in their meaning (sātthā) and expression (sabyañjanā)—proclaim the holy life (brahmacariyaṃ) that is entirely perfect (kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ), surpassingly pure (parisuddhaṃ abhivadanti): those teachings he has listened to often, retained, discussed, accumulated, examined with his mind, & well-penetrated in terms of his views (dhammā bahussutā honti dhātā vacasā paricitā manasānupekkhitā diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā). And the fact that he has heard much, friends, a monk has heard much…well-penetrated in terms of his views.
This quality also creates a protector (ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo).

“And further, friends, a monk has admirable friends (kalyāṇamitto), admirable comrades (kalyāṇasahāyo), admirable companions (kalyāṇasampavaṅko).
And the fact that he has admirable friends, friends, a monk has admirable friends, admirable comrades, admirable companions.
This quality also creates a protector (ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo).

“And further, friends, a monk is easy to speak to (suvaco hoti), endowed with qualities that make him easy to speak to (sovacassakaraṇehi dhammehi samannāgato), patient (khamo), respectful to instruction (padakkhiṇaggāhī anusāsaniṃ).
And the fact that he is easy to speak to, friends, a monk is easy to speak to…respectful to instruction.
This quality also creates a protector (ayampi dhammo nāthakaraṇo).
“And furthermore, the monk, friend, is skilled in the various tasks that are to be done for his fellow monks in the holy life. He is energetic, endowed with the wisdom to use the appropriate means in those tasks, capable of doing them and arranging them. And the fact that the monk is skilled in those various tasks for his fellows in the holy life…capable of arranging them, this is a quality creating a protector (nāthakaraṇo dhammo).

“And furthermore, the monk, friend, is one who desires the Dhamma, speaks in an endearing way, and takes great joy in the Abhidhamma and Abhivinaya. And the fact that the monk is one who desires the Dhamma…takes great joy, this is a quality creating a protector.

“And furthermore, the monk, friend, is content with any kind of robe cloth, alms food, lodging, and medicinal requisites for curing sickness. And the fact that the monk is content…requisites, this is a quality creating a protector.

“And furthermore, the monk, friend, dwells with aroused energy for the abandoning of unwholesome qualities and the undertaking of wholesome qualities; strong, firm in his effort, not laying down the burden in regard to wholesome qualities. And the fact that the monk dwells with aroused energy…not laying down the burden, this is a quality creating a protector.

“And furthermore, the monk, friend, is mindful (satimā), endowed with supreme skill in mindfulness, remembering and recollecting what was done and said long ago. And the fact that the monk is mindful…recollecting, this is a quality creating a protector.

“And furthermore, the monk, friend, is wise (paññavā), endowed with wisdom (paññā) that leads to the arising and passing away of phenomena, noble, penetrative, leading to the complete destruction of suffering (sammādukkhakkhayagāminiyā). And the fact that the monk is wise…leading to the complete destruction of suffering, this is a quality creating a protector.
[p.]
345. Iti navakavasena sāmaggirasaṃ dassetvā idāni dasakavasena dassetuṃ puna desanaṃ ārabhi. Tattha nāthakaraṇāti ‘‘sanāthā, bhikkhave, viharatha mā anāthā, dasa ime, bhikkhave, dhammā nāthakaraṇā’’ti (a. ni. 10.18) evaṃ akkhātā attano patiṭṭhākarā dhammā. Kalyāṇamittotiādīsu sīlādiguṇasampannā kalyāṇā assa mittāti kalyāṇamitto. Te cassa ṭhānanisajjādīsu saha ayanato sahāyāti kalyāṇasahāyo. Cittena ceva kāyena ca kalyāṇamittesu eva sampavaṅko onatoti kalyāṇasampavaṅko. Suvacohotīti sukhena vattabbo hoti sukhena anusāsitabbo. Khamoti gāḷhena pharusena kakkhaḷena vuccamāno khamati, na kuppati. Padakkhiṇaggāhī anusāsaninti yathā ekacco ovadiyamāno vāmato gaṇhāti, paṭippharati vā asuṇanto vā gacchati, evaṃ akatvā ‘‘ovadatha, bhante , anusāsatha, tumhesu anovadantesu ko añño ovadissatī’’ti padakkhiṇaṃ gaṇhāti. Kalyāṇamitto kalyāṇamitto. kalyāṇasahāyo. kalyāṇasampavaṅko. Suvaco hotī Khamo Padakkhiṇaggāhī anusāsani Uccāvacānīti uccāni ca avacāni ca. Kiṃ karaṇīyānīti kiṃ karomīti evaṃ vatvā kattabbakammāni. Tattha uccakammāni nāma cīvarassa karaṇaṃ rajanaṃ cetiye sudhākammaṃ uposathāgāracetiyagharabodhiyagharesu kattabbanti evamādi. Avacakammaṃ nāma pādadhovanamakkhanādikhuddakakammaṃ. Tatrupāyāyāti tatrupagamanīyā. Alaṃ kātunti kātuṃ samattho hoti. Alaṃ saṃvidhātunti vicāretuṃ samattho. Uccāvacānī Kiṃ karaṇīyānī uccakammāni Avacakammaṃ Tatrupāyāyā Alaṃ kātu Alaṃ saṃvidhātu Dhamme assa kāmo sinehoti dhammakāmo, tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ piyāyatīti attho. Piyasamudāhāroti parasmiṃ kathente sakkaccaṃ suṇāti, sayañca paresaṃ desetukāmo hotīti attho. ‘‘Abhidhamme abhivinaye’’ti ettha dhammo abhidhammo, vinayo abhivinayoti catukkaṃ veditabbaṃ. Tattha dhammoti suttantapiṭakaṃ. Abhidhammoti satta pakaraṇāni. Vinayoti ubhatovibhaṅgā. Abhivinayoti khandhakaparivārā. Atha vā suttantapiṭakampi abhidhammapiṭakampi dhammo eva. Maggaphalāni abhidhammo. Sakalaṃ vinayapiṭakaṃ vinayo. Kilesavūpasamakāraṇaṃ abhivinayo. Iti sabbasmimpi ettha dhamme abhidhamme vinaye abhivinaye ca. Uḷārapāmojjoti bahulapāmojjo hotīti attho. dhammakāmo, Piyasamudāhāro dhammo Abhidhammo Vinayo Abhivinayo dhammo abhidhammo. vinayo. abhivinayo. Uḷārapāmojjo Kusalesudhammesūti kāraṇatthe bhummaṃ, catubhūmakakusaladhammakāraṇā, tesaṃ adhigamatthāya anikkhittadhuro hotīti attho. Kusalesu dhammesū
345. Thus, having shown the taste of harmony by means of the group of nine, now, in order to show [it] by means of the group of ten, again he began the teaching.

There, nāthakaraṇā means, "Monks, live with support, not without support. These ten things, monks, are supportive" (A. Ni. 10.18). Thus, the qualities that are declared as making one's own support.

Kalyāṇamitto etc., [means] "He has good qualities such as moral conduct, etc., he is a good friend," [therefore] kalyāṇamitto. And for him, in places such as sitting and lying down, he is a companion in going together, [therefore] kalyāṇasahāyo. With mind and body, he is inclined to good friends, so he is kalyāṇasampavaṅko. Suvaco means he is easy to speak to and easy to instruct. Khamo means when being spoken to harshly, coarsely, and severely, he is patient, he does not get angry. Padakkhiṇaggāhī anusāsani means, just as some, when being advised, grasp it from the left, or refute it, or go away without listening, not doing thus, [but thinking,] "Advise me, venerable sir, instruct me; when you do not advise, who else will advise?" he grasps it from the right.

Kalyāṇamitto Kalyāṇamitto. Kalyāṇasahāyo. Kalyāṇasampavaṅko. Suvaco. Khamo. Padakkhiṇaggāhī anusāsani.

Uccāvacānī means high and low. Kiṃ karaṇīyānī means saying "What should I do?" [and doing] the tasks to be done. There, uccakammāni are namely, making a robe, dyeing, doing plaster work on a cetiya, and doing work in the uposatha hall, cetiya house, and bodhi tree house, and so forth. Avacakammaṃ is namely, washing the feet, anointing, and other small tasks. Tatrupāyāyā means those who should be approached there. Alaṃ kātu means he is capable of doing. Alaṃ saṃvidhātu means he is capable of considering.

Uccāvacānī Kiṃ karaṇīyānī Uccakammāni Avacakammaṃ Tatrupāyāyā Alaṃ kātu Alaṃ saṃvidhātu He has desire and affection for the Dharma, therefore [he is a] dharmakāmo, meaning he loves the Tripiṭaka, the word of the Buddha. Piyasamudāhāro means when another is speaking, he listens respectfully, and he himself wants to teach others, this is the meaning. "In abhidhamma and abhivinaya," here, dharma is abhidhamma, and vinaya is abhivinaya, this tetrad should be understood. There, dharma is the sūtrānta-piṭaka. Abhidhamma is the seven treatises. Vinaya is the ubhatovibhaṅga. Abhivinaya is the khandhaka-parivāra. Or else, the sūtrānta-piṭaka and the abhidhamma-piṭaka are also simply the dharma. The paths and fruits [are] abhidhamma. The entire vinaya-piṭaka is vinaya. The cause of pacifying defilements is abhivinaya. Thus, in all of this, here, in dharma, abhidhamma, vinaya, and abhivinaya. Uḷārapāmojjo means he has abundant joy, this is the meaning.
He is a lover of the Dharma (dhammakāmo), one whose speech is affectionate (piyasamudāhāro), [skilled in] the Dharma (dhammo), the Abhidhamma (abhidhammo), the Vinaya (vinayo), the Abhivinaya (abhivinayo), the Dharma (dhammo), the Abhidhamma (abhidhammo), the Vinaya (vinayo), the Abhivinaya (abhivinayo), one who has lofty joy (uḷārapāmojjo).

"In wholesome qualities (kusalesu dhammesū)": a pleonasm in the sense of cause, because of the wholesome qualities of the four planes, meaning he does not lay aside the burden for the sake of attaining them.

In wholesome qualities (kusalesu dhammesū).
346
346. Dasa kasiṇāyatanāni. Pathavīkasiṇameko sañjānāti, uddhaṃ adho tiriyaṃ advayaṃ appamāṇaṃ. Āpokasiṇameko sañjānāti…pe… tejokasiṇameko sañjānāti… vāyokasiṇameko sañjānāti… nīlakasiṇameko sañjānāti… pītakasiṇameko sañjānāti… lohitakasiṇameko sañjānāti… odātakasiṇameko sañjānāti… ākāsakasiṇameko sañjānāti… viññāṇakasiṇameko sañjānāti, uddhaṃ adho tiriyaṃ advayaṃ appamāṇaṃ.
346. The ten kasiṇa spheres of perception (kasiṇāyatanāni).

One perceives the earth kasiṇa (pathavīkasiṇa), above, below, and across, undivided and immeasurable.
One perceives the water kasiṇa (āpokasiṇa) ... the fire kasiṇa (tejokasiṇa) ... the wind kasiṇa (vāyokasiṇa) ... the blue kasiṇa (nīlakasiṇa) ... the yellow kasiṇa (pītakasiṇa) ... the red kasiṇa (lohitakasiṇa) ... the white kasiṇa (odātakasiṇa) ... the space kasiṇa (ākāsakasiṇa) ... the consciousness kasiṇa (viññāṇakasiṇa), above, below, and across, undivided and immeasurable.
346. Kasiṇadasake sakalaṭṭhena kasiṇāni. Tadārammaṇānaṃ dhammānaṃ khettaṭṭhena vā adhiṭṭhānaṭṭhena vā āyatanāni. Uddhanti upari gaganatalābhimukhaṃ. Adhoti heṭṭhā bhūmitalābhimukhaṃ. Tiriyanti khettamaṇḍalamiva samantā paricchinditvā. Ekacco hi uddhameva kasiṇaṃ vaḍḍheti, ekacco adho, ekacco samantato. Tena tena vā kāraṇena evaṃ pasāreti ālokamiva rūpadassanakāmo. Tena vuttaṃ ‘‘pathavīkasiṇameko sañjānāti uddhaṃ adho tiriya’’nti. Advayanti idaṃ pana ekassa aññabhāvānupagamanatthaṃ vuttaṃ. Yathā hi udakaṃ paviṭṭhassa sabbadisāsu udakameva hoti, na aññaṃ, evameva pathavīkasiṇaṃ pathavīkasiṇameva hoti , natthi tassa añño kasiṇasambhedoti. Esa nayo sabbattha. Appamāṇanti idaṃ tassa tassa pharaṇaappamāṇavasena vuttaṃ. Tañhi cetasā pharanto sakalameva pharati, na ‘‘ayamassa ādi, idaṃ majjha’’nti pamāṇaṃ gaṇhātīti. Viññāṇakasiṇanti cettha kasiṇugghāṭimākāse pavattaviññāṇaṃ. Tattha kasiṇavasena kasiṇugghāṭimākāse kasiṇugghāṭimākāsavasena tattha pavattaviññāṇe uddhaṃ adho tiriyatā veditabbā. Ayamettha saṅkhepo. Kammaṭṭhānabhāvanānayena panetāni pathavīkasiṇādīni vitthārato visuddhimagge vuttāneva. Akusalakammapathadasakavaṇṇanā
346. In the kasiṇa decade, the kasiṇas are so called in the sense of totality. The sense spheres (āyatana) [are so called] in the sense of the field or in the sense of the basis of phenomena with that as their object. "Upward" (uddhaṃ) means facing the upper sky. "Downward" (adho) means facing the lower ground. "Across" (tiriyaṃ) means having delimited all around, like a field circle. For one extends a kasiṇa upwards only, another downwards, another all round. Or, for some reason or another, one projects it thus, like a person wanting to see forms [projects] light. Therefore, it was said, "One perceives the earth kasiṇa upward, downward, across."

"Non-dual" (advayaṃ) is said here in order to avoid the acceptance of otherness of one. Just as there is water and nothing else in all directions for one who has entered into water, so too, the earth kasiṇa is the earth kasiṇa only; there is no mixture of it with any other kasiṇa. This method applies to all [the other kasiṇas].

"Immeasurable" (appamāṇaṃ) is said with reference to the immeasurable extent of the spreading of that, that is, the spreading of each of them. For, spreading it with the mind, one spreads it entirely; one does not take a measure, thinking, "This is its beginning, this its middle."

Here, the "consciousness kasiṇa" (viññāṇakasiṇa) is the consciousness occurring in the space of the removed kasiṇa. There, upwardness, downwardness, and acrossness should be understood with respect to the kasiṇa, with respect to the space of the removed kasiṇa in terms of the kasiṇa, and with respect to the consciousness occurring there in terms of the space of the removed kasiṇa.

This is a brief explanation (ayaṃ ettha saṅkhepo).

But these earth kasiṇas and so on have been spoken of extensively in the Visuddhimagga according to the method of meditation subject and cultivation.

Explanation of the decade of unwholesome paths of action
347
347. ‘‘Dasa akusalakammapathā – pāṇātipāto, adinnādānaṃ, kāmesumicchācāro, musāvādo, pisuṇā vācā, pharusā vācā, samphappalāpo, abhijjhā, byāpādo, micchādiṭṭhi. ‘‘Dasa kusalakammapathā – pāṇātipātā veramaṇī, adinnādānā veramaṇī, kāmesumicchācārā veramaṇī, musāvādā veramaṇī, pisuṇāya vācāya veramaṇī, pharusāya vācāya veramaṇī, samphappalāpā veramaṇī, anabhijjhā, abyāpādo, sammādiṭṭhi. kusalakammapathā –
“Ten unskillful courses of action ( akuśalakammapathā): taking life ( pāṇātipāto), taking what is not given (adinnādānaṃ), sexual misconduct (kāmesumicchācāro), telling lies (musāvādo), divisive speech (pisuṇā vācā), harsh speech (pharusā vācā), idle chatter (samphappalāpo), greed (abhijjhā), ill will (byāpādo), wrong view (micchādiṭṭhi).

“Ten skillful courses of action (kusalakammapathā): refraining from taking life (pāṇātipātā veramaṇī), refraining from taking what is not given (adinnādānā veramaṇī), refraining from sexual misconduct (kāmesumicchācārā veramaṇī), refraining from telling lies (musāvādā veramaṇī), refraining from divisive speech (pisuṇāya vācāya veramaṇī), refraining from harsh speech (pharusāya vācāya veramaṇī), refraining from idle chatter (samphappalāpā veramaṇī), non-greed (anabhijjhā), non-ill will (abyāpādo), right view (sammādiṭṭhi).
347. Kammapathesu kammāneva sugatiduggatīnaṃ pathabhūtattā kammapathā nāma. Tesu pāṇātipāto adinnādānaṃ musāvādādayo ca cattāro brahmajāle vitthāritā eva. Kāmesumicchācāroti ettha pana kāmesūti methunasamācāresu methunavatthūsu vā. Micchācāroti ekantanindito lāmakācāro. Lakkhaṇato pana asaddhammādhippāyena kāyadvārappavattā agamanīyaṭṭhānavītikkamacetanā kāmesumicchācāro. Tattha agamanīyaṭṭhānaṃ nāma purisānaṃ tāva māturakkhitā, piturakkhitā, mātāpiturakkhitā, bhāturakkhitā, bhaginirakkhitā, ñātirakkhitā, gottarakkhitā, dhammarakkhitā, sārakkhā, saparidaṇḍāti māturakkhitādayo dasa. Dhanakkītā, chandavāsinī, bhogavāsinī, paṭavāsinī, odapattakinī, obhatacumbaṭā, dāsī ca bhariyā ca, kammakārī ca bhariyā ca, dhajāhaṭā, muhuttikāti etā dhanakkītādayo dasāti vīsati. Itthīsu pana dvinnaṃ sārakkhasaparidaṇḍānaṃ dasannañca dhanakkītādīnanti dvādasannaṃ itthīnaṃ aññe purisā. Idaṃ agamanīyaṭṭhānaṃ nāma. So panesa micchācāro sīlādiguṇarahite agamanīyaṭṭhāne appasāvajjo. Sīlādiguṇasampanne mahāsāvajjo. Tassa cattāro sambhārā agamanīyavatthu, tasmiṃ sevanacittaṃ, sevanappayogo, maggenamaggappaṭipattiadhivāsananti. Eko payogo sāhatthiko eva. agamanīyaṭṭhānaṃ Abhijjhāyatīti abhijjhā, parabhaṇḍābhimukhī hutvā tanninnatāya pavattatīti attho. Sā ‘‘aho vata idaṃ mamassā’’ti evaṃ parabhaṇḍābhijjhāyanalakkhaṇā adinnādānaṃ viya appasāvajjā mahāsāvajjā ca. Tassā dve sambhārā parabhaṇḍaṃ, attano pariṇāmanañca. Parabhaṇḍavatthuke hi lobhe uppannepi na tāva kammapathabhedo hoti, yāva ‘‘aho vatīdaṃ mamassā’’ti attano na pariṇāmeti. abhijjhā, Hitasukhaṃ byāpādayatīti byāpādo. So paraṃ vināsāya manopadosalakkhaṇo pharusāvācā viya appasāvajjo mahāsāvajjo ca. Tassa dve sambhārā parasatto ca, tassa vināsacintā ca. Parasattavatthuke hi kodhe uppannepi na tāva kammapathabhedo hoti, yāva ‘‘aho vatāyaṃ ucchijjheyya vinasseyyā’’ti tassa vināsaṃ na cinteti. byāpādo. Yathābhuccagahaṇābhāvena micchā passatīti micchādiṭṭhi. Sā ‘‘natthi dinna’’ntiādinā nayena viparītadassanalakkhaṇā. Samphappalāpo viya appasāvajjā mahāsāvajjā ca. Apica aniyatā appasāvajjā, niyatā mahāsāvajjā. Tassā dve sambhārā vatthuno ca gahitākāraviparītatā, yathā ca taṃ gaṇhāti, tathābhāvena tassūpaṭṭhānanti. micchādiṭṭhi. Imesaṃ pana dasannaṃ akusalakammapathānaṃ dhammato koṭṭhāsato ārammaṇato vedanāto mūlatoti pañcahākārehi vinicchayo veditabbo. Tattha dhammatoti etesu hi paṭipāṭiyā satta cetanādhammāva honti. Abhijjhādayo tayo cetanāsampayuttā. dhammato Koṭṭhāsatoti paṭipāṭiyā satta, micchādiṭṭhi cāti ime aṭṭha kammapathā eva honti, no mūlāni. Abhijjhābyāpādā kammapathā ceva mūlāni ca. Abhijjhā hi mūlaṃ patvā lobho akusalamūlaṃ hoti. Byāpādo doso akusalamūlaṃ hoti. Koṭṭhāsato Ārammaṇatoti pāṇātipāto jīvitindriyārammaṇato saṅkhārārammaṇo hoti. Adinnādānaṃ sattārammaṇaṃ vā saṅkhārārammaṇaṃ vā, micchācāro phoṭṭhabbavasena saṅkhārārammaṇo. ‘‘Sattārammaṇo’’tipi eke. Musāvādo sattārammaṇo vā saṅkhārārammaṇo vā, tathā pisuṇavācā. Pharusavācā sattārammaṇāva. Samphappalāpo diṭṭhasutamutaviññātavasena sattārammaṇo vā saṅkhārārammaṇo vā. Tathā abhijjhā. Byāpādo sattārammaṇova. Micchādiṭṭhi tebhūmakadhammavasena saṅkhārārammaṇā. Ārammaṇato Vedanātoti pāṇātipāto dukkhavedano hoti. Kiñcāpi hi rājāno coraṃ disvā hasamānāpi ‘‘gacchatha naṃ ghātethā’’ti vadanti, sanniṭṭhāpakacetanā pana dukkhasampayuttāva hoti. Adinnādānaṃ tivedanaṃ. Micchācāro sukhamajjhattavasena dvivedano. Sanniṭṭhāpakacitte pana majjhattavedano na hoti. Musāvādo tivedano. Tathā pisuṇavācā. Pharusavācā dukkhavedanā. Samphappalāpo tivedano. Abhijjhā sukhamajjhattavasena dvivedanā tathā micchādiṭṭhi. Byāpādo dukkhavedano. Vedanāto Mūlatoti pāṇātipāto dosamohavasena dvimūlako hoti. Adinnādānaṃ dosamohavasena vā lobhamohavasena vā. Micchācāro lobhamohavasena. Musāvādo dosamohavasena vā lobhamohavasena vā tathā pisuṇavācā samphappalāpo ca. Pharusavācā dosamohavasena. Abhijjhā mohavasena ekamūlā. Tathā byāpādo. Micchādiṭṭhi lobhamohavasena dvimūlāti. Mūlato Kusalakammapathadasakavaṇṇanā Pāṇātipātā veramaṇiādīni samādānasampattasamucchedavirativasena veditabbāni. Pāṇātipātā veramaṇi Dhammato pana etesupi paṭipāṭiyā satta cetanāpi vattanti viratiyopi. Ante tayo cetanāsampayuttāva. Dhammato Koṭṭhāsatoti paṭipāṭiyā satta kammapathā eva, no mūlāni. Ante tayo kammapathā ceva mūlāni ca. Anabhijjhā hi mūlaṃ patvā alobho kusalamūlaṃ hoti. Abyāpādo adoso kusalamūlaṃ. Sammādiṭṭhi amoho kusalamūlaṃ. Koṭṭhāsato Ārammaṇatoti pāṇātipātādīnaṃ ārammaṇāneva etesaṃ ārammaṇāni. Vītikkamitabbatoyeva hi veramaṇī nāma hoti. Yathā pana nibbānārammaṇo ariyamaggo kilese pajahati, evaṃ jīvitindriyādiārammaṇāpete kammapathā pāṇātipātādīni dussīlyāni pajahantīti veditabbā. Ārammaṇato Vedanātoti sabbe sukhavedanā honti majjhattavedanā vā. Kusalaṃ patvā hi dukkhavedanā nāma natthi. Vedanāto Mūlatoti paṭipāṭiyā satta ñāṇasampayuttacittena viramantassa alobhaadosaamohavasena timūlāni honti, ñāṇavippayuttacittena viramantassa dvimūlāni. Anabhijjhā ñāṇasampayuttacittena viramantassa dvimūlā, ñāṇavippayuttacittena ekamūlā. Alobho pana attanāva attano mūlaṃ na hoti. Abyāpādepi eseva nayo. Sammādiṭṭhi alobhādosavasena dvimūlā evāti. Mūlato Ariyavāsadasakavaṇṇanā
347. In the kammapathas, the karmas themselves are called kammapathas because they are the path to good and bad destinies. Among these, killing, taking what is not given, lying, and so forth, the first four, have already been extensively described in the Brahmajāla [Sutta]. As for kāmesumicchācāra, kāmesu means sexual intercourse or objects of sexual intercourse. Micchācāra means utterly blameworthy, base conduct. As to its characteristic, kāmesumicchācāra "sexual misconduct" is the volition to transgress bounds occurring through the body door by way of unrighteous intent.

Herein, agamanīyaṭṭhānaṃ means, firstly, for men, the ten kinds of women beginning with those protected by the mother, namely, “protected by the mother, protected by the father, protected by the mother and father, protected by the brother, protected by the sister, protected by relatives, protected by the clan, protected by the dharma, under protection, entailing a penalty.” The twenty [women] are the ten beginning with those purchased with money, namely, “one purchased with money, one who lives by her will, one who lives by her wealth, one who lives under protection, one who provides water, a kept woman, a female slave and a wife, a female worker and a wife, one captured in war, and a prostitute.” For women, other men are out of bounds for the twelve kinds of women consisting of the two, namely, under protection and entailing a penalty, and the ten beginning with those purchased with money. This is called agamanīyaṭṭhānaṃ. This sexual misconduct is less blameworthy when the one who is out of bounds is without good qualities such as ethical discipline, etc., and more blameworthy when the one who is out of bounds possesses good qualities such as ethical discipline, etc. There are four constituents of this act: an object which is out of bounds (agamanīy वस्तु), the mind to engage in that, the effort to engage, and consent to the union of sexual organs. The means (upayoga) is single: one’s own person.

Agamanīyaṭṭhānaṃ

Abhijjha, "covetousness": It covets, thus it is covetousness; the meaning is "having become directed towards others’ goods, it occurs through inclination towards them". It has the characteristic of coveting others’ goods thus: “Oh, that this were mine!” It is less blameworthy and more blameworthy as in the case of taking what is not given (adinnādāna). Its constituents are two: another’s goods, and the inclination for them to be one’s own. For even though greed (lobha) has arisen based on another’s goods, it is not classed as a course of karma so long as one does not incline to them as one’s own, “Oh, that this were mine!”

Abhijjha

Byāpādo, "ill will": It destroys welfare and happiness. It is less blameworthy and more blameworthy as in the case of harsh speech, its characteristic is mental corruption for the destruction of another. Its constituents are two: another being (parasattva), and the wish for that being’s destruction. For even though anger (kodha) has arisen based on another being (parasattva), there is no breach of a course of karma so long as one does not wish, “Oh, that this being might be cut off and destroyed!”

Byāpādo

Micchādiṭṭhi, "wrong view": It sees wrongly due to the absence of a correct grasp of things, thus it is wrong view. Its characteristic is the mistaken view that “there is no giving,” etc. It is less blameworthy and more blameworthy as in the case of gossip (samphappalāpa). Moreover, it is less blameworthy when not fixed in destiny, and more blameworthy when fixed. Its constituents are two: a mistaken manner of grasping the basis (vastu), and the appearance of that in accordance with the manner in which it has been grasped.

Micchādiṭṭhi

Now the exposition of these ten courses of unwholesome karma should be understood in five ways: as to mental state (dhammato), as to category (koṭṭhāsato), as to object (ārammaṇato), as to feeling (vedanāto), and as to root (mūlato).

Herein, as to mental state (dhammato): The first seven among these are volitional states (cetanādhammā) only. The three beginning with covetousness (abhijjhā) are associated with volition.

Dhammato

As to category (koṭṭhāsato): the first seven in order, and wrong view (micchādiṭṭhi), these eight are courses of karma only, not roots (mūlāni).
Covetousness and ill will are courses of action (kamma) and also roots.
For covetousness, having arrived at a root, is the unwholesome root greed (lobha).
And ill will is the unwholesome root hate (dosa).

From the standpoint of…
From the standpoint of object: killing living beings has formations as its object because of the object that is the life faculty.
Taking what is not given has beings or formations as object; misconduct has formations as object by way of tangible object.
Some say that it has only beings as object.
False speech has beings or formations as object; likewise malicious speech.
Harsh speech has only beings as object.
Gossip has either beings or formations as object by way of the seen, heard, sensed and cognized.
Likewise covetousness.
Ill will has only beings as object.
Wrong view has formations as object by way of the states belonging to the three planes .

From the standpoint of feeling: killing living beings has painful feeling.
For although kings, seeing a robber, say laughingly, “Go and execute him,” their volition consummating the action is associated only with pain.
Taking what is not given has three feelings.
Misconduct has two feelings, pleasant and neutral.
But in the mind which consummates the action there is no neutral feeling.
False speech has three feelings.
likewise malicious speech.
Harsh speech has painful feeling only.
Gossip has three feelings.
Covetousness has two feelings, pleasant and neutral; likewise wrong view.
Ill will has painful feeling only.

From the standpoint of root: killing living beings has two roots, by way of hate and delusion.
Taking what is not given, by way of hate and delusion or by way of greed and delusion.
Misconduct, by way of greed and delusion.
False speech, by way of hate and delusion or by way of greed and delusion; likewise for malicious speech and gossip.
Harsh speech, by way of hate and delusion.
Covetousness has one root, by way of delusion.
Likewise ill will.
Wrong view has two roots, by way of greed and delusion.

Description of the ten wholesome courses of action
The abstaining from killing living beings and so on should be understood by way of undertaking, accomplishment, utter destruction, and abstention.

Abstaining from killing living beings
From the standpoint of the norm, in these, in due order, seven volitions occur, as well as abstinences.
the last three are associated with volition only.

From the standpoint of the norm, from the standpoint of division, in due order, there are seven courses of action only, not roots.
At the end of those, the courses of action and the roots...

And, for non-covetousness, having attained the root, non-greed (alobha) is a wholesome root (kusalamūla).

Non-ill will (abyāpāda) and non-hatred (adosa) are wholesome roots (kusalamūla).

Right view (sammādiṭṭhi) and non-delusion (amoha) are wholesome roots (kusalamūla).

From the aspect of sections...

From the aspect of object (ālambanato): the objects of actions such as killing living beings are indeed their objects.

For restraint is called abstinence.

Just as the noble path (ariyamagga), which has Nirvāṇa as its object (nibbānārammaṇa), abandons defilements (kilesa), so too should it be understood that these courses of action (kammapatha), which have the life faculty (jīvitindriya), etc., as object, abandon evil conduct (dussīlya) beginning with killing living beings (pāṇātipātādīni).

From the aspect of object (ālambanato)...

From the aspect of feeling (vedanāto): all have pleasant feeling (sukhavedanā) or neutral feeling (majjhattavedanā).

For there is no painful feeling ( dukkhavedanā) which arrives at the wholesome (kusala).

From the aspect of feeling (vedanāto)...

From the aspect of root (mūlato): in due order, for one who abstains by means of consciousness associated with knowledge (ñāṇasampayuttacittena), there are three roots (timūlāni) in terms of non-greed (alobha), non-hatred (adosa), and non-delusion (amoha); for one who abstains by means of consciousness dissociated from knowledge (ñāṇavippayuttacittena), there are two roots (dvimūlāni).

Non-covetousness (anabhijjhā) has two roots (dvimūlā) in one who abstains by means of consciousness associated with knowledge (ñāṇasampayuttacittena), one root (ekamūlā) by means of consciousness dissociated from knowledge (ñāṇavippayuttacittena).

Non-greed (alobha), however, is not its own root (mūla).

And the same method (nayā) applies in the case of non-ill will (abyāpāda).

Right view (sammādiṭṭhi) has two roots (dvimūlā) in terms of non-greed (alobha) and non-hatred (adosa).

From the aspect of root (mūlato)...

Explanation of the Decade on the Noble Life (ariyavāsadasakavaṇṇanā).
348
348. ‘‘Dasa ariyavāsā. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu pañcaṅgavippahīno hoti, chaḷaṅgasamannāgato, ekārakkho, caturāpasseno, paṇunnapaccekasacco, samavayasaṭṭhesano, anāvilasaṅkappo, passaddhakāyasaṅkhāro, suvimuttacitto, suvimuttapañño. ‘‘Kathañcāvuso, bhikkhu pañcaṅgavippahīno hoti? Idhāvuso, bhikkhuno kāmacchando pahīno hoti, byāpādo pahīno hoti, thinamiddhaṃ pahīnaṃ hoti, uddhaccakukuccaṃ pahīnaṃ hoti, vicikicchā pahīnā hoti. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu pañcaṅgavippahīno hoti. ‘‘Kathañcāvuso, bhikkhu chaḷaṅgasamannāgato hoti? Idhāvuso, bhikkhu cakkhunā rūpaṃ disvā neva sumano hoti na dummano, upekkhako viharati sato sampajāno. Sotena saddaṃ sutvā…pe… manasā dhammaṃ viññāya neva sumano hoti na dummano, upekkhako viharati sato sampajāno. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu chaḷaṅgasamannāgato hoti. ‘‘Kathañcāvuso , bhikkhu ekārakkho hoti? Idhāvuso, bhikkhu satārakkhena cetasā samannāgato hoti. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu ekārakkho hoti . ‘‘Kathañcāvuso, bhikkhu caturāpasseno hoti? Idhāvuso, bhikkhu saṅkhāyekaṃ paṭisevati, saṅkhāyekaṃ adhivāseti, saṅkhāyekaṃ parivajjeti, saṅkhāyekaṃ vinodeti. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu caturāpasseno hoti. ‘‘Kathañcāvuso, bhikkhu paṇunnapaccekasacco hoti? Idhāvuso, bhikkhuno yāni tāni puthusamaṇabrāhmaṇānaṃ puthupaccekasaccāni, sabbāni tāni nunnāni honti paṇunnāni cattāni vantāni muttāni pahīnāni paṭinissaṭṭhāni. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu paṇunnapaccekasacco hoti. ‘‘Kathañcāvuso , bhikkhu samavayasaṭṭhesano hoti? Idhāvuso, bhikkhuno kāmesanā pahīnā hoti, bhavesanā pahīnā hoti, brahmacariyesanā paṭippassaddhā. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu samavayasaṭṭhesano hoti. ‘‘Kathañcāvuso, bhikkhu anāvilasaṅkappo hoti? Idhāvuso, bhikkhuno kāmasaṅkappo pahīno hoti, byāpādasaṅkappo pahīno hoti, vihiṃsāsaṅkappo pahīno hoti. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu anāvilasaṅkappo hoti. ‘‘Kathañcāvuso, bhikkhu passaddhakāyasaṅkhāro hoti ? Idhāvuso, bhikkhu sukhassa ca pahānā dukkhassa ca pahānā pubbeva somanassadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamā adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu passaddhakāyasaṅkhāro hoti. ‘‘Kathañcāvuso, bhikkhu suvimuttacitto hoti? Idhāvuso, bhikkhuno rāgā cittaṃ vimuttaṃ hoti, dosā cittaṃ vimuttaṃ hoti, mohā cittaṃ vimuttaṃ hoti. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu suvimuttacitto hoti. ‘‘Kathañcāvuso, bhikkhu suvimuttapañño hoti? Idhāvuso, bhikkhu ‘rāgo me pahīno ucchinnamūlo tālāvatthukato anabhāvaṃkato āyatiṃ anuppādadhammo’ti pajānāti. ‘Doso me pahīno ucchinnamūlo tālāvatthukato anabhāvaṃkato āyatiṃ anuppādadhammo’ti pajānāti. ‘Moho me pahīno ucchinnamūlo tālāvatthukato anabhāvaṃkato āyatiṃ anuppādadhammo’ti pajānāti. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu suvimuttapañño hoti. ‘‘Dasa asekkhā dhammā – asekkhā sammādiṭṭhi, asekkho sammāsaṅkappo, asekkhā sammāvācā, asekkho sammākammanto, asekkho sammāājīvo, asekkho sammāvāyāmo, asekkhā sammāsati, asekkho sammāsamādhi, asekkhaṃ sammāñāṇaṃ, asekkhā sammāvimutti. asekkhā dhammā – ‘‘Ime kho, āvuso, tena bhagavatā jānatā passatā arahatā sammāsambuddhena dasa dhammā sammadakkhātā. Tattha sabbeheva saṅgāyitabbaṃ na vivaditabbaṃ, yathayidaṃ brahmacariyaṃ addhaniyaṃ assa ciraṭṭhitikaṃ, tadassa bahujanahitāya bahujanasukhāya lokānukampāya atthāya hitāya sukhāya devamanussāna’’nti.
348. “Ten noble abodes (dasa ariyavāsā). Here, friends, a monk has abandoned five factors (pañcaṅgavippahīno hoti), is endowed with six (chaḷaṅgasamannāgato), guards one (ekārakkho), is supported in four (caturāpasseno), has shaken off idiosyncratic truths (paṇunnapaccekasacco), has thoroughly given up searching (samavayasaṭṭhesano), is undisturbed in his resolves (anāvilasaṅkappo), is calmed in his bodily-fabrication (passaddhakāyasaṅkhāro), is well-released in mind (suvimuttacitto), is well-released in discernment (suvimuttapañño).

“And how, friends, has a monk abandoned five factors? Here, friends, a monk’s sensual desire (kāmacchando) is abandoned (pahīno hoti), his ill will (byāpādo) is abandoned, his sloth & torpor (thinamiddhaṃ) is abandoned, his restlessness & anxiety (uddhaccakukuccaṃ) is abandoned, his uncertainty (vicikicchā) is abandoned. This is how a monk has abandoned five factors.

“And how, friends, is a monk endowed with six? Here, friends, a monk, on seeing a form (rūpaṃ) via the eye (cakkhunā), is neither gladdened (sumano) nor saddened (dummano), but remains equanimous (upekkhako), mindful (sato), & alert (sampajāno). On hearing a sound (saddaṃ) via the ear (sotena)… On cognizing an idea (dhammaṃ) via the intellect (manasā), he is neither gladdened nor saddened, but remains equanimous, mindful, & alert. This is how a monk is endowed with six.

“And how, friends, is a monk guarding one? Here, friends, a monk is endowed with an awareness guarded by mindfulness (satārakkhena cetasā samannāgato hoti). This is how a monk is guarding one.

“And how, friends, is a monk supported in four? Here, friends, a monk uses one [factor] with reflection (saṅkhāya), endures one with reflection, avoids one with reflection, dispels one with reflection. This is how a monk is supported in four.

“And how, friends, has a monk shaken off idiosyncratic truths? Here, friends, whatever idiosyncratic truths (puthupaccekasaccāni) there may be among various ascetics (samaṇa) and brahmins (brāhmaṇa), all of those are rejected, shaken off, discarded, spewed out, released, abandoned, relinquished. This is how a monk has shaken off idiosyncratic truths.

“And how, friends, has a monk thoroughly given up searching? Here, friends, a monk has abandoned his search for sensuality (kāmesanā), has abandoned his search for becoming (bhavesanā), and has allayed his search for a holy life (brahmacariyesanā). This is how a monk has thoroughly given up searching.

“And how, friends, is a monk undisturbed in his resolves? Here, friends, a monk's resolve for sensuality (kāmasaṅkappo) is abandoned, his resolve for ill-will (byāpādasaṅkappo) is abandoned, his resolve for harmfulness (vihiṃsāsaṅkappo) is abandoned. This is how a monk is undisturbed in his resolves.

“And how, friends, is a monk calmed in his bodily fabrication (passaddhakāyasaṅkhāro)? Here, friends, a monk, with the abandoning of pleasure (sukha) & pain (dukkha)—as with the earlier disappearance of elation (somanassa) & distress (domanassa)—enters & remains in the fourth jhāna: purity of equanimity (upekkhā) & mindfulness (sati), neither pleasure nor pain (adukkhamasukhaṃ). This is how a monk is calmed in his bodily fabrication.

“And how, friends, is a monk well-released in mind? Here, friends, a monk’s mind is released from lust (rāgā cittaṃ vimuttaṃ hoti), his mind is released from hate (dosā cittaṃ vimuttaṃ hoti), his mind is released from delusion (mohā cittaṃ vimuttaṃ hoti). This is how a monk is well released in mind.

“And how, friends, is a monk well-released in discernment? Here, friends, a monk discerns, ‘Lust (rāgo) is abandoned in me, its root destroyed, made like a palmyra stump, deprived of the conditions of development, not destined for future arising.’ ‘Hate (doso) is abandoned in me, its root destroyed, made like a palmyra stump, deprived of the conditions of development, not destined for future arising.’ ‘Delusion (moho) is abandoned in me, its root destroyed, made like a palmyra stump, deprived of the conditions of development, not destined for future arising.’ This is how a monk is well-released in discernment.
Ten qualities of one beyond training (aśaikṣā dharmā): the right view of one beyond training, (aśaikṣā samyagdṛṣṭi), the right resolve of one beyond training (aśaikṣaḥ samyaksaṃkalpaḥ), the right speech of one beyond training (aśaikṣā samyagvācā), the right action of one beyond training (aśaikṣo samyakkarmāntaḥ), the right livelihood of one beyond training (aśaikṣo samyagājīvaḥ), the right effort of one beyond training (aśaikṣā samyagvyāyāmaḥ), the right mindfulness of one beyond training (aśaikṣā samyaksmṛtiḥ), the right concentration of one beyond training (aśaikṣo samyaksamādhiḥ), the right knowledge of one beyond training (aśaikṣaṃ samyagjñānam), the right release of one beyond training (aśaikṣā samyagvimuktiḥ).

Qualities of one beyond training:
"These, friend, are the ten qualities well-proclaimed by that Blessed One—the Knower, the Seer, the Worthy One, the perfectly Awakened One. There all should recite together, without dispute, so that this spiritual life (brahmacarya) will endure and last a long time, for the benefit and happiness of many, out of compassion for the world, for the welfare, benefit, and happiness of gods and humans."
348.Ariyavāsāti ariyā eva vasiṃsu vasanti vasissanti etesūti ariyavāsā. Pañcaṅgavippahīnoti pañcahi aṅgehi vippayuttova hutvā khīṇāsavo avasi vasati vasissatīti tasmā ayaṃ pañcaṅgavippahīnatā, ariyassa vāsattā ariyavāsoti vutto. Esa nayo sabbattha. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu chaḷaṅgasamannāgato hotīti chaḷaṅgupekkhāya samannāgato hoti. Chaḷaṅgupekkhā nāma keti? Ñāṇādayo. ‘‘Ñāṇa’’nti vutte kiriyato cattāri ñāṇasampayuttacittāni labbhanti. ‘‘Satatavihāro’’ti vutte aṭṭha mahācittāni. ‘‘Rajjanadussanaṃ natthī’’ti vutte dasa cittāni labbhanti. Somanassaṃ āsevanavasena labbhati. Evaṃ kho, āvuso, bhikkhu chaḷaṅgasamannāgato hotī Satārakkhena cetasāti khīṇāsavassa hi tīsu dvāresu sabbakālaṃ sati ārakkhakiccaṃ sādheti . Tenevassa ‘‘carato ca tiṭṭhato ca suttassa ca jāgarassa ca satataṃ samitaṃ ñāṇadassanaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hotī’’ti vuccati. Satārakkhena cetasā Puthusamaṇabrāhmaṇānanti bahūnaṃ samaṇabrāhmaṇānaṃ. Ettha ca samaṇāti pabbajjupagatā. Brāhmaṇāti bhovādino. Puthupaccekasaccānīti bahūni pāṭekkasaccāni, idameva dassanaṃ saccaṃ, idameva dassanaṃ saccanti evaṃ pāṭiyekkaṃ gahitāni bahūni saccānīti attho. Nunnānīti nihatāni. Paṇunnānīti suṭṭhu nihatāni. Cattānīti vissaṭṭhāni. Vantānīti vamitāni. Muttānīti chinnabandhanāni katāni. Pahīnānīti pajahitāni. Paṭinissaṭṭhānīti yathā na puna cittaṃ āruhanti, evaṃ paṭinissajjitāni. Sabbāneva tāni gahitaggahaṇassa vissaṭṭhabhāvavevacanāni. Puthusamaṇabrāhmaṇāna samaṇā Brāhmaṇā Puthupaccekasaccānī Nunnānī Paṇunnānī Cattānī Vantānī Muttānī Pahīnānī Paṭinissaṭṭhānī Samavayasaṭṭhesanoti ettha avayāti anūnā. Saṭṭhāti vissaṭṭhā. Sammā avayā saṭṭhā esanā assāti samavayasaṭṭhesano. Sammā vissaṭṭhasabbaesanoti attho. Rāgā cittaṃ vimuttantiādīhi maggassa kiccanipphatti kathitā. Samavayasaṭṭhesano avayā Saṭṭhā samavayasaṭṭhesano. Rāgā cittaṃ vimutta Rāgome pahīnotiādīhi paccavekkhaṇāya phalaṃ kathitaṃ. Rāgo me pahīno Asekkhadhammadasakavaṇṇanā Asekkhā sammādiṭṭhītiādayo sabbepi phalasampayuttadhammā eva. Ettha ca sammādiṭṭhi, sammāñāṇanti dvīsu ṭhānesu paññāva kathitā. Sammāvimuttīti iminā padena vuttāvasesā. Phalasamāpattidhammā saṅgahitāti veditabbā. Asekkhā sammādiṭṭhī Sammāvimuttī ‘‘Ime kho, āvuso’’tiādi vuttanayeneva yojetabbaṃ. Iti channaṃ dasakānaṃ vasena samasaṭṭhi pañhe kathento thero sāmaggirasaṃ dassesīti. Dasakavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā. Pañhasamodhānavaṇṇanā
348. Ariyavāsā means those in whom the noble ones have dwelt, do dwell, and will dwell, thus ariyavāsā.

Pañcaṅgavippahīno means that, having become one who is disconnected from the five constituents, an arhat has lived, does live, and will live; therefore, this is pañcaṅgavippahīnatā, because of the dwelling of the noble one, it is called ariyavāso.

This method applies to all the other cases.

Thus, friends, a bhikkhu is endowed with six factors means he is endowed with six factors of equanimity.

What are the six factors of equanimity? They are wisdom and so on.

When “wisdom” is stated, four consciousnesses associated with wisdom are obtained from the action.

When “constant abiding” is stated, eight great consciousnesses [are obtained].

When “there is no attachment or aversion” is stated, ten consciousnesses are obtained.

Joy is obtained by way of cultivation.

Thus, friends, a bhikkhu is endowed with six factors.

Satārakkhena cetasā means mindfulness constantly fulfills the function of guarding in the three doors for an arhat.

Therefore it is said of him, “Whether I am walking or standing or asleep or awake, knowledge and vision are continuously and uninterruptedly present to me.”

Satārakkhena cetasā

Puthusamaṇabrāhmaṇāna means of many samaṇas and brāhmaṇas.

Here, samaṇā means those who have undertaken ordination.

Brāhmaṇā means those who use the word “bho” (a term of address).

Puthupaccekasaccānī means many individual truths; the meaning is many truths individually grasped thus: “This alone is the true view, this alone is the true view.”

Nunnānī means struck down.

Paṇunnānī means well struck down.

Cattānī means abandoned.

Vantānī means vomited.

Muttānī means made with bonds cut.

Pahīnānī means relinquished.

Paṭinissaṭṭhānī means rejected in such a way that they do not rise in the mind again.

All of these are synonyms for the state of having abandoned the grasped-grasping.

Puthusamaṇabrāhmaṇāna, samaṇā, brāhmaṇā, puthupaccekasaccānī, nunnānī, paṇunnānī, cattānī, vantānī, muttānī, pahīnānī, paṭinissaṭṭhānī

Samavayasaṭṭhesano here, avayā means not deficient.

Saṭṭhā means relinquished.

He whose seeking is rightly not deficient and relinquished is samavayasaṭṭhesano. The meaning is: one who has rightly relinquished all seeking.

The accomplishment of the function of the path is described by way of "[his] mind is liberated from passion" and so on.

Samavayasaṭṭhesano, avayā, saṭṭhā, samavayasaṭṭhesano., rāgā cittaṃ vimutta

The fruit of retrospection is described by way of "rāgo me pahīno" and so on.

Rāgo me pahīno

Description of the Ten Qualities of One Beyond Training

Asekhā sammādiṭṭhi and so on, are all qualities associated with the fruition.

Here, wisdom alone is described in the two instances of sammādiṭṭhi (right view), and sammāñāṇa (right knowledge).

Sammāvimuttī (right liberation) is what remains unsaid and is stated by this term.
Phalasamāpattidhammā saṅgahitāti veditabbā. should be understood as included in the qualities of fruition attainment.

Asekhā sammādiṭṭhi sammāvimutti. "These, friend," etc., should be construed in the same way as stated above.

Thus, the Elder, speaking of sixty questions in terms of six decades, showed the taste of concord.

The exposition of the decade is finished.

The exposition of the resolution of the questions.
349
349. Atha kho bhagavā uṭṭhahitvā āyasmantaṃ sāriputtaṃ āmantesi – ‘sādhu sādhu, sāriputta, sādhu kho tvaṃ, sāriputta, bhikkhūnaṃ saṅgītipariyāyaṃ abhāsī’ti. Idamavocāyasmā sāriputto, samanuñño satthā ahosi. Attamanā te bhikkhū āyasmato sāriputtassa bhāsitaṃ abhinandunti. Saṅgītisuttaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ dasamaṃ.
Then the Blessed One, having risen, addressed the Venerable Sāriputta, saying: "Excellent, excellent, Sāriputta! Excellent indeed, Sāriputta, is the way you have spoken to the monks about the saṅgīti-pariyāya (sequence for reciting together)!"

This is what the Venerable Sāriputta said, and the Teacher approved.

The monks were pleased and delighted in the Venerable Sāriputta's words.

The Saṅgīti Sutta (Sequence for Recitation Discourse), the tenth, is complete.
349. Idha pana ṭhatvā pañhā samodhānetabbā. Imasmiñhi sutte ekakavasena dve pañhā kathitā. Dukavasena sattati. Tikavasena asītisataṃ. Catukkavasena dvesatāni. Pañcakavasena tiṃsasataṃ. Chakkavasena bāttiṃsasataṃ. Sattakavasena aṭṭhanavuti. Aṭṭhakavasena aṭṭhāsīti. Navakavasena catupaṇṇāsa. Dasakavasena samasaṭṭhīti evaṃ sahassaṃ cuddasa pañhā kathitā. Imañhi suttantaṃ ṭhapetvā tepiṭake buddhavacane añño suttanto evaṃ bahupañhapaṭimaṇḍito natthi. Bhagavā imaṃ suttantaṃ ādito paṭṭhāya sakalaṃ sutvā cintesi – ‘‘dhammasenāpati sāriputto buddhabalaṃ dīpetvā appaṭivattiyaṃ sīhanādaṃ nadati. Sāvakabhāsitoti vutte okappanā na hoti, jinabhāsitoti vutte hoti, tasmā jinabhāsitaṃ katvā devamanussānaṃ okappanaṃ imasmiṃ suttante uppādessāmī’’ti. Tato vuṭṭhāya sādhukāraṃ adāsi. Tena vuttaṃ ‘‘atha kho bhagavā vuṭṭhahitvā āyasmantaṃ sāriputtaṃ āmantesi, sādhu, sādhu, sāriputta, sādhu kho tvaṃ sāriputta, bhikkhūnaṃ saṅgītipariyāyaṃ abhāsī’’ti. Tattha saṅgītipariyāyanti sāmaggiyā kāraṇaṃ. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – ‘‘sādhu, kho tvaṃ, sāriputta, mama sabbaññutaññāṇena saṃsanditvā bhikkhūnaṃ sāmaggirasaṃ abhāsī’’ti. Samanuñño satthā ahosīti anumodanena samanuñño ahosi. Ettakena ayaṃ suttanto jinabhāsito nāma jāto. Desanāpariyosāne imaṃ suttantaṃ manasikarontā te bhikkhū arahattaṃ pāpuṇiṃsūti. saṅgītipariyāya Samanuñño satthā ahosī Sumaṅgalavilāsiniyā dīghanikāyaṭṭhakathāya Saṅgītisuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.
349. Here, after having stood, questions should be brought to a conclusion. In this sutta, two questions are spoken of in a singular way.
Seventy in a duality way.
Eighty along with a hundred in a triplicity way.
Two hundreds in a quadruplicity way.
Thirty along with a hundred in a quintuplicity way.
Thirty-two along with a hundred in a sextuplicity way.
Ninety-eight in a septuplicity way.
Eighty-eight in an octuplicity way.
Fifty-four in a nonuplicity way.
Sixty along with a hundred in a decuplicity way. Thus, one thousand four hundred questions are spoken of.

Indeed, setting aside this suttanta, in the Tripiṭaka, in the Buddha's word, there is no other suttanta thus adorned with many questions.
The Blessed One, having heard this entire suttanta from the beginning, reflected: "The general of the Dharma, Sāriputta, having illuminated the Buddha's power, roars the lion's roar that cannot be turned back.
If it is said "spoken by a disciple," conviction does not occur; if it is said "spoken by the Victor," it occurs; therefore, having made it spoken by the Victor, I will produce conviction in gods and humans in this suttanta."
Then, having risen, he gave applause.
By him it was said: "Then, the Blessed One, having risen, addressed the venerable Sāriputta, 'Excellent, excellent, Sāriputta, excellent, Sāriputta, you have spoken of the Saṅgīti-pariyāya to the monks.'"

There, saṅgītipariyāya means the cause of harmony.
This is said: "Excellent, Sāriputta, having harmonized with my all-knowing knowledge, you have spoken of the flavor of harmony to the monks."
"The Teacher was in accordance" means he was in accordance with approval.
By this much, this suttanta became named "spoken by the Victor."
At the end of the teaching, those monks, attending to this suttanta, attained Arhatship.

Saṅgītipariyāya The Teacher was in accordance
The Saṅgīti Sutta Commentary in the Sumaṅgalavilāsinī, Dīghanikāyaṭṭhakathā, is finished.
